Traces of powers of random matrices over local fields
Noam Pirani

Abstract
Let M M be chosen uniformly at random w.r.t. the Haar measure on the unitary group U n U_{n} , the unitary symplectic group U ​ S ​ p 2 ​ n USp_{2n} or the orthogonal group O n O_{n} . Diaconis and Shashahani proved that the traces tr ⁡ ( M ) , tr ⁡ ( M 2 ) , … , tr ⁡ ( M k ) \mathrm{tr}(M),\mathrm{tr}(M^{2}),\ldots,\mathrm{tr}(M^{k}) converge in distribution to independent normal random variables as k k is fixed and n → ∞ n\to\infty . Recently, Gorodetsky and Rodgers proved analogs for these results for matrices chosen from certain finite matrix groups. For example, let M M be chosen uniformly at random from U n ​ ( 𝔽 q ) U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q}) . They show that { tr ⁡ ( M i ) } i = 1 , p ∤ i k \{\mathrm{tr}(M^{i})\}_{i=1,p\nmid i}^{k} converge in distribution to independent uniform random variables in 𝔽 q 2 \mathbb{F}_{q^{2}} as k k is fixed and n → ∞ n\to\infty . They also consider the case where we let k → ∞ k\to\infty with n n . We prove analogs for these results over local fields. Let ℱ \mathcal{F} be a local field with a ring of integers 𝒪 \mathcal{O} , a uniformizer π \pi , and a residue field of odd characteristic. Let 𝒦 / ℱ \mathcal{K}/\mathcal{F} be an unramified extension of degree 2 2 with a ring of integers ℛ \mathcal{R} . Let M M be chosen uniformly at random w.r.t. the Haar measure on the unitary group U n ​ ( 𝒪 ) U_{n}(\mathcal{O}) , and fix k k . We prove that the traces of powers { tr ⁡ ( M i ) } i = 1 , p ∤ i k \{\mathrm{tr}(M^{i})\}_{i=1,p\nmid i}^{k} converge to independent uniform random variables on ℛ \mathcal{R} , as n → ∞ n\to\infty . We also consider the case where k k may tend to infinity with n n . We show that for some constant c c (coming from the mod π \pi distribution), the total variation distance from independent uniform random variables on ℛ \mathcal{R} is o ⁡ ( 1 ) o(1) as n → ∞ n\to\infty , as long as k < c ⋅ n k<c\cdot n . We also consider other matrix groups over local fields, namely G = G ​ L n ​ ( 𝒪 ) , S ​ L n ​ ( 𝒪 ) , S ​ p 2 ​ n ​ ( 𝒪 ) , S ​ O n ​ ( 𝒪 ) G=GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}),SL_{n}(\mathcal{O}),Sp_{2n}(\mathcal{O}),SO_{n}(\mathcal{O}) , and prove similar results for them. Moreover, we consider traces of powers M p ​ i M^{pi} and traces of negative powers, and show that apart from certain necessary modular restrictions, they also equidistribute in the limit. The proofs rely on considering the lifting of a matrix from G ⁡ ( 𝒪 / π k − 1 ​ 𝒪 ) G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k-1}\mathcal{O}) to G ⁡ ( 𝒪 / π k ​ 𝒪 ) G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}) , and analyzing how the characteristic polynomial behaves under such lifts. We also rely on results on the distribution of conjugacy classes in finite matrix groups due to Fulman and the finite fields results of Gorodetsky-Rodgers.



1
Introduction

Diaconis and Shahshahani
[
5
]
studied the joint distribution of traces of powers of a matrix over classical matrix groups, initiating a line of work in random matrix theory. Concretely, consider
U_{n}=\{g\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{C}):g\overline{g}^{t}=I_{n}\}
, the unitary group, and let
Z=X+iY
be a standard complex normal random variable, with
X
,
Y
independent and
X,Y\sim N(0,\frac{1}{2})
are real Gaussians. Let
M
be a random matrix chosen uniformly w.r.t. the Haar measure on
U_{n}
. Diaconis and Shahshahani proved that for a fixed
k
the vector

(\mathrm{tr}(M),\mathrm{tr}(M^{2}),\ldots,\mathrm{tr}(M^{k}))

converges in distribution, as
n\to\infty
, to the vector

(\sqrt{1}Z_{1},\sqrt{2}Z_{2},\ldots,\sqrt{k}Z_{k})

where
Z_{j}\sim Z
are i.i.d random variables. They also prove similar results for the orthogonal and unitary symplectic groups. Their results were extended by various methods, ranging from representation theory (See e.g
[
4
,
2
,
29
,
3
,
25
]
) to analytic techniques (See e.g
[
21
]
) or combinatorial methods (See e.g
[
16
]
).

Confirming a conjecture of Diaconis, Johansson
[
18
]
proved that the rate of convergence is super-exponential in
n
, that is

\Pr[\mathrm{Re}(\mathrm{tr}(M^{k}))\leq x,\mathrm{Im}(\mathrm{tr}(M^{k}))\leq y]-\Pr[\mathrm{Re}(\sqrt{k}Z_{k})\leq x,\mathrm{Im}(\sqrt{k}Z_{k})\leq y]=O_{k}(e^{-c_{k}n\log n}).

More recently, Johansson and Lambert
[
17
]
proved that the convergence rate is also super-exponential for the joint moments. More precisely, set
\mathbf{T}=(\mathrm{tr}(M^{i}))_{i=1}^{k}
,
\mathbf{Z}=(Z_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
. For a random variable
X
on
\mathbb{C}^{k}
, define a probability measure by setting
\mu_{X}(A)=\Pr[X\in A]
for every Borel measurable
A\subset\mathbb{C}^{k}
. For
Y
another random variable on
\mathbb{C}^{k}
, define the total variation distance
d_{TV}(X,Y)=\sup_{A}|\mu_{X}(A)-\mu_{Y}(A)|
, where
A
ranges over all Borel measurable sets. Johansson and Lambert proved that
d_{TV}(\mathbf{T},\mathbf{K})
converges super-exponentially (in
n
) to zero as we take
n,k\to\infty
(with a complicated but explicit error term), provided that
k<\frac{\sqrt{n}}{6.45(\log n)^{1/4}}
.

Recently, Gorodetsky and Rodgers
[
12
]
considered similar questions over finite fields. For example, for the unitary case, they define
U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=\{g\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}):g\overline{g}^{t}=I_{n}\}
, where
\overline{g}
is defined via the action of Frobenius on the entries of
g
. In this case,
\mathrm{tr}(M),\mathrm{tr}(M^{2}),\ldots,\mathrm{tr}(M^{k})
do not converge to independent random variables since we have

\mathrm{tr}(M^{p})=\mathrm{tr}(M)^{p}
. Instead, they consider traces of powers that are coprime with
p
. They prove that as
n\to\infty
, the random variables
(\mathrm{tr}(M^{b_{i}}))_{i=1}^{k}
for
p\nmid b_{i}
converge in total variation distance to
k
independent, uniform random variables taking values in
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, provided that
\max(b_{i})/n
is bounded by some constant depending on
q
. They also consider the distribution of traces of powers for the groups
GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,
SL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,
O^{\pm}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,
Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Gorodetsky and Kovaleva
[
11
]
considered a single trace of power (or a single linear combination of traces of powers) for
GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
and improved the range of equidistribution much further, showing that
\mathrm{tr}(g^{k})
converges (in total variation distance) to a uniform random variable when
n\to\infty
, as long as
\log(k)=o(n^{2})
. They also prove that this range is sharp.

Random
p
-adic matrices were previously considered mainly in the context of the average size of their cokernels, motivated by applications in number theory. See for example

[
6
,
23
,
34
,
32
]
. The work
[
6
]
is particularly related to our paper since it considers a piece of the characteristic polynomial of a Haar random matrix
M\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
(corresponding to the
(x-1)
-primary part modulo
p
) and computes its limiting distribution.

The present paper considers
p
-adic analogs of the above results. For the rest of the paper, let
p
be an odd prime,
q
a power of
p
. Let
\mathcal{F}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
be an unramified finite extension with a ring of integers
\mathcal{O}
, such that the residue field of
\mathcal{O}
is
\mathbb{F}_{q}
and the uniformizer is
\pi
. Let
G
be one of the compact
p
-adic matrix groups
U_{n}(\mathcal{O})
,
GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
,
SL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
,
SO_{n}(\mathcal{O})
,
Sp_{2n}(\mathcal{O})
. Let
A
be a matrix from
G
, chosen uniformly at random w.r.t. the Haar measure. We examine the joint distribution of the traces of powers of
A
,
(\mathrm{tr}(A^{i}))_{i=1\atop{p\nmid i}}^{r}
, when
r<c_{G}\cdot n
for some constant
c_{G}
depending on
G
and
q
. Under these conditions, we prove that the total variation distance from a uniform distribution is
o(1)
as
n\to\infty
. We also examine the distribution of a single trace of a power
\mathrm{tr}(M^{r})
,
p\nmid r
, for a matrix
M\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
. We obtain a range that exceeds the range obtained as a corollary from the joint distribution result, building on the results of
[
11
]
.

Similarly to the finite field case, we note that when
p|r
, there are dependencies between
\mathrm{tr}(A^{r})
and the previous traces of powers. Let
\sigma\in\mathrm{Gal}(\mathcal{F}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
be the Frobenius element
\left(\frac{\pi}{p}\right)
. Then, if
p^{i}||r
,
i\geq 1
, we have

\mathrm{tr}(A^{r})\equiv\sigma(\mathrm{tr}(A^{r/p}))\pmod{\pi^{i}}.

This is proved in Lemma
3.8
below, which extends a phenomenon first observed by Arnold
[
1
]
and later extended in
[
20
]
. However, there are no further restrictions on the traces of powers of
p
-adic matrices. This motivates the following

Definition 1.1
.

A trace datum of length
d
over
\mathcal{O}
is a sequence of the form
(\frac{1}{|i|_{p}}a_{i})_{i=1}^{d}
, for
a_{i}\in\mathcal{O}
. For a matrix
M\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
we attach a trace datum of length
d
by setting the sequence
(\mathrm{tr}(M^{i})-1_{p|i}\sigma(\mathrm{tr}(A^{i/p})))_{i=1}^{d}
.

To handle the distribution of traces of powers which can be both positive and negative, we define

Definition 1.2
.

Let
d_{1},d_{2}>0
be two integers such that
d=d_{1}+d_{2}
. We define a
(d_{1},d_{2})
-trace datum to be a sequence of the form
(\frac{1}{|i|_{p}}a_{i})_{0\neq i=-d_{1}}^{d_{2}}
. The length of a
(d_{1},d_{2})
-trace datum is
d
. For a matrix
M
we attach a
(d_{1},d_{2})
trace datum by concatenating the
d_{1}
trace datum for
M^{-1}
with the
d_{2}
trace datum for
M
.

Finally, we define the uniform trace data,
\mathrm{UTR}_{d}
, which is simply a sequence
(\frac{1}{|i|_{p}}a_{i})_{i=1}^{d}
, for
a_{i}\in\mathcal{O}
chosen uniformly at random w.r.t. the Haar measure. We define similarly a uniform
(d_{1},d_{2})
-trace data
\mathrm{UTR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}
.

For a random variable
X
taking values in
\mathcal{O}^{l}
, we define the probability measure
\mu_{X}
on
\mathcal{O}^{l}
by
\mu_{X}(A)=\Pr[X\in A]
, for every Borel measurable
A
. We define the total variation distance between two measures
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
on
\mathcal{O}^{l}
by setting
d_{TV}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\sup_{A}|\mu_{1}(A)-\mu_{2}(A)|
where
A
ranges over all Borel measurable sets
A\subset\mathcal{O}^{l}
. Our results for the joint distribution of the traces of powers are as follows (in all cases, the implicit constant depends only on the group
G
and on
q
):

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
d<n
, and let
\mathrm{TR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{GL}
be the random variable attaching to a matrix
M\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
its
(d_{1},d_{2})
-trace datum of length
d
. Then,

d_{TV}(\mu_{\mathrm{TR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{GL}},\mu_{\mathrm{UTR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}})=O\left(q^{d-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+d}{n}+q^{d-\frac{n^{2}}{d}+o(n)}\right).

In particular, there is some constant
c_{q}^{GL}
, such that for
d<c_{q}^{GL}\cdot n
, this total variation distance is
o(1)
as
n\to\infty
.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
d<n
, and let
\mathrm{TR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{SL}
be the random variable attaching to a matrix
M\in SL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
its
(d_{1},d_{2})
-trace datum of length
d
. Then,

d_{TV}(\mu_{\mathrm{TR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{SL}},\mu_{\mathrm{UTR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}})=O\left(q^{d-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+d}{n}+q^{d-\frac{n^{2}}{d}+o(n)}\right).

In particular, there is some constant
c_{q}^{SL}
, such that for
d<c_{q}^{SL}\cdot n
, this total variation distance is
o(1)
as
n\to\infty
.

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
d<n
, and let
\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{Sp}
be the random variable attaching to a matrix
M\in Sp_{2n}(\mathcal{O})
its trace datum of length
d
. Then,

d_{TV}(\mu_{\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{Sp}},\mu_{\mathrm{UTR}_{d}})=O\left(q^{d-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}+\frac{n}{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q^{2}-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+2d-1}{n}+q^{2d-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}+o(n)}\right).

In particular, there is some constant
c_{q}^{Sp}
, such that for
d<c_{q}^{Sp}\cdot n
, this total variation distance is
o(1)
as
n\to\infty
.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
d<\frac{n}{2}
, and let
\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{SO}
be the random variable attaching to a matrix
M\in SO_{n}(\mathcal{O})
its trace datum of length
d
. Then,

d_{TV}(\mu_{\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{SO}},\mu_{\mathrm{UTR}_{d}})=O\left(q^{d-\frac{n^{2}}{8d}+\frac{n}{4}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q^{2}-1}\right)^{n/2}\binom{n/2+2d-1}{n/2}+q^{2d+\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n^{2}}{4d}+o(n)}\right).

In particular, there is some constant
c_{q}^{SO}
, such that for
d<c_{q}^{SO}\cdot n
, this total variation distance is
o(1)
as
n\to\infty
.

Theorem 1.7
.

Let
\mathcal{K}/\mathcal{F}
be an unramified quadratic extension, and let
\mathcal{R}
be its ring of integers. Let
d<\frac{n}{2}
, and let
\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{U}
be the random variable attaching to a matrix
M\in U_{n}(\mathcal{O})
its trace datum of length
d
. Let
\mathrm{UTR}_{d}
be a uniform trace datum over
\mathcal{R}
. Then,

d_{TV}(\mu_{\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{U}},\mu_{\mathrm{UTR}_{d}})=O\left(q^{2d+\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n^{2}}{4d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+2d-3}{n}\right).

In particular, there is some constant
c_{q}^{U}
, such that for
d<c_{q}^{U}\cdot n
, this total variation distance is
o(1)
as
n\to\infty
.

Remark 1.8
.

The sizes of the constants
c_{q}^{G}
for the various
G
come from the finite field results of Gorodetsky-Rodgers.

1.

For
GL_{n}
and
SL_{n}
, one can see that the range of
d
for which the bound is useful is growing with
q
, and can reach
d<\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}n
for
q
large.

2.

For
Sp_{2n}
, we must have
d<n
. This stems from the fact that if
M\in Sp_{2n}
has a characteristic polynomial
\mathrm{char}(M)=x^{2n}+a_{2n-1}x^{2n-1}+\cdots+a_{1}x+1
, then
a_{2n-i}=a_{i}
. Using Newton’s formula, this shows that
\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{Sp}
is not close to
\mathrm{UTR}_{d}
when
d>n
. Our results are meaningful in a range
d<(\frac{1}{2}-o_{q}(1))n
.

3.

For
SO_{n}
, for reasons similar to those in the symplectic group,
\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{SO}
is not close to
\mathrm{UTR}_{d}
when
d>n/2
. The actual range we get is
d<(\frac{1}{4}-o_{q}(1))n
.

4.

For
U_{n}
the situation is again similar to the symplectic case and thus
\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{U}
is not close to
\mathrm{UTR}_{d}
when
d>n/2
. The actual range we get is
d<(\frac{1}{4}-o_{q}(1))n
.

As an immediate consequence of the last Theorem we obtain a direct analog for one of the results of Diaconis and Shahshahani
[
5
, Theorem 1]
:

Theorem 1.9
.

Fix
d>0
, and let
B_{1},\ldots,B_{d}
be fixed balls in
\mathcal{R}
. Let
Z_{i}\sim\frac{1}{|i|_{p}}U_{i}
, where
U_{i}
are independent uniform random variables on
\mathcal{R}
. Then,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\Pr_{M\in U_{n}(\mathcal{O})}\left[\mathrm{tr}(M^{i})-1_{p|i}\sigma(\mathrm{tr}(M^{i/p}))\in B_{i},i=1,\ldots,d\right]=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\Pr[Z_{i}\in B_{i}].

We have analogous results for each one of the groups we consider, appearing in the corresponding section of the paper. We also get an extended range for a single trace in
GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
.

Theorem 1.10
.

Let
U
be the uniform distribution on
\mathcal{O}
, and let
p\nmid r
be an integer, which may depend on
n
, such that
\log r=o(n^{2})
. Then,

d_{TV}(\mu_{\mathrm{tr}(A^{r})},\mu_{U})\to_{n\to\infty}0.

We prove Theorems
1.3
,
1.4
,
1.5
,

1.6
and
1.7
using similar ideas. Let
G
be one of the groups
GL_{n}
,
SL_{n}
,
USp_{2n}
,
U_{n}
,
SO_{n}
. The basic idea is to understand, given
A_{0}\in G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k-1}\mathcal{O})
, what is the structure of the characteristic polynomial
\mathrm{char}(A)
for
A\in G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
a lift of
A_{0}
. As it turns out, the structure is regulated by the image of a certain linear map
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}
. We identify
\mathcal{O}/\pi\mathcal{O}
with
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, and we let
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be the Lie algebra associated with the matrix group
G(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. We also denote by
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<n}
the set of polynomials of degree
<n
with coefficients in
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Abusing notation slightly, the lifts
A
of
A_{0}
to
G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
are of the form
A=A_{0}(I+\pi^{k-1}A_{1})
, where
A_{1}\in\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
and
I
is the identity matrix. The function
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}
is a linear map from
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
with an image in
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<n}
, such that

\mathrm{char}(A)=\mathrm{char}(A_{0})+\pi^{k-1}\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}(A_{1}).

(1.1)

In all cases, we compute the image of
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}
explicitly. This involves finding explicit representatives for conjugacy classes of elements in
G(\mathbb{F}_{q^{l}})
, for an extension
\mathbb{F}_{q^{l}}/\mathbb{F}_{q}
in which
\mathrm{char}(A_{0})\pmod{\pi}
splits completely. It turns out that the dimension of the image is equal to
\deg(\min(A_{0})\pmod{\pi})
in the
GL_{n}
case (and is determined explicitly by the degree of the minimal polynomial in the other cases).

After studying this map, we estimate the probability that
\mathrm{char}(A)
lands in some short interval (or some Hayes residue class). Using Lemma
3.10
below (essentially Newton’s formulae), this is equivalent to understanding the joint distribution of the first
k
traces of powers of
M
, that is the distribution of the vector
(\mathrm{tr}(M^{i}))_{i=1}^{k}
. This is done by an induction argument, estimating the conditional probability that
\mathrm{char}(A)
lands in some short interval modulo
\pi^{k}
given that it landed in the interval modulo
\pi^{k-1}
. By equation (
1.1
) and since the dimension of
\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}})
is big when
\deg(\min A_{0}\pmod{\pi})
is, when the degree of the minimal polynomial is large the conditional probability is
q^{-d}
as expected heuristically.

In Section
5
we show that with high probability, the degree of the minimal polynomial is large (in a precise sense described there). Using this, we get good control on the discrepancy of the probability that
\mathrm{char}(M)
lands in a certain interval or Hayes residue class, which allows us to compute the total variation distance of the trace data from the uniform measure on trace data. We remark that we can not get a useful discrepancy bound for every short interval, unlike the strong result in
[
12
]
. Instead, our bound is, in a sense, an average discrepancy bound. The proof of Theorem
1.10
is somewhat similar, based on the structure of
\mathrm{tr}(A^{r})
for lifts of
A_{0}
. For every one of the results, we rely on equidistribution results in the residue field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, due to Gorodetsky-Rodgers
[
12
]
or Gorodetsky-Kovaleva
[
11
]
.

Remark 1.11
.

The assumption that the extension
\mathcal{F}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
is unramified can probably be removed, however, we decided to leave it in this form to ease the presentation. Similarly, one can consider the same problem over an extension of
\mathbb{F}_{q}((t))
, the field of formal Laurent series over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Our methods work in this setting as well, giving identical results, but we decided to leave this out of the presentation to avoid unnecessary complications.

The paper is structured as follows. In Section
2
we provide the main preliminary results we need, mainly about the distribution of conjugacy classes in matrix groups due to Fulman
[
8
]
and ideas from Gorodetsky-Rodgers
[
12
]
. In Section
3
we prove some general results on polynomials and traces of powers of matrices needed later. In Section
4
we compute explicit representatives for certain conjugacy classes in
G(\mathbb{F}_{q})
for every group
G
we consider. In Section
5
we discuss the typical degree of a minimal polynomial in
G(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. In the rest of the sections, we prove results on the joint distribution of traces of powers for
G(\mathcal{O})
:

1.

In Section
6
we discuss results for
GL_{n}
and prove Theorem
1.3
and Theorem
1.10
.

2.

In Section
7
we discuss results for
SL_{n}
and prove Theorem
1.4
.

3.

In Section
8
we discuss results for
Sp_{2n}
and prove Theorem
1.5
.

4.

In Section
9
we discuss results for
SO_{n}
and prove Theorem
1.6
.

5.

In the last section, Section
10
, we discuss results for
U_{n}
and prove Theorem
1.7
and Theorem
1.9
.

The author would also like to thank Itamar Hadas for many useful discussions regarding the problems and Noam Ta-Shma for discussions leading to the proof of Lemma
3.3
, and also Zeév Rudnick, Ofir Gorodetsky and Brad Rodgers for helpful comments on earlier versions of the paper.

2
Preliminary background

The following section describes some preliminary background needed later in the paper. First, we want to fix some notation used throughout the work.

Everywhere in the paper,
p
is an odd prime and
q
is a power of
p
. We denote by
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}
the set of polynomials of degree
n
in
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
. The subset
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
consists of the monic polynomials of degree
n
. We similarly denote by
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{\leq n},\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<n}
the polynomials of degree at most
n
and strictly smaller than
n
(respectively). A polynomial
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
is irreducible if it cannot decompose non-trivially. A polynomial is called prime if it is irreducible and monic. We denote the set of primes in
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
by
\mathcal{P}
.

We now fix a finite, unramified field extension
\mathcal{F}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
with a ring of integers
\mathcal{O}
. We also fix
\pi
to be a uniformizer in
\mathcal{O}
, that is, an element of
\mathcal{O}
with
p
-adic valuation equal to
1
. We assume that the residue field of
\mathcal{F}
, that is the field
\mathcal{O}/\pi\mathcal{O}
, is isomorphic to
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. We let
\sigma\in\mathrm{Gal}(\mathcal{F}/\mathbb{Q}_{p})
be the Frobenius element
\left(\frac{\pi}{p}\right)
, that is the unique element such that
\sigma(x)\equiv x^{p}\pmod{\pi}
for every
x\in\mathcal{O}
. Finally, we let
\mathcal{K}/\mathcal{F}
be an unramified quadratic extension, and denote the generator of
\mathrm{Gal}(\mathcal{K}/\mathcal{F})
by
\tau
.

For a matrix
M
(which can be in
M_{n}(\mathcal{O})
,
M_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,
M_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
, etc.) we denote by
\mathrm{char}(M)
the characteristic polynomial of
M
and by
\min(M)
the minimal polynomial of
M
. Also, for us
G
will always be one of the groups
GL
,
SL
,
Sp
,
SO
,
U
. We will typically use the parenthesis to declare the ring of definition (e.g., write
GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
,
GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
or
GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
) unless it should be clear from the context. When we want to emphasize the dependence on
n
, we will sometimes write
G_{n}
(for the case of
Sp
,
G_{n}=Sp_{2n}
).

2.1
Distribution of conjugacy classes for matrices over finite fields

We recall some facts about the distribution of conjugacy classes for matrices over a finite field
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. For a much more extensive review, describing also some generating functions techniques and discussing the details for other groups, see
[
8
]
.

We first recall that conjugacy classes for
GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
may be parameterized using rational canonical forms. For a monic irreducible polynomial
\phi\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
, associate a partition
\lambda_{\phi}
of some non-negative integer
|\lambda_{\phi}|
. We impose the restrictions
|\lambda_{x}|=0
and
\sum_{\phi\in\mathcal{P}}|\lambda_{\phi}|\deg(\phi)=n
.

Now let
\phi=x^{\deg\phi}+a_{\deg\phi-1}x^{\deg\phi-1}+\cdots+a_{1}x+a_{0}
. We recall that the companion matrix
C(\phi)
is the matrix

\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}0&1&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&1&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\cdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&0&1\\
-a_{0}&-a_{1}&-a_{2}&\cdots&-a_{\deg(\phi)-1}\end{array}\right).

Let
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{k}
be a collection of monic, irreducible polynomials, together with partitions
|\lambda_{\phi_{i}}|>0
,
\lambda_{\phi_{i}}=(\lambda_{\phi_{i},j})_{j=1}^{r_{\phi}}
. We order the parts of
\lambda_{\phi_{i}}
so that
\lambda_{\phi_{i},1}\geq\lambda_{\phi_{i},2}\geq\ldots
. A collection of partitions
(\lambda_{\phi_{i}})_{i=1}^{k}
is called a
conjugacy class datum
and we attach to it a matrix

M=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}R_{1}&0&0&\cdots&0\\
0&R_{2}&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\cdots&\vdots\\
0&0&0&R_{k-1}&0\\
0&0&0&\cdots&R_{k}\end{array}\right),

where
R_{i}
is the matrix

\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}C(\phi_{i}^{\lambda_{\phi_{i},1}})&0&0&\cdots&0\\
0&C(\phi_{i}^{\lambda_{\phi_{i},2}})&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\cdots&\vdots\\
\end{array}\right).

The conjugacy class of the matrix
M
is
a matrix attached to the conjugacy class datum. Every conjugacy class of
GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is obtained from a unique conjugacy class datum. Many algebraic properties of
M
may be derived from its conjugacy class datum alone. For example, the characteristic polynomial is
\mathrm{char}(M)=\prod_{\phi\in\mathcal{P}}\phi^{|\lambda_{\phi}(M)|}
, and the minimal polynomial is
\min(M)=\prod_{\phi\in\mathcal{P}}\phi^{\lambda_{\phi,1}(M)}
. Moreover, if one draws a matrix uniformly at random from
GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, the probability of getting a matrix from the conjugacy class of
M
is (see for example
[
9
, Section 2.1]
)

\frac{1}{\prod_{\phi}q^{\deg\phi\sum_{i}(\lambda_{\phi,i}^{\prime})^{2}}\prod_{i\geq 1}\left(\frac{1}{q^{\deg(\phi)}}\right)_{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}}.

Here,
m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})
is the number of parts of
\lambda_{\phi}
of size
i
, and
\lambda_{\phi,i}^{\prime}
is the partition dual to
\lambda_{\phi}
, that is, the partition

|\lambda_{\phi}^{\prime}|=\sum_{i=1}^{|\lambda_{\phi}|}m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})
. Finally,

\left(\frac{1}{q^{\deg(\phi)}}\right)_{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}:=\left(1-\frac{1}{q^{\deg(\phi)}}\right)\cdots\left(1-\frac{1}{q^{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})\deg(\phi)}}\right).

Fulman
[
8
,
7
]
derive similar formulas for other classical finite matrix groups, which we use in the paper.

2.2
Hayes characters

Here we recall briefly basic facts about Hayes characters, introduced by Hayes
[
15
]
. For a more extensive review, see
[
13
]
, on which we rely in this short introduction. We also remark that this is a special case of Hecke characters over function fields (see
[
27
, §9]
).

2.2.1
Hayes’ equivalence relation

Let
\mathcal{M}_{q}\subset\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
be the set of monic polynomials. We note that it is a monoid under multiplication, and define an equivalence relation respecting this structure. Let
l\geq 0
be an integer, and let
0\neq H\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
be a monic polynomial. We define an equivalence relation
R_{l,H}
on
\mathcal{M}_{q}
, by saying that
A\equiv B

mod
R_{l,H}

if and only if
A,B

have the same
l
-next-to-leading coefficients, and also
A\equiv B
mod
H
. We remark that we adopt the convention that if
\deg(A)<j
, the
j
-th next-to-leading coefficient of
A
is
0
. Note that we do not demand that
\deg(A)=\deg(B)
. For example, we have

x^{5}+x^{3}+1\equiv x^{3}+x+1\pmod{R_{2,x}},

for any
q
. One can show that
R_{l,H}
respects the multiplication on
\mathcal{M}_{q}
, and that the quotient
\mathcal{M}_{q}/R_{l,H}
is finite. Further, the unit group
(\mathcal{M}_{q}/R_{l,H})^{\times}
of the quotient
\mathcal{M}_{q}/R_{l,H}
(which is composed precisely of the conjugacy classes of polynomials
A
which are coprime with
H
) is of size
q^{l}\varphi(H)
, where
\varphi
is Euler’s totient function (for an introduction to the Euler’s totient function over
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
, see
[
27
, §2]
).

When we look at the subset
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}\subset\mathcal{M}_{q}
of degree
n
monic polynomials, the equivalence relation
R_{l,1}
detects short intervals. That is, the equivalence classes restricted to
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
are of the form

I(f,l):=\{g\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}:\text{ The $l$ next-to-leading coeficcients of }g\text{ are the same as those of }f\}.

These sets are analogous to intervals in
\mathbb{Z}
, hence the name.

2.2.2
Characters

Let
G_{l,H}
be the finite abelian group
(\mathcal{M}_{q}/R_{l,H})^{\times}
. For a character
\chi
of
G_{l,H}
, we define a function (which we also denote by
\chi
) over the full monoid
\mathcal{M}_{q}
. The definition is analogous to that of Dirichlet characters,

\chi(A)=\begin{cases}\chi(A\text{ mod }{R_{l,H}}),&(A,H)=1,\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

These are called Hayes characters. We denote the group of Hayes characters by
\hat{R}_{l,H}
. As usual, we will denote by
\chi_{0}
the Hayes character corresponding to the trivial character of
(\mathcal{M}_{q}/R_{l,H})^{\times}
. As in the case of Dirichlet characters, orthogonality of characters now implies that for
n\geq l+\deg(H)
,

\frac{1}{q^{n-\deg(H)}\varphi(H)}\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}}\chi(f)=\begin{cases}1,&\chi=\chi_{0},\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(2.1)

and for any
f,g\in\mathcal{M}_{q}
, coprime with
H
,

\frac{1}{q^{l}\varphi(H)}\sum_{\chi\in\hat{R}_{l,H}}\chi(f)\overline{\chi}(g)=\begin{cases}1,&f\equiv g\pmod{R_{l,H}},\\
0,&\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

(2.2)

The first orthogonality relation follows from the fact that for
n\geq l+\deg(H)
,
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
is a disjoint union of
q^{n-l-\deg(H)}
systems of representatives for the Hayes equivalence relation
R_{l,H}
.

Similarly to the way that Dirichlet characters are useful to detect modular relations, using the orthogonality relation (
2.2
) short interval characters may be used to detect if an element belongs to a short interval in
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
.

2.2.3
Extension to
\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}

We extend the Hayes relation for polynomials in
\left(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}\right)[x]
in the following way. Let
d\geq 0
be an integer and let
H\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
be a polynomial. We define the Hayes relation modulo
\pi^{k}
,
R_{d,H,\pi^{k}}
by saying that two monic polynomials
A,B\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
are equivalent (denoted
A\equiv B\text{ mod }R_{d,H,\pi^{k}}
) if
A-B=0\pmod{H}
and
A,B
have the same
d
next-to-leading coefficients. We again adopt the convention that if
\deg(A)<j
, the
j
-th next-to-leading coefficient of
A
is
0
.

The special case
k=1
is precisely the case of the “regular” Hayes relation defined above. Finally, we will sometimes denote the Hayes characters (defined only for
d=1
) by
\widehat{R}_{d,H,\pi}
.

2.3
Distribution over finite fields

The distribution of the traces of powers and characteristic polynomials of a matrix in
GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
(and other groups) is studied in
[
12
]
. They start from the pointwise probability for a given characteristic polynomial. Let
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
be monic such that
f(0)\neq 0
(this condition is necessary for a polynomial to be a characteristic polynomial of a matrix in
GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
). Factor
f
to irreducible polynomials:
f=\prod_{i=1}^{r}P_{i}^{e_{i}}
. Denote
\deg(P_{i})=m_{i}
. Then, a classical result proved by Reiner
[
26
, Theorem 2]
and Gerstenhaber
[
10
, Section 2]
(independently) shows that

P_{GL}(f):=\Pr_{A\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}[\mathrm{char}(A)=f]=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\frac{q^{m_{i}e_{i}(e_{i}-1)}}{|GL_{e_{i}}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{m_{i}}})|}.

(2.3)

Fixing a Hayes character
\chi
, Gorodetsky-Rodgers define an
L
-function

L_{GL}(u,\chi)=\sum_{f\in\mathcal{M}_{q}^{gl}}\chi(f)u^{\deg f},\quad|u|<1/q.

(Here
\mathcal{M}_{q}^{\mathrm{gl}}
is the set of all monic polynomials
f
with
f(0)\neq 0
). Using the formula (
2.3
) and its structure, Gorodetsky-Rodgers show that the generating function

\sum_{f\in\mathcal{M}_{q}^{\mathrm{gl}}}P_{GL}(f)\chi(f)u^{\deg f}

factors as a product
\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}L_{GL}(\frac{u}{q^{i}},\chi)
. Now using character orthogonality and a variant of the Riemann hypothesis for the
L
-function
L_{GL}(u,\chi)
, they prove the following

Theorem 2.1

(
[
12
]
, Theorem 1.3)
.

Let
A\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be a random matrix chosen according to the Haar measure. Then for
0\leq d<n
and for
f\in\mathcal{M}_{n,q}
,

\left|\Pr[\mathrm{char}(A)\in I(f,n-d)]-q^{-d}\right|<q^{-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+d-1}{n}.

Using similar ideas Gorodetsky-Rodgers also prove a theorem regarding the equidistribution of traces of powers:

Theorem 2.2

(
[
12
]
, Theorem 1.1)
.

Let
A\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be a random matrix chosen according to the Haar measure. Fix a strictly increasing sequence
b_{1},\ldots,b_{k}
of positive integers coprime with
p=\mathrm{char}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Then for any sequence
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}
of elements from
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, we have

\left|\Pr[\forall 1\leq i\leq k:\mathrm{tr}(A^{b_{i}})=a_{i}]-q^{-k}\right|<q^{-\frac{n^{2}}{2b_{k}}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n-1+b_{k}}{n}.

They have analogous results for other classical finite matrix groups, which we use throughout the present paper. In Section
3.4
we discuss the results and methods of
[
12
]
more extensively and we also prove a slight strengthening of the results for
GL_{n}
and
SL_{n}
, to include traces of negative powers.

2.4
p
-adic matrix groups and measures

We recall that throughout the paper,
p
is an odd prime. We fix some finite, unramified field extension
\mathcal{F}/\mathbb{Q}_{p}
with a ring of integers
\mathcal{O}
. We also denote by
\mathcal{K}/\mathcal{F}
a quadratic, unramified extension with a ring of integers
\mathcal{R}
. We recall the definitions of the various
p
-adic groups we consider and set some notation for the rest of the paper.

Definition 2.3
.

The group
GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
consists of all invertible matrices
M\in M_{n}(\mathcal{O})
with
M^{-1}\in M_{n}(\mathcal{O})
. The group
SL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
is the subgroup of
GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
of matrices with determinant
1
.

Definition 2.4
.

Let
\Omega_{n}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}&I_{n}\\
-I_{n}&\end{array}\right)
be the standard symplectic form. The group
Sp_{2n}(\mathcal{O})
is the subgroup of
GL_{2n}(\mathcal{O})
of matrices preserving the form defined by
\Omega_{n}
, that is of all matrices
M
with
M\Omega_{n}M^{t}=\Omega_{n}
.

Definition 2.5
.

The group
O_{n}(\mathcal{O})
is the subgroup of
GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
of matrices
M
satisfying
MM^{t}=I
. The group
SO_{n}(\mathcal{O})
is the subgroup of
O_{n}(\mathcal{O})
, consisting of matrices with determinant
1
.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
\tau
be the non-trivial Galois automorphism of
\mathcal{K}/\mathcal{F}
.
For a matrix
M\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{R})
, define
M^{*}
to be the matrix
\tau(M^{t})
. The group
U_{n}(\mathcal{O})
is the subgroup of
GL_{n}(\mathcal{R})
of matrices
M
satisfying
MM^{*}=I
.

We remark that these are not all the possible compact
p
-adic algebraic groups, and modifications of our methods could be used to treat some other groups.

2.4.1
Lifting from the mod
\pi
group

Throughout the paper
G
is one of the groups
GL_{n}
,
SL_{n}
,
Sp_{2n}
,
O_{n}
or
U_{n}
. We note that the
p
-adic groups project onto their mod
p
analogs. For an element
x\in\mathcal{O}
, define its mod
\pi^{k}
reduction
\rho_{k}(x)=x\pmod{\pi^{k}}
. For a matrix
M\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
, we define its mod
\pi^{k}
reduction
\rho_{k}(M)
by applying
\rho_{k}
on every entry of
M
. This defines a map
\rho_{k}
for every one of the groups
G
considered (we remark that for
U_{n}
the image is inside
GL_{n}(\mathcal{R}/\pi^{k})
and not
GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
). As a special case, when
k=1
, we denote
\overline{x}:=\rho_{1}(x)
and for a matrix
M\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
we denote
\overline{M}:=\rho_{1}(M)
.

We note that the
p
-adic groups project onto their
\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}
analogs. This allows us to carry out some of our main ideas in Section
4.2
below.

Lemma 2.7
.

Let
G
be one of the groups considered. Then the map
\rho_{k}:G(\mathcal{O})\to G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
is surjective.

Proof.

The claim follows from
[
22
, §3.3]
since every
G
we consider is reductive.
∎

2.4.2
Measures

Each of the groups we consider is equipped with a Haar probability measure, which is simply the limit of the uniform measures on the groups
G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
. We will translate our main theorems to claims about the uniform measures on the finite groups
G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
and prove the claims about the
\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}
groups.

Let
(\Omega,\Sigma)
be a measure space, and let
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
be two probability measures on
(\Omega,\Sigma)
. Then we recall that one defines the total variation distance of
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
by

d_{TV}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\sup_{A\in\Sigma}|\mu_{1}(A)-\mu_{2}(A)|.

(2.4)

Definition 2.8
.

Let
\mu
be a Borel probability measure on
\mathcal{O}^{d}
. We define a probability measure
\rho_{k}(\mu)
on
(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})^{d}
by setting
\rho_{k}(\mu)(x)=\mu(x+\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}^{d})
for every
x\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})^{d}
.

We remark that
\rho_{k}(\mu)=(\rho_{k})_{*}\mu
is the push-forward measure of
\mu
through the measurable map
\rho_{k}
.

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
\mu
be a Borel probability measure on
\mathcal{O}^{d}
. Let
A\subset\mathcal{O}^{d}
be a measurable set, and denote
A_{k}\subset(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})^{d}
the set
\rho_{k}(A)
. Then,
\lim_{k\to\infty}\rho_{k}(\mu)(A_{k})=\mu(A)
.

Proof.

Indeed, we have
\rho_{k}(\mu)(A_{k})=\mu(A+\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}^{d})
. Since
A\subset A+\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}^{d}
, we have
\rho_{k}(\mu)(A_{k})-\mu(A)=\mu((A+\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}^{d})-A)
. Define

B_{k}:=(A+\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}^{d})-A.

We note that

\ldots\subset B_{k}\subset\ldots\subset B_{2}\subset B_{1}

is a decreasing chain of sets and that
\cap_{k=1}^{\infty}B_{k}=\emptyset
. Hence,
\lim_{k\to\infty}\mu(B_{k})=0
, and the claim is proved.
∎

Lemma 2.10
.

Let
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
be two Borel probability measures on
\mathcal{O}^{d}
. Then,

d_{TV}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})=\lim_{k\to\infty}d_{TV}(\rho_{k}(\mu_{1}),\rho_{k}(\mu_{2})).

Proof.

We note that for every choice of
k
,
d_{TV}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\geq d_{TV}(\rho_{k}(\mu_{1}),\rho_{k}(\mu_{2}))
. Indeed, every subset
A
of
(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})^{d}
is measurable, and if we let
\tilde{A}
be some lift of
A
to
\mathcal{O}^{d}
, then
\rho_{k}(\mu_{i})(A)=\mu_{i}(\tilde{A}+\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}^{d})
(note that
\tilde{A}+\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}^{d}
is always measurable). Hence

|\rho_{k}(\mu_{1})(A)-\rho_{k}(\mu_{2})(A)|=|\mu_{1}(\tilde{A}+\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}^{d})-\mu_{2}(\tilde{A}+\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}^{d})|\leq d_{TV}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}),

and by taking the supremum over all choices of
A
we get
d_{TV}(\rho_{k}(\mu_{1}),\rho_{k}(\mu_{2}))\leq d_{TV}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
.

In the other direction, fix some
\epsilon>0
and take a measurable set
A\subset\mathcal{O}^{d}
such that
|\mu_{1}(A)-\mu_{2}(A)|\geq d_{TV}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})-\epsilon
. Define
A_{k}=\rho_{k}(A)
. We have, by the triangle inequality,

|\mu_{1}(A)-\mu_{2}(A)|\leq|\mu_{1}(A)-\rho_{k}(\mu_{1})(A_{k})|+|\rho_{k}(\mu_{1})(A_{k})-\rho_{k}(\mu_{2})(A_{k})|+|\mu_{2}(A)-\rho_{k}(\mu_{2})(A_{k})|.

We get that

|\rho_{k}(\mu_{1})(A_{k})-\rho_{k}(\mu_{2})(A_{k})|\geq d_{TV}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})-\epsilon-|\mu_{1}(A)-\rho_{k}(\mu_{1})(A_{k})|-|\mu_{2}(A)-\rho_{k}(\mu_{2})(A_{k})|.

Taking the limit
k\to\infty
and using Proposition
2.9
we get that
d_{TV}(\rho_{k}(\mu_{1}),\rho_{k}(\mu_{2}))\geq d_{TV}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})-\epsilon
. This is true for every
\epsilon>0
, hence we get
d_{TV}(\rho_{k}(\mu_{1}),\rho_{k}(\mu_{2}))\geq d_{TV}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})
and thus both sides are equal.
∎

3
Preliminary results

3.1
Polynomials with small radical

In what follows, we would need some results on the number of polynomials in
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
with a small radical. We recall that for a polynomial
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
, one can factor
f
into irreducibles as
P_{1}^{e_{1}}\cdots P_{k}^{e_{k}}
. The radical of
f
is the polynomial
\mathrm{rad}(f)=P_{1}\cdots P_{g}
.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
n,d
be natural numbers such that
d<n
. Let
g\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{\leq d}^{\mathrm{mon}}
be a fixed, squarefree polynomial. We denote

F_{g}(n)=|\{f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}:\mathrm{rad}(f)|g\}|.

We order the prime polynomials in
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
by the degree, breaking ties arbitrarily,

\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2},\ldots,\varphi_{k},\ldots

(3.1)

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
n,d
be natural numbers such that
d<n
. Let
g\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{\leq d}^{\mathrm{mon}}
be a fixed, squarefree polynomial. Let
g=P_{1}\cdots P_{r}
be the prime factorization of
g
.

1.

F_{g}(n)
is the number of solutions to the equation
e_{1}\deg(P_{1})+\cdots+e_{r}\deg(P_{r})=n
(in non-negative integers
e_{1},\ldots,e_{r}
).

2.

Assume that
P_{1},\ldots,P_{r}
are ordered w.r.t. the order defined in (
3.1
). Assume further that
(P_{1},\ldots,P_{r})\neq(\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{r})
. Then, there exist a squarefree polynomial
h\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]^{\mathrm{mon}}_{\leq d}
, with prime factorization
h=P_{1}\cdots P_{r-1}Q_{r}
, where
Q_{r}
is different from
P_{1},\ldots,P_{r}
and
Q_{r}
is smaller than
P_{r}
w.r.t. the order defined in (
3.1
), and such that
F_{h}(n)\geq F_{g}(n)
.

Proof.

1.

A monic polynomial
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}
such that
\mathrm{rad}(f)|g
has prime factorization
P_{1}^{e_{1}}\cdots P_{r}^{e_{r}}
, where
e_{i}
are non-negative integers. Conversely, every prime factorization of this form gives a unique polynomial counted by
F_{g}(n)
, hence the result.

2.

We divide the proof into two cases.

For all
\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
,
x-\alpha
is one of
(P_{i})_{i=1}^{r}
.
Choose
Q_{r}
to be the smallest polynomial among
\varphi_{1},\ldots,\varphi_{r}
which is not one of
P_{1},\ldots,P_{r}
. We construct an injection from the set of solutions of the equation

e_{1}\deg(P_{1})+\cdots+e_{r}\deg(P_{r})=n,

into the set of solutions of the equation

e_{1}^{\prime}\deg(P_{1})+\cdots+e_{r-1}^{\prime}\deg(P_{r-1})+e_{r}^{\prime}\deg(Q_{r})=n.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
\deg(P_{1})=1
. We construct the map by sending

(e_{1},\ldots,e_{r})\mapsto(e_{1}+e_{r}(\deg P_{r}-\deg Q_{r}),e_{2},\ldots,e_{r-1},e_{r}).

This is a bijection since
e_{2},\ldots,e_{r}
are determined from the image, and in particular since
e_{r}
is determined also
e_{1}
can be reconstructed (note that by our choice,
\deg Q_{r}\leq\deg P_{r}
so all numbers are non-negative).

There is some
\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
such that
x-\alpha
is not one of
(P_{i})_{i=1}^{r}
.
Denote this particular
\alpha
by
\alpha_{0}
. Take
Q_{r}=x-\alpha_{0}
. We construct an injection from the set of solutions of the equation

e_{1}\deg(P_{1})+\cdots+e_{r}\deg(P_{r})=n,

into the set of solutions of

e_{1}^{\prime}\deg(P_{1})+\cdots+e_{r-1}^{\prime}\deg(P_{r-1})+e_{r}^{\prime}\deg(x-\alpha_{0})=n.

This is done by sending the solution
(e_{1},\cdots,e_{r})\mapsto(e_{1},\cdots,e_{r-1},\deg(P_{r})\cdot e_{r})
. This is an injection hence we get the bound.

∎

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
n,d
natural numbers such that
d<n
. Then

|\{f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}:\deg\mathrm{rad}(f)\leq d\}|=q^{d+o(n)}.

Here, the rate of decay in
n
is uniform in
d
.

Proof.

We have

|\{f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}:\deg\mathrm{rad}(f)\leq d\}|\leq\sum_{h\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{\leq d}^{\mathrm{mon}}\atop{h\text{ squarefree}}}F_{h}(n).

We will prove that
F_{h}(n)=q^{o(n)}
for every squarefree
h
of degree
\leq d
(uniformly in
d
), and this will prove the claim since there are at most
q^{d}
such polynomials
h
. From now on fix some particular squarefree
h\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{\leq d}^{\mathrm{mon}}
. Write
h=P_{1}\cdots P_{r}
its prime factorization. Then by Proposition
3.2
,
F_{h}(n)
is the number of non-negative integer solutions to the equation

n=\deg(f)=e_{1}\deg(P_{1})+\cdots+e_{r}\deg(P_{r}).

Using Proposition
3.2
again, we see that we can replace
P_{r}
by some smaller polynomial (w.r.t. the order defined in (
3.1
)), without decreasing the number of possible solutions. Now Set
\pi_{q}(k)
to be the number of monic prime polynomials (from the prime polynomial theorem,
\pi_{q}(k)=\frac{q^{k}}{k}+O(q^{k/2})
). By the above, and possibly adding some new variables
e_{i}
, we may bound
F_{h}(n)
by the number of non-negative integer solutions to the equation

(e_{1}^{(1)}+\cdots+e_{\pi_{q}(1)}^{(1)})+2(e_{1}^{(2)}+\cdots+e_{\pi_{k}(2)}^{(2)})+\cdots+l(e_{1}^{(l)}+\cdots+e_{\pi_{q}(l)}^{(l)})=n,

(3.2)

where
l<\log_{q}(n)+1
is the least integer such that
\sum_{k=1}^{l}k\pi_{q}(k)>n
. Now let
\sigma\vdash n
be a partition of
n
, such that
i|\sigma(i)
. If we let
e_{1}^{(i)}+\cdots+e_{\pi_{q}(i)}^{(i)}=\frac{\sigma(i)}{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,l
be our new equivalent system of equations, we get that the number of possible solutions for Equation (
3.2
) is

\sum_{\sigma\vdash n}\prod_{i=1}^{l}\binom{\frac{\sigma(i)}{i}+\pi_{q}(i)-1}{\pi_{q}(i)-1}.

(3.3)

By the Hardy-Ramanujan formula for the partition function
[
14
]
, the number of summands in Equation
3.3
is
q^{o(n)}
. Hence it is enough to show that for every partition
\sigma\vdash n
,

\prod_{i=1}^{l}\binom{\frac{\sigma(i)}{i}+\pi_{q}(i)-1}{\pi_{q}(i)-1}=q^{o(n)}.

We note that since for any
l,k\geq 0
integers,
\binom{l}{k}<\frac{l^{k}}{k!}
. Using Striling’s formula and the prime polynomials theorem we get

\ln\left[\binom{\frac{\sigma(i)}{i}+\pi_{q}(i)-1}{\pi_{q}(i)-1}\right]=\ln\left[\binom{\frac{\sigma(i)}{i}+\frac{q^{i}}{i}+O(q^{i/2})}{\frac{q^{i}}{i}+O(q^{i/2})}\right]<\left(\frac{q^{i}}{i}+O(q^{i/2})\right)\ln\left(\frac{\sigma(i)+q^{i}+O(q^{i/2})}{q^{i}+O(q^{i/2})}\right)+\frac{q^{i}}{i}+O(q^{i/2}).

We note that

\sum_{i=1}^{l}\left(\frac{q^{i}}{i}+O(q^{i/2})\right)\ll\frac{q^{l}}{l}<n/l=o(n).

since
l\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
(to be precise,
l
grows logarithmically in
n
). Regarding the second term, it sums to

\ll\sum_{i=1}^{l}\frac{q^{i}}{i}\ln\left(1+\frac{\sigma(i)}{q^{i}}\right)=:S
. We fix some
A>0
, and divide the sum
S
to the terms with
q^{i}>A
and those with
q^{i}\leq A
:

\ll S=\sum_{i=1}^{l}\frac{q^{i}}{i}\ln\left(1+\frac{\sigma(i)}{q^{i}}\right)=\sum_{q^{i}\leq A}\frac{q^{i}}{i}\ln\left(1+\frac{\sigma(i)}{q^{i}}\right)+\sum_{q^{i}>A}\frac{\sigma(i)}{i}\frac{q^{i}}{\sigma(i)}\ln\left(1+\frac{\sigma(i)}{q^{i}}\right).

The function
x\mapsto\frac{\ln(1+x)}{x}
is bounded above by
1
, hence

\sum_{q^{i}>A}\frac{\sigma(i)}{i}\frac{q^{i}}{\sigma(i)}\ln\left(1+\frac{\sigma(i)}{q^{i}}\right)\leq\sum_{q^{i}>A}\frac{\sigma(i)}{i}<\frac{1}{\log_{q}(A)}\sum_{q^{i}>A}\sigma(i)<\frac{n}{\log_{q}(A)}.

If
q^{i}\leq A
we have
\ln\left(1+\frac{\sigma(i)}{q^{i}}\right)<\ln(n+1)
hence

\sum_{q^{i}\leq A}\frac{q^{i}}{i}\ln\left(1+\frac{\sigma(i)}{q^{i}}\right)<\ln(n+1)\sum_{q^{i}\leq A}\frac{q^{i}}{i}\ll\ln(n+1)\cdot A.

Overall we get that
S<\ln(n+1)\cdot A+\frac{n}{\log_{q}(A)}
. In particular,

\frac{S}{n}<\frac{A\ln(n+1)}{n}+\frac{1}{\log_{q}(A)}
, so that
\limsup\frac{S}{n}<\frac{1}{\log_{q}(A)}
. Since
A>0
was arbitrary, letting
A\to\infty
we get that
S=o(n)
.
∎

3.2
Characteristic polynomial coefficients and traces of powers

A basic concept we use is that of an interval in
(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
. This is an extension of an interval in
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
, which we define as follows:

Definition 3.4
.

Let
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
be a monic polynomial of degree
n
. We define

I(g,n-d)=\left\{h\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]:\text{ monic },\deg(h)=n,\deg(g-h)<n-d\right\}.

I(g,n-d)
is called the interval of length
n-d
around
g
.

To consider also traces of negative powers, we also define

Definition 3.5
.

Let
h\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
be a monic polynomial, such that
h(0)
is invertible in
\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}
. We define
h^{\mathrm{r}}=\frac{x^{n}h(1/x)}{h(0)}
. We define

I^{\mathrm{r}}(g,n-d)=\{h\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]\text{ monic },h(0)\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})^{\times},h^{\mathrm{r}}\in I(g,n-d)\}.

For later use, we will establish a formula relating the probability

\Pr_{M\in G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})}[\mathrm{tr}(M^{i})=a_{i},i=1,\ldots,d],a_{i}\in\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O},

to the probability

\Pr_{M\in G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})}[\mathrm{char}(M)\in I(g,n-d)],g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]\text{ monic}.

This will be done in Lemma
3.10
, which connects the distribution of traces of positive powers and the distribution of
\mathrm{char}(M)
in short intervals. In Lemma
3.14
we will generalize this connection, to include traces of negative powers. This time, the distribution of traces of powers will be related to the distribution of
\mathrm{char}(M)
in Hayes residue classes. Both Lemmas are analogs of
[
12
, Lemma 2.1]
and we will follow its proof closely. For our setting however more data need to be specified to determine the traces, thus we define the concept of
trace data
.

Definition 3.6
.

Let
k,n\geq 1
be integers, and let
1\leq d\leq n
.

1.

A trace data modulo
\pi^{k}
of length
d
is a sequence
(a_{i})_{1\leq i\leq\atop p^{k}\nmid i}
of elements
a_{i}
in
\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}
, such that for every
i
with
p^{k}\nmid i
, there is some
b_{i}\in\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}
so that
a_{i}=p^{r}b_{i}
, where
p^{r}||i
.

2.

A
p
-adic trace data of length
d
is a sequence
(\pi^{v_{p}(i)}a_{i})_{i=1}^{d}
, where
a_{i}\in\mathcal{O}
is some
p
-adic integer. Note that since
p
is unramified, we get the same object by picking a sequence
(\frac{a_{i}}{|i|_{p}})_{i=1}^{d}
.

We remark that if
(a_{i})_{i=1}^{d}
is a
p
-adic trace datum of length
d
, then the sequence
(a_{i}\text{ mod }\pi^{k})_{i=1\atop{p^{k}\nmid i}}^{d}
is a trace datum modulo
\pi^{k}
of length
d
. This could be an alternative definition for the trace data modulo
\pi^{k}
.

Definition 3.7
.

Define
S=S(d,k)=\left[\frac{d}{p}\right]+\left[\frac{d}{p^{2}}\right]+\cdots+\left[\frac{d}{p^{k}}\right]
. The number of different possible values for a
d
-trace data modulo
\pi^{k}
is
q^{kd-S}
.

We note the significance of the trace data. Since we are working in
\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}
or
\mathcal{O}
, there are dependencies between the traces of different powers. For example,
\mathrm{tr}(M^{p})=\mathrm{tr}(M)^{p}\pmod{\pi}
. This is generalized in the next lemma. The lemma generalizes a phenomenon first observed by Arnold
[
1
]
for
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, though he proved it only for the ring
\mathbb{Z}/p^{3}\mathbb{Z}
. Later the result was extended for the
p
-adics in
[
20
]
. The lemma generalizes it to
\mathcal{O}
.

We recall that we denote by
\sigma
the Frobenius automorphism
\left(\frac{\pi}{p}\right):\mathcal{O}\to\mathcal{O}
, and also its induced mod
\pi^{k}
action. We extend
\sigma
to act coordinate-wise on matrices and polynomials.

Lemma 3.8
.

Let
n,i,k\geq 1
be natural numbers, and let
M\in M_{n}(\mathcal{O})
be a matrix. Let
p^{j^{\prime}}||i
, and assume that
j^{\prime}\geq 1
. Then

\mathrm{tr}(M^{i})\equiv\sigma(\mathrm{tr}(M^{i/p}))\pmod{\pi^{j^{\prime}}}.

In particular, if we now let
M\in M_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
, and
j=\min(j^{\prime},k)
, then

\mathrm{tr}(M^{i})\equiv\sigma(\mathrm{tr}(M^{i/p}))\pmod{\pi^{j^{\prime}}}.

Proof.

Our proof is based on the main result of
[
20
]
which essentially proves the lemma for the case
\mathcal{O}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}
. Let
M\in M_{n}(\mathcal{O})
. We will prove that for any integer
k\geq 1
,

\mathrm{char}(M^{p^{k}})\equiv\mathrm{char}(\sigma(M)^{p^{k-1}})\equiv\sigma(\mathrm{char}(M^{p^{k-1}}))\pmod{\pi^{k}}.

(3.4)

This will prove the lemma by looking at the matrix
M^{i/p^{j}}
and looking at the next-to-leading coefficient of the polynomials in (
3.4
).

Let
e_{i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),i=1,\ldots,n
be the
i
-th symmetric polynomial (so
e_{i}
is homogenous of degree
i
,
e_{1}=x_{1}+\cdots+x_{n}
, etc.). Define as in
[
20
]
the polynomials
T_{m,i}
by the formula

e_{i}(x_{1}^{m},\ldots,x_{n}^{m})=T_{m,i}(e_{1}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),\ldots,e_{n}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})).

One can always write such an identity with
T_{m,i}\in\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
by the main theorem on symmetric polynomials (see
[
24
, Theorem 3.1.1]
). Now for a monic polynomial
f=x^{n}+\sum_{i=1}^{n-1}(-1)^{i}a_{i}x^{n-i}
, define
T_{m}(f)
(again, following
[
20
]
) by setting

T_{m}(f)=x^{n}+\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(-1)^{i}T_{m,i}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})x^{n-i}.

We remark that if
f=\mathrm{char}(M)
for some matrix
M\in M_{n}(\mathcal{O})
, then
T_{m}(\mathrm{char}(M))=\mathrm{char}(M^{m})
(See in
[
20
]
, in the discussion after lemma 2.3).
By
[
20
, Corollary 2.2]
,

T_{p,i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\equiv x_{i}^{p}\pmod{p},

that is,
T_{p,i}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=x_{i}^{p}+ph_{i}
, for some
h_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
. Since
\pi
divides
p
, we get that if we let
\mathrm{char}(M)=x^{n}+\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(-1)^{i}a_{i}x^{n-i}
, then

T_{p}(\mathrm{char}(M))\equiv x^{n}+\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}(-1)^{i}a_{i}^{p}x^{n-i}\pmod{\pi},

so that
\mathrm{char}(M^{p})=T_{p}(\mathrm{char}(M))\equiv\sigma(\mathrm{char}(M))\pmod{\pi}
. In particular, since we have the equality
\sigma(\mathrm{char}(M))=\mathrm{char}(\sigma(M))
, we proved (
3.4
) for
k=1
.

We will now prove (
3.4
) by induction on
k
. The base case for the induction was already proved, so assume we know the claim for
k\geq 1
. By
[
20
, Theorem 2.5]
, if
f,g\in\mathcal{O}[x]
are such that
f\equiv g\pmod{\pi^{k}}
then
T_{p}(f)\equiv T_{p}(g)\pmod{\pi^{k+1}}
. Thus using the induction hypothesis,

\mathrm{char}(M^{p^{k+1}})=T_{p}(\mathrm{char}(M^{p^{k}}))\equiv T_{p}(\mathrm{char}(\sigma(M)^{p^{k-1}}))=\mathrm{char}(\sigma(M)^{p^{k}})\pmod{\pi^{k}},

and we proved the result.
∎

Definition 3.9
.

Let
n,d\geq 1
be two integers such that
d<n
.

1.

For a matrix
M\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
, we define its trace data of length
d
,
\mathrm{TR}^{d}(M)
, to be the sequence
(\mathrm{tr}(M^{i})-1_{p|i}\sigma(\mathrm{tr}(M^{i/p})))_{i=1\atop{p^{k}\nmid i}}^{d}
.

2.

For a matrix
M\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
, we define its trace data of length
d
,
\mathrm{TR}^{d}(M)
, to be the following sequence
(\mathrm{tr}(M^{i})-1_{p|i}\sigma(\mathrm{tr}(M^{i/p})))_{i=1}^{d}
.

By Lemma
3.8
, the trace data of a matrix in
GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
is a trace data modulo
\pi^{k}
. The trace data of a matrix in
GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
is a
p
-adic trace data, again by the same lemma. Recall that we denote by
G_{n}
the
n\times n
group (e.g, if
G=GL
,
G_{n}=GL_{n}
, etc.).

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
d,n\geq 1
be integers, and assume that
d\leq n
. Let

(a_{i})_{1\leq i\leq d,p^{k}\nmid i}
be a trace datum. Let
S=S(d,k)=\left[\frac{d}{p}\right]+\left[\frac{d}{p^{2}}\right]+\cdots+\left[\frac{d}{p^{k}}\right]
. Finally, let
M\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
. There are monic polynomials of degree
n
,
(f_{i})_{1\leq i\leq q^{S}\subset(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]}
, depending on
d,n,k
, such that the following two conditions are equivalent:

1.

For all
1\leq i\leq d
with
p^{k}\nmid i
, we have
\mathrm{tr}(M^{i})-1_{p|i}\sigma(a_{i/p})=a_{i}
(i.e.,
\mathrm{TR}^{d}(M)=(a_{i})_{1\leq i\leq d,p^{k}\nmid i}
).

2.

For some
1\leq j\leq q^{S}
we have
\mathrm{char}(M)\in I(f_{j},n-d-1)
.

Moreover, the sets
\{I(f_{j},n-d-1)\}_{1\leq j\leq q^{S}}
are disjoint, so if
G
is one of the groups we consider then

\Pr_{M\in G_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})}[\forall 1\leq i\leq d:\mathrm{tr}(M^{i})-1_{p|i}\sigma(a_{i/p})=a_{i}]=\sum_{j=1}^{q^{S}}\Pr_{M\in G_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})}[\mathrm{char}(M)\in I(f_{j},n-d-1)].

Proof.

For
M\in G_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
, write its characteristic polynomial as

\mathrm{char}(M)=x^{n}+e_{1}(M)x^{n-1}+e_{2}(M)x^{n-2}+\cdots+e_{n}(M),

where
(-1)^{i}e_{i}(M)
is the
i
-th symmetric polynomial in the eigenvalues of
M
. Using Newton’s identities (See
[
24
, §3.1.1]
), we can write

\mathrm{tr}(M^{i})=-ie_{i}(M)+P_{i}(e_{1}(M),\ldots,e_{i-1}(M)),

(3.5)

for some explicit
P_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]
. Let
p^{j^{\prime}(i)}||i
,
j(i)=\min(k,j^{\prime}(i))
. Then equation (
3.5
) determines
e_{i}(M)\pmod{\pi^{k-j(i)}}
, if
\mathrm{tr}(M^{i})
is given and the previous coefficients
e_{1}(M),\ldots,e_{i-1}(M)
were already determined. In particular,
e_{i}(M)
has
q^{j(i)}
options. Thus iteratively we get that
\mathrm{char}(M)
is in a set of intervals
\{I(f_{j},n-d-1)\}_{1\leq j\leq q^{S}}
, determined completely by the traces of powers, and of size
q^{S}
.

This works in the other direction as well: let
f_{r}
be one of the polynomials constructed above,
f_{r}=x^{n}+a_{1}^{(r)}+x^{n-1}+\cdots+a_{n}^{(r)}
(only the
d
next-to-leading coefficients are relevant, the others may be picked arbitrarily). If
e_{i}(M)=a_{i}^{(r)}
, for

i=1,\ldots,d
, then equation (
3.5
) determines all the traces of powers
\mathrm{tr}(M^{i})
, for
i=1,\ldots,d
. Moreover, the resulting traces of powers do not depend on the choice of
r
, since
a_{i}^{(r)}\equiv a_{i}^{(r^{\prime})}\pmod{\pi^{k-j(i)}}
, and only this joint value is relevant for equation (
3.5
).
∎

Finally, we define random variables
\mathrm{UTR}_{d}
capturing the distribution of “uniformly random” trace data. We also define the random variable
\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{G}
, assigning to a matrix
M\in G(\mathcal{O})
its
d
-trace data.

Definition 3.11
.

Let
d,n
be integers with
1\leq d<n
, and let
G
be one of the groups considered in the paper.

1.

Define the random variable
\mathrm{UTR}_{d}
to be the sequence
(u_{i})_{i=1}^{d}
of
d

p
-adic integers, such that
u_{i}\sim\frac{1}{|i|_{p}}U(\mathcal{O})
are independent random variables, and
U(\mathcal{O})
is the uniform random variable (w.r.t. the Haar probability measure).

2.

Define the associated
\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}
random variable
\mathrm{UTR}_{d}^{k}
via the equation
\mathrm{UTR}_{d}\pmod{\pi^{k}}
.

3.

Define
\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{G}
to be the random variable
\mathrm{TR}^{d}(M)
where
M
is distributed uniformly at random w.r.t. the Haar probability measure on
G(\mathcal{O})
.

4.

Define the associated
\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}
random variable
\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{G,k}=\mathrm{TR}_{d}(M)
where
M\in G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
is chosen uniformly at random from
G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
(equivalently, it is
\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{G}\pmod{\pi^{k}}
).

When
G\neq GL_{n},SL_{n}
, the traces of negative powers for a matrix
M\in G
may be directly obtained from the traces of positive powers. For example, if
M\in O_{n}(\mathcal{O})
, then since
M^{-1}=M^{t}
we get that for a positive integer
k
,
\mathrm{tr}(M^{-k})=\mathrm{tr}(M^{k})
. We thus consider the traces of negative powers only for
GL_{n},SL_{n}
, and for the rest of the subsection, we assume
G
is one of them.

Definition 3.12
.

Let
0\leq d_{1},d_{2}<n
be two integers such that
d_{1}+d_{2}=d<n-1
. A
p
-adic
(d_{1},d_{2})
-trace data is obtained by a taking a sequence
(\frac{a_{i}}{|i|_{p}})_{0\neq i=-d_{1}}^{d_{2}}
, where
a_{i}\in\mathcal{O}
are some
p
-adic integers. A
(d_{1},d_{2})
-trace data for
\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}
is obtained by reducing the
p
-adic trace data modulo
\pi^{k}
. The length of the trace data is
d=d_{1}+d_{2}
.

Definition 3.13
.

For a matrix
M\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
, denote the
d_{1}
-trace data for
M^{-1}
by
(a_{-i})_{i=1}^{d_{1}}
and the
d_{2}
-trace data for
M
by
(a_{i})_{i=1}^{d_{2}}
. Define the
(d_{1},d_{2})
-trace data of
M
, denoted by
\mathrm{TR}^{d_{1},d_{2}}(M)
, by the sequence
(a_{i})_{0\neq i=-d_{1}}^{d_{2}}
. We define similarly the
(d_{1},d_{2})
-trace data for a matrix
M\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
.

As a corollary of the above, we get

Lemma 3.14
.

Fix
d_{1},d_{2}\in[n]
such that
d_{1}+d_{2}=d<n-1
, and let

\mathfrak{a}
be a sequence of
(d_{1},d_{2})
-trace data. Let
S=S(d,k)=\left[\frac{d}{p}\right]+\left[\frac{d}{p^{2}}\right]+\cdots+\left[\frac{d}{p^{k}}\right]
. There are monic polynomials of degree
n
,

\{f_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq q^{S(d_{1},k)+S(d_{2},k)+k}\subset(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]},

such that the following two conditions are equivalent:

1.

The
(d_{1},d_{2})
-trace data of
M
is equal to
\mathfrak{a}
.

2.

For some
1\leq i\leq q^{S(d_{1},k)+S(d_{2},k)+k}
we have
\mathrm{char}(M)\equiv f_{i}\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}}
.

Moreover, the residue classes of
R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}
are disjoint, so if
G\subset GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
is a set of matrices with a fixed determinant then

\Pr_{M\in G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})}\left[M\text{ has }(d_{1},d_{2})\text{-trace data equal to }\mathfrak{a}\right]=\\
=\sum_{i=1}^{q^{S(d_{1},k)+S(d_{2},k)+k}}\Pr_{M\in G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})}\left[\mathrm{char}(M)\equiv f_{i}\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}}\right].

Proof.

By Lemma
3.10
, having a
(d_{1},d_{2})
-trace data equal to
\mathfrak{a}
translates to having
\mathrm{char}(M)
in
I(f_{i},n-d_{1})
for one of
q^{S(d_{1},k)}
polynomials and simultaneously
\mathrm{char}(M)\in I^{\mathrm{r}}(g_{j},n-d_{2})
for one of
q^{S(d_{2},k)}
polynomials. Note however that being in
I(f_{i},n-d_{1})\cap I^{\mathrm{r}}(g_{j},n-d_{2})
determines
q^{k}
Hayes residue classes in
R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}
, since the free coefficient is not determined.
∎

Definition 3.15
.

Let
0\leq d_{1},d_{2}<n
be two integers such that
d_{1}+d_{2}=d<n-1
, and let
G
be one of the matrix groups considered in the paper.

1.

Define the random variable
\mathrm{UTR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}
to be the sequence
(u_{i})_{0\neq i=-d_{1}}^{d_{2}}
of
d

p
-adic integers, such that
u_{i}\sim\frac{1}{|i|_{p}}U(\mathcal{O})
are independent random variables, and
U(\mathcal{O})
is the uniform (w.r.t. the Haar probability measure) random variable.

2.

Define the associated
\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}
random variable
\mathrm{UTR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{k}
by
\mathrm{UTR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}\pmod{\pi^{k}}
.

3.

Define
\mathrm{TR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{G}
to be
\mathrm{TR}^{d_{1},d_{2}}(M)
for a matrix
M
which is distributed uniformly at random w.r.t. the Haar probability measure on
G(\mathcal{O})
.

4.

The associated
\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}
random variable is denoted by
\mathrm{TR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{G,k}
, and is defined by
\mathrm{TR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{G}\pmod{\pi^{k}}
.

As a final remark, we note that the number of different possible values for a
(d_{1},d_{2})
-trace data is
q^{d-S(d_{1},k)-S(d_{2},k)}
.

3.3
Hayes characters of palindromic polynomials

Definition 3.16
.

Let
n\geq 1
be an integer.

1.

A polynomial
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{\leq n}
is called
n
-palindromic (or just palindromic, if the value of
n
is clear from the context) if
x^{n}f(1/x)=f(x)
.

2.

A monic polynomial
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
with
f(0)\neq 0
is called self-reciprocal if
x^{\deg f}\frac{f(1/x)}{f(0)}=f(x)
.

We remark that the two notions are related: e.g, if
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}
is self-reciprocal such that
f(0)=1
, then
f
is
\deg(f)
-palindromic. Conversely, if
\deg(f)=n
,
f(0)=1
, and
f
is
n
-palindromic, then
f
is self-reciprocal. Also, if
f
is a polynomial of degree
<n
and
f
is
n
-palindromic, then
x^{n}+f+1
is self-reciprocal (note that the free coefficient of
f
must be
0
). Every monic,
n
-palindromic polynomial can be obtained this way.

We recall that in this paper, we denote the set of monic polynomials in
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
by
\mathcal{M}_{q}
. We denote the set of monic, self-reciprocal polynomials in
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
by
\mathcal{M}_{q}^{\mathrm{sr}}
. Finally, we denote the set of
n
-palindromic polynomials of degree
\leq n
(or
<n
, or
=n
) by
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]^{\mathrm{pal}}_{\leq n}
(or
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]^{\mathrm{pal}}_{<n}
, or
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]^{\mathrm{pal}}_{=n}
). Self-reciprocal polynomials and their relations with Hayes characters were studied in
[
12
]
, and the key construction there is that of the map

\Psi:\mathcal{M}_{q}\to\mathcal{M}_{q}^{sr},g\mapsto g\left(x+\frac{1}{x}\right)x^{\deg(g)},

and its inverse
\Phi
. We adapt the results in
[
12
, Lemma 6.12, Proposition 6.13]
to a slightly different setting which we will use later.

Proposition 3.17
.

Define
\Psi_{2n}:\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}\to\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=2n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
by
\Psi_{2n}(g)=g\left(x+\frac{1}{x}\right)x^{n}
. Then,

1.

\Psi_{2n}
is injective.

2.

The image of
\Psi_{2n}
is precisely
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]^{\mathrm{pal}}_{=2n}\cap\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=2n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
.

3.

Denote the inverse of
\Psi_{2n}
, defined on
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]^{\mathrm{pal}}_{=2n}\cap\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=2n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
, by
\Phi_{2n}
. Let
\chi\in\hat{R}_{l,1}
. There is a unique
\chi^{\prime}\in\hat{R}_{l,1}
such that
\chi(f)=\chi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2n}(f))
for every
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]^{\mathrm{pal}}_{=2n}\cap\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=2n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
. Moreover,
\chi
is non-trivial iff
\chi^{\prime}
is non-trivial.

Proof.

The fact that
\Psi_{2n}
is injective follows from the fact that
\Psi
is injective, proved in
[
12
, Lemma 6.12]
. We note further that by direct computation, for every
g
,
\Psi_{2n}(g)
is palindromic. The set of monic
2n
-palindromic polynomials of degree
2n
is a vector space of dimension
n
, hence
\Psi_{2n}
cover all monic
2n
palindromic polynomials.

For the last assertion, define

\chi^{\prime}(g)=\chi(g\left(x+\frac{1}{x}\right)x^{\deg(g)})
. By
[
12
, Proposition 6.13]
, this function is a short interval character of
l
coefficients and is non-trivial iff
\chi
is non-trivial. Hence, denoting
g=\Phi_{2n}(f)
,
\chi(f)=\chi(g\left(x+\frac{1}{x}\right)x^{n})=\chi^{\prime}(g)
as claimed.
∎

We adapt this also to palindromic polynomials of odd degrees. We note that if
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=2n-1}^{\mathrm{pal}}
, then
f(-1)=0
. Hence
f=(x+1)g
, where
g
is
(2n-2)
-palindromic. We thus define
\Psi_{2n-1}:\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n-1}^{\mathrm{mon}}\to\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=2n-1}^{\mathrm{pal}}
by
\Psi_{2n-1}(g)=(x+1)g\left(x+\frac{1}{x}\right)x^{n-1}
.

Proposition 3.18
.

Define
\Psi_{2n-1}:\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n-1}^{\mathrm{mon}}\to\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=2n-1}^{\mathrm{pal}}
by
\Psi_{2n-1}(g)=(x+1)g\left(x+\frac{1}{x}\right)x^{n-1}
by
\Psi_{2n-1}(g)=(x+1)g\left(x+\frac{1}{x}\right)x^{n-1}
. Then,

1.

\Psi_{2n-1}
is injective.

2.

The image of
\Psi_{2n-1}
is precisely
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]^{\mathrm{pal}}_{=2n-1}\cap\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=2n-1}^{\mathrm{mon}}
.

3.

Denote the inverse of
\Psi_{2n-1}
, defined on
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]^{\mathrm{pal}}_{=2n-1}\cap\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=2n-1}^{\mathrm{mon}}
, by
\Phi_{2n-1}
. Let
\chi\in\hat{R}_{l,1}
. There is a unique
\chi^{\prime}\in\hat{R}_{l,1}
and a constant
\alpha(\chi)\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
such that
\chi(f)=\alpha(\chi)\chi^{\prime}(\Phi_{2n-1}(f))
for every
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]^{\mathrm{pal}}_{=2n-1}\cap\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=2n-1}^{\mathrm{mon}}
. Moreover,
\chi
is non-trivial iff
\chi^{\prime}
is non-trivial, and in the case that
\chi
is trivial,
\alpha(\chi)=1
.

Proof.

The fact that
\Psi_{2n-1}
is injective follows from the claim on
\Psi_{2n}
. Regarding the image of
\Psi_{2n-1}
, we note that
\Psi_{2n-1}(g)
is always monic and palindromic, and that the dimension of the linear space
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n-1}
is
n-1
which is equal to the dimension of
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]^{\mathrm{pal}}_{=2n-1}\cap\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=2n-1}^{\mathrm{mon}}
.

Finally, let
\chi
be a short interval character of length
l
. Now
f=(x+1)f_{0}
where
f_{0}
is
(2n-2)
-palindromic. Here,
\Phi_{2n-1}(f)=\Psi_{2n-2}(f_{0})
. Denote their joint value by
g
. Then by the last proposition, there is a unique
\chi^{\prime}
such that
\chi(f_{0})=\chi^{\prime}(g)
. Letting
\alpha(\chi)=\chi(x+1)
we get the result.
∎

We now prove an orthogonality relation for Hayes characters evaluated at palindromic polynomials.

Lemma 3.19
.

Let
n
be an integer and let
\delta=\lceil\frac{n-1}{2}\rceil
. Let
\chi
be a short interval character of
l
coefficients, with
l\leq\delta
. Then,

q^{-\delta}\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<n}^{\mathrm{pal}}}\chi(x^{n}+f)=1_{\chi=\chi_{0}}.

Proof.

Since
\delta\geq l
, we have in particular that
n>l
and we may change the free coefficient of the polynomial without changing the value of the character. Hence,

q^{-\delta}\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<n}^{\mathrm{pal}}}\chi(x^{n}+f)=q^{-\delta}\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<n}^{\mathrm{pal}}}\chi(x^{n}+f+1).

We use the map
\Psi_{n}:\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=\delta}^{\mathrm{mon}}\to\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{pal}}\cap\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
. By Propositions
3.17
,
3.18
we have a unique character
\chi^{\prime}
and a constant
\alpha(\chi)
(which is
1
if
\chi=\chi_{0}
) such that

\chi(x^{n}+f+1)=\alpha(\chi)\chi^{\prime}(\Phi_{n}(x^{n}+f+1))
. In particular, by the orthogonal relations for Hayes characters (
2.1
),

q^{-\delta}\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<n}^{\mathrm{pal}}}\chi(x^{n}+f+1)=q^{-\delta}\alpha(\chi)\sum_{g\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=\delta}^{\mathrm{mon}}}\chi^{\prime}(g)=1_{\chi=\chi_{0}}.

∎

We will also consider the closely related notions of
*
-symmetric polynomials and skew-palindromic polynomials. Let
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}/\mathbb{F}_{q}
be a quadratic extension, and let
\tau
denote the action of
q
-Frobenius on
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}
, that is
\tau(\alpha)=\alpha^{q}
for
\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}
.
Let
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<n}
be a polynomial, and let
\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}
be such that
\alpha\tau(\alpha)=1
. Also for a polynomial
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}(x)
, define
f^{*}(x)=x^{n}\tau(f)(1/x)
. We say that
f
is
\alpha
-skew palindromic polynomial (or simply skew-palindromic polynomial, if
\alpha
is clear from the context) if
\frac{f^{*}(x)}{\tau(\alpha)}=f
. If we want to emphasize the dependence on
n
, we say that
f
is skew-palindromic w.r.t.
n
.

We note that due to the assumption on
\alpha
,
f\mapsto\frac{f^{*}(x)}{\tau(\alpha)}
is an involution on
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<n}
. We denote the set of
\alpha
-skew palindromic polynomials of degree
<n
(or
\leq n
,
=n
) by
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<n}^{\alpha\text{-skpal}}
(or
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{\leq n}^{\alpha-\mathrm{skpal}}
,
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{=n}^{\alpha-\mathrm{skpal}}
respectively), or simply
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<n}^{\text{skpal}}
(or
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{\leq n}^{\mathrm{skpal}}
,
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{skpal}}
respectively) if
\alpha
is clear from the context. When
\alpha=1
, we call the polynomial
*
-symmetric, and we denote the set of
*
-symmetric polynomials of degree
<n
(or
\leq n
,
=n
) by
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<n}^{*-\mathrm{sym}}
(or
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{\leq n}^{*-\mathrm{sym}}
,
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{=n}^{*-\mathrm{sym}}
respectively). For the rest of the subsection, skew-palindromic or
*
-symmetric polynomials are assumed to be skew-palindromic w.r.t.
n
.

Claim 3.20
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<n}^{\alpha-\mathrm{skpal}}
. Then there exist a
\beta=\beta(\alpha)\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}
and
g\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<n}^{*-\mathrm{sym}}
such that
\beta f=g
.

Proof.

By Hilbert’s theorem 90, one can write
\alpha=\frac{\tau(\beta)}{\beta}
. Hence,

f^{*}(x)=\tau(\alpha)f\iff(\beta f)^{*}=\tau(\beta)f^{*}(x)=\beta f.

∎

We see that to study the structure of
\alpha
skew-palindromic polynomials it is enough to study the structure of
*
-symmetric polynomials.

Claim 3.21
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<n}^{*-\mathrm{sym}}
. Then,

1.

If
n
is odd, there exist a unique
h\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{\leq\frac{n-1}{2}}
with
h(0)=0
such that
f(x)=h(x)+h^{*}(x)
.

2.

If
n
is even, there exist a unique
h\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{\leq\frac{n-1}{2}}
with
h(0)=0
and
\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
such that
f(x)=h(x)+\alpha x^{d}+h^{*}(x)
.

Moreover, any choice of
h
gives a
*
-symmetric polynomial. In each case, the
l
next-to-leading coefficients of
x^{n}+f
, for
l\leq\frac{n-1}{2}
, are simply the
l
next-to-leading coefficients of
x^{n}+h^{*}
.

Proof.

Note that for
f
to be in
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<n}^{*\text{-sym}}
,
f(0)
must be
0
. We split into cases depending on the parity of
n
.

n=2d+1
is odd.

Write
f(x)=a_{1}x+a_{2}x^{2}+\cdots+a_{2d}x^{2d}
, the condition that
f
is
*
-symmetric is equivalent to requiring
a_{2d+1-i}=\tau(a_{i})
. Setting
h(x)=a_{1}x+\cdots+a_{d}x^{d}
we get the result.

n=2d
is even.

Write
f(x)=a_{1}x+a_{2}x^{2}+\cdots+a_{2d-1}x^{2d-1}
, the condition that
f
is
*
-symmetric is equivalent to requiring
a_{2d-i}=\tau(a_{i})
. In particular,
\tau(a_{d})=a_{d}
and thus
a_{d}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Thus, setting
h=a_{1}x+\cdots+a_{d-1}x^{d-1}
we get the result.
∎

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
n
be an integer and set
\delta=\lceil\frac{n-1}{2}\rceil
.
Let
\chi
be a short interval character of
l
coefficients, with
l\leq\delta
. Then,

q^{-\delta}\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<n}^{\alpha-\mathrm{skpal}}}\chi(x^{n}+f)=1_{\chi=\chi_{0}}.

Proof.

We first note by Claim
3.20
there is a
\beta\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}
such that
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<n}^{\alpha\text{-skpal}}=\beta\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<n}^{*\text{-sym}}
. In particular,

q^{-\delta}\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<n}^{\alpha-\mathrm{skpal}}}\chi(x^{n}+f)=q^{-\delta}\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<n}^{*-\mathrm{sym}}}\chi(x^{n}+\beta f).

We now split the proof for the case of
n
even and
n
odd, and use Claim
3.21
.

n
odd.

In this case

q^{-\delta}\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<n}^{*-\mathrm{sym}}}\chi(x^{n}+\beta f)=q^{-\delta}\sum_{h\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<\delta}}\chi(x^{n}+\beta h+\beta h^{*}).

Here
\chi(x^{n}+\beta f)=\chi(\beta h^{*})
, and since
h
is arbitrary, the last sum is equal to
1_{\chi=\chi_{0}}
by the Hayes orthogonality relation (
2.1
).

n
even.

In this case

q^{-\delta}\sum_{f\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<n}^{*-\mathrm{sym}}}\chi(x^{n}+\beta f)=q^{-\delta}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{q}}\sum_{h\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<\delta-1}}\chi(x^{n}+\beta h+\beta\alpha x^{\delta}+\beta h^{*})=1_{\chi=\chi_{0}},

again using the Hayes orthogonality relation.
∎

3.4
Traces of negative powers for the finite groups

We show that slight modifications in the proofs of
[
12
]
lead to results on the traces of negative powers for
GL_{n},SL_{n}
. For the other groups in consideration, traces of negative powers depend simply on the traces of positive powers:

1.

For
M\in Sp_{2n}(\mathcal{O})
we have
M\Omega_{n}M^{t}=\Omega_{n}
. In particular
M^{-1}=\Omega_{n}M^{t}\Omega_{n}^{-1}
so that
\mathrm{tr}(M^{-1})=\mathrm{tr}(M^{t})=\mathrm{tr}(M)
. This generalizes to all negative powers of
M
.

2.

For
SO_{n}(\mathcal{O})
we have
MM^{t}=I
so that
M^{-1}=M^{t}
, and in particular
\mathrm{tr}(M^{-1})=\mathrm{tr}(M^{t})=\mathrm{tr}(M)
. Similarly to the case of
Sp_{2n}
, this generalizes to all negative powers of
M
.

3.

For
U_{n}(\mathcal{O})
we have
MM^{*}=I
, so that
M^{-1}=M^{*}
. In particular,
\mathrm{tr}(M^{-1})=\mathrm{tr}(M^{*})=\mathrm{tr}(\sigma(M^{t}))=\sigma(\mathrm{tr}(M))
. This generalizes to all negative powers of
M
.

Fix a non-trivial additive character
\psi
of
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. For a vector
\overrightarrow{\lambda}=(\lambda_{i})_{i=1\atop{p\nmid i}}^{k}
defined over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
and
f\in\mathcal{M}_{q}
, Gorodetsky-Rodgers define in
[
12
, §2.2]
the function
\chi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}(f)=\psi(\sum_{i=1\atop{p\nmid i}}^{k}\lambda_{i}(\alpha_{1}^{i}+\cdots+\alpha_{n}^{i}))
, where
\alpha_{i}
are the roots of
f
in
\overline{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
, listed with multiplicities. We extend this notation to include cases where the powers
i
can be negative (but are always non-zero).

Claim 3.23
.

Let
k
be a positive integer coprime with
p
, and let
\overrightarrow{\lambda}=(\lambda_{-i})_{i=1\atop{p\nmid i}}^{k}\subset\mathbb{F}_{q}
with
\lambda_{k}\neq 0
. Let

\overrightarrow{\eta}=(\eta_{i})_{i=1\atop{p\nmid i}}^{k}=(\lambda_{-i})_{i=1\atop{p\nmid i}}^{k}
. Then,
\chi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}(f)=\chi_{\overrightarrow{\eta}}(f^{\mathrm{r}})
. In particular, the function
\chi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}(f)
is a Dirichlet character with modulus
x^{k+1}
. It is non-trivial when
k>0
.

Proof.

By
[
12
, Lemma 2.2]
,
\chi_{\overrightarrow{\eta}}(f^{\mathrm{r}})
is a short interval character of
k
coefficients evaluated at the polynomial
f^{\mathrm{r}}
. That means that it is a multiplicative function depending only on the
k
next-to-leading coefficients of
f^{\mathrm{r}}
, which in turn, depend only on the coefficients of
1,x,x^{2},\ldots,x^{k}
in
f
. Since clearly
\chi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}(f)
is multiplicative we get that it is a Dirichlet character modulo
x^{k+1}
.

To see that
\chi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}
is non-trivial, notice that
f\mapsto f^{\mathrm{r}}
is an involution on the monic polynomials and use the fact that
\chi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}(f)
is non-trivial (proved in
[
12
, Lemma 2.2]
).
∎

Lemma 3.24
.

Let
l,k
be two non-negative integers, and let
\overrightarrow{\lambda}=(\lambda_{i})_{p\nmid i=-l}^{k}\subset\mathbb{F}_{q}
. The function
\chi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}(f)
is a Hayes character modulo
R_{k,x^{l+1}}
.

Proof.

Indeed,
\chi_{\overrightarrow{\lambda}}(f)
is the product of a short interval character of
k
coefficients with a Dirichlet character of modulus
x^{l+1}
.
∎

We can get as a result an extension of the results of Gorodetsky-Rodgers for negative traces as well, and also results on equidistribution in Hayes residue classes.

Corollary 3.25
.

Let
M\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be a matrix chosen uniformly at random w.r.t. the Haar measure. Let
(b_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
be an increasing sequence of integers coprime with
p
. Let
d=\max(0,b_{k})-\min(0,b_{1})+1
.

Then, for any sequence
(a_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
of elements in
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, we have

\left|\Pr_{M\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}\left[(\mathrm{tr}(M^{b_{i}}))_{i=1}^{k}=(a_{i})_{i=1}^{k}\right]-q^{-k}\right|\leq q^{-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+d}{n}.

Now let
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
be a monic polynomial. Let
0\leq d_{1},d_{2}<n
be integers such that
d_{1}+d_{2}=d<n-1
. Then,

\left|\Pr_{M\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}\left[\mathrm{char}(M)\equiv f\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}}}}\right]-q^{-d}\right|<q^{-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+d}{n}.

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as the proof of
[
12
, Theorem 1.1]
and
[
12
, Theorem 1.3]
, using our Lemma
3.24
.
∎

Corollary 3.26
.

Let
M\in SL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be a matrix chosen uniformly at random w.r.t. the Haar measure. Let
(b_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
be an increasing sequence of integers coprime with
p
. Let
d=\max(0,b_{k})-\min(0,b_{1})+1
.

Then, for any sequence
(a_{i})_{i=1}^{k}
of elements in
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, we have

\left|\Pr_{M\in SL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}\left[(\mathrm{tr}(M^{b_{i}}))_{i=1}^{k}=(a_{i})_{i=1}^{k}\right]-q^{-k}\right|\leq q^{-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}}(q-1)\left(1+\frac{1}{q-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+d}{n}.

Now let
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
be a monic polynomial with
f(0)=(-1)^{n}
. Let
0\leq d_{1},d_{2}<n
be integers such that
d_{1}+d_{2}=d<n-1
. Then,

\left|\Pr_{M\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}\left[\mathrm{char}(M)\equiv f\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}}}}\right]-q^{-d}\right|<q^{-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}}(q-1)\left(1+\frac{1}{q-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+d}{n}.

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as the proof of
[
12
, Theorem 4.6]
and
[
12
, Theorem 4.5]
, using our Lemma
3.24
.
∎

4
Finite groups of Lie type and their Lie algebras

4.1
Finite matrix groups

For every group considered in this paper, we recall some basic facts which will be used later, regarding the Jordan type decomposition of the group and some related constructions.

4.1.1
GL_{n}
and
SL_{n}

Recall that
GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is the set of invertible
n\times n
matrices over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
,
SL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\subset GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
the set of matrices with determinant
=1
. It is well known that the associated Lie algebras
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
are
M_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,
\{M\in M_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|\mathrm{tr}(M)=0\}
(respectively). We note that
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is invariant under conjugation from
GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, a property that will be used later.

Let
M
be a matrix in either group. The vector space
V=\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}
becomes a
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
-module with the action
f(x)v=f(M)v
, for every
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
,
v\in V
. A polynomial
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
is called prime if it is irreducible and monic. Let
\mathcal{P}
be the set of primes in
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
. By the structure theorem for finitely generated modules over a PID, one can decompose

V\cong\bigoplus_{f\in\mathcal{P}}\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k_{f}}\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]/(f)^{m_{i}}\cong\bigoplus_{f\in\mathcal{P}}V_{f},V_{f}\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k_{f}}\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]/(f)^{m_{i}}.

Here
V_{f}
is called the
f
-primary part. The primary components of
M
in
GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
(or in
SL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
) determine its conjugacy class in
GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
(or in
SL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
).

Definition 4.1
.

For a matrix
M\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
