we define the conjugacy class data of
M
to be the multiset
(f^{m_{i}})_{f\in\mathcal{P}\atop{i=1,\cdots,k_{f}}}
.

Finally, we want to understand how to combine matrices with different conjugacy class data. The idea is to take the direct sum of the spaces, and in the case of
GL_{n},SL_{n}
this is straightforward.

Definition 4.2
.

Let
A,B\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q}),GL_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Their direct sum is the matrix
A\oplus B\in GL_{n+m}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, which in block form looks as

A\oplus B=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A&0\\
0&B\end{array}\right).

Let
(f^{m_{i}})_{f\in\mathcal{P}\atop{i=1,\ldots,k_{f}}}
,
(f^{e_{j}})_{f\in\mathcal{P}\atop{j=1,\ldots,l_{f}}}
be two conjugacy class data. We define their join to be the union of conjugacy class data (counted multiplicities),
(f^{m_{i}})_{f\in\mathcal{P}\atop{i=1,\ldots,k_{f}}}\cup(f^{e_{j}})_{f\in\mathcal{P}\atop{j=1,\ldots,l_{f}}}
. A direct computation shows that the conjugacy class data of
A\oplus B
is the join of the conjugacy class data for
A
,
B
.

4.1.2
The symplectic group

Throughout the paper
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is a finite field of characteristic
p\neq 2
. Define the standard alternating form via the matrix
\Omega_{n}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&I_{n}\\
-I_{n}&0\end{array}\right)
. We define the symplectic group

Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=\left\{M\in GL_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|M^{t}\Omega_{n}M=\Omega_{n}\right\}.

The associated Lie algebra is the algebra

\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=\left\{X\in M_{2n\times 2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q}):\Omega_{n}X+X^{t}\Omega_{n}=0\right\}.

More explicitly, writing an element
X
in
\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
as a
2\times 2
block matrix by

X=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A&B\\
C&D\end{array}\right),

we get that
X\in\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is equivalent to
B,C
being symmetric and
A^{t}=-D
. We note that the characteristic polynomial of an element in the symplectic group admits symmetry.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
be a polynomial such that
f(0)\neq 0
. We define its reciprocal by the formula
f^{\mathrm{r}}(x)=\frac{x^{\deg f}f(1/x)}{f(0)}
.

For
g\in Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,
\mathrm{char}(g)
is self-reciprocal (i.e,
\mathrm{char}(g)^{\mathrm{r}}=\mathrm{char}(g)
). We call a polynomial
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
reciprocally irreducible if it cannot be decomposed (non-trivially) to self-reciprocal polynomials, and we denote the set of reciprocally irreducible polynomials by
\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{r}}
. As an example, over
\mathbb{F}_{5}
,
x^{2}-4
is in
\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{r}}
even though it is decomposable in
\mathbb{F}_{5}[x]
. The minimal polynomial of
g
,
\min(g)
, is also self-reciprocal.

Similarly to the
GL_{n}
case, the Jordan decomposition is obtained from examining the action of the group
Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
on
V=\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2n}
. For
g\in Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, the action of
g
on this space induces a structure of
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
-module. As explained in
[
31
]
, one can decompose this finitely generated module
V
as a direct sum

\bigoplus_{f\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{r}},i}\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]/(f)^{m_{i}}
. Moreover, the action of
g
restricted to each space
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]/(f)^{m_{i}}
is given by a symplectic matrix (
[
31
, §4]
).

For a given
f\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{r}}
, we define its
f
-primary component

V_{f}\cong\bigoplus_{i\geq 1}(\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]/(f^{m_{i}})),

where here the direct sum is finite. Let
g\in Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be a matrix such that one of the primary components

V_{x\pm 1}\cong\bigoplus(\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]/(x\pm 1)^{i})^{m_{i}}

is non-trivial. Wall
[
33
, p. 36]
proved that for odd
i
,
2|m_{i}
. Moreover, for even
i
, he attached a certain bilinear form (one of two forms,
\Psi_{-}
and
\Psi_{+}
) to the space
(\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]/(x\pm 1)^{i})^{m_{i}}
. The sign of the form is determined by the induced action of
g
on
(\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]/(x\pm 1)^{i})^{m_{i}}
. He then proved the following classification of conjugacy classes in
Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
.

Theorem 4.4

(
[
33
]
, p. 36, rephrased)
.

Two matrices
g,h\in Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
are conjugates if and only if

1.

Their conjugacy class data is the same and in addition,

2.

For every even
i
, the sign of the form corresponding with
(\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]/(x\pm 1)^{i})^{m_{i}}
is the same between
g,h
.

This motivates the following

Definition 4.5
.

For a matrix
g\in Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, we define the symplectic conjugacy class data by the multiset

((f^{i},m_{i}))_{f\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{r}}\atop{f\neq x\pm 1\atop{i=1,\ldots}}}\cup((f^{i},{m_{i}}))_{2\nmid i\atop{f=x\pm 1\atop{i=1,\ldots}}}\cup((f^{i},{m_{i}},\mu(i)))_{2|i\atop{f=x\pm 1\atop{i=1,\ldots}}},

where
\mu(i)=\pm
is the sign of the invariant attached to the space
(\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]/(x\pm 1)^{i})^{m_{i}}
.

If it is clear from the context that we consider the conjugacy class of
g
in
Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, we will sometimes call the symplectic conjugacy class data of
g
just the conjugacy class data of
g
. The previous theorem of Wall states that the symplectic conjugacy class data parametrizes the conjugacy classes of
Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. We also define a symplectic signed partition, which provides a purely combinatorial parametrization of
Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-conjugacy classes.

Definition 4.6
.

A symplectic signed partition
\lambda
is a collection of partitions
(\lambda_{f})_{f\in\mathcal{P}}
(some of them are tagged partitions), at most finitely many of them are non-empty, such that

1.

For
\alpha=\pm 1
,

(a)

Each odd part of
\lambda_{x-\alpha}
has even multiplicity.

(b)

Let
i>0
be even and assume that there is at least one part in
\lambda_{x-\alpha}
with size
i
. To the collection of all parts of size
i
we attach a sign
\mu(i)=\pm
(corresponding to
\mu(i)
in definition
4.5
).

2.

For
f\in\mathcal{P}
with
f\neq x\pm 1
,
\lambda_{f}=\lambda_{f^{\mathrm{r}}}
.

Let
\lambda
be a symplectic signed partition. We will denote
|\lambda|=\sum_{f\in\mathcal{P}}|\lambda_{f}|
. The set of symplectic signed partitions with
|\lambda|=2n
gives a combinatorial parametrization of
Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-conjugacy classes, since a polynomial
f\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{r}}
is either in
\mathcal{P}
or factors as
f=hh^{\mathrm{r}}
where
h,h^{\mathrm{r}}\in\mathcal{P}
.

To complement this view, we would like to understand how to join matrices with different primary components (meanwhile preserving the induced action). This will be done using the
triangular join
. Given two matrices
A\in Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,
B\in Sp_{2m}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, where
A
is a lower triangular block matrix

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A_{1}&0\\
A_{3}&A_{2}\end{array}\right),

one may define their
triangular join
, a matrix in
Sp_{2n+2m}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
whose conjugacy class datum corresponds with the join of the conjugacy class data for
A
and
B
together, and such that the induced action is preserved (see
[
31
, §5]
).

Definition 4.7
.

Let

A=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A_{1}&0\\
A_{3}&A_{2}\end{array}\right)\in Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q}),B\in Sp_{2m}(\mathbb{F}_{q}).

The triangular join of
A,B
, also denoted by
A\triangle B
, is a matrix in
Sp_{2n+2m}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
given by

\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}A_{1}&0&0\\
0&B&0\\
A_{3}&0&A_{2}\end{array}\right).

As a special case, we mention the case where
A,B
are both block diagonal,

A=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A_{1}&0\\
0&A_{2}\end{array}\right),B=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}B_{1}&0\\
0&B_{2}\end{array}\right),

in which case the triangular join is called the
diagonal join
and is given by

A\triangle B=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}A_{1}&0&0&0\\
0&B_{1}&0&0\\
0&0&B_{2}&0\\
0&0&0&A_{2}\end{array}\right).

Later, we will be able to construct explicit representatives to conjugacy classes of
Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
by patching together matrices with different primary components, using the triangular join.

4.1.3
The special orthogonal group

We remind the reader again that for the current paper,
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is a finite field of odd characteristic
p
. Let
K\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be a symmetric matrix. The orthogonal group preserving the quadratic form with matrix
K
is

O_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q},K)=\left\{g\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q}):g^{t}Kg=K\right\}.

When the forms defined by the matrices
K_{1},K_{2}
are congruent, the corresponding orthogonal groups are conjugate in
GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Let
\chi_{2}
be the quadratic character of
\mathbb{F}_{q}^{\times}
. For each
n
, there are two congruence classes for invertible symmetric matrices of size
n
: one for symmetric matrices with
\chi_{2}(\det(K)(-1)^{[n/2]})=1
and one for those with
\chi_{2}(\det(K)(-1)^{[n/2]})=-1
.

For concreteness, following
[
30
]
, we may pick the following representatives for the congruence classes. From now on, these representatives will be called the standard symmetric forms (or just standard forms when the context is clear).

n=2m+1
is odd.

Let

\Lambda_{m}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}0&0&\cdots&0&1\\
0&0&\cdots&1&0\\
&&\ddots&&\\
0&1&\cdots&0&0\\
1&0&\cdots&0&0\\
\end{array}\right),

and pick
\delta\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
which is not a square. Then the matrices are

K_{1}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&\Lambda_{m}\\
0&1&0\\
\Lambda_{m}&0&0\\
\end{array}\right),K_{\delta}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&\Lambda_{m}\\
0&\delta&0\\
\Lambda_{m}&0&0\\
\end{array}\right).

We denote the corresponding orthogonal groups by
O_{n}^{+}(\mathbb{F}_{q}),O_{n}^{-}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
(respectively). We remark (for later use) that the two groups become conjugate in
GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}})
.

n=2m
is even.

Then the matrices are

K_{0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\Lambda_{m}\\
\Lambda_{m}&0\end{array}\right),K_{1,-\delta}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&\Lambda_{m-1}\\
0&J_{2}&0\\
\Lambda_{m-1}&0&0\\
\end{array}\right),

where here
J_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&-\delta\\
\end{array}\right)
. Again, We denote the corresponding orthogonal groups by
O_{n}^{+}(\mathbb{F}_{q}),O_{n}^{-}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
(respectively), and note that they become conjugate in the quadratic extension
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}/\mathbb{F}_{q}
.

Fix
\epsilon=\pm 1
. We define the special orthogonal group

SO_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=\{g\in O_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q}):\det(g)=1\}.

Let
K
be the form preserved by
O_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,
SO_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. The associated Lie algebra of
O_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is

\mathfrak{o}_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=\mathfrak{o}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q},K)=\left\{A\in M_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q}):KA=-A^{t}K\right\},

(4.1)

and that of
SO_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is

\mathfrak{so}_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=\mathfrak{so}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q},K)=\left\{A\in M_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q}):\mathrm{tr}(A)=0,KA=-A^{t}K\right\}.

Note that in characteristic
\neq 2
,
\mathfrak{so}^{\epsilon}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=\mathfrak{o}^{\epsilon}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, since for
A\in\mathfrak{o}_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
we have
\mathrm{tr}(A)=\mathrm{tr}(-K^{-1}A^{t}K)=-\mathrm{tr}(A)
.

We pay special attention to the two forms
K_{0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\Lambda_{m}\\
\Lambda_{m}&0\end{array}\right)=\Lambda_{2m},K_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&0&\Lambda_{m}\\
0&1&0\\
\Lambda_{m}&0&0\\
\end{array}\right)=\Lambda_{2m+1}
. We define the anti-diagonal transpose of a matrix
A
by reflecting it along the main anti-diagonal (analogously to the regular transpose). Denote this anti-diagonal transpose of
A
by
A^{\mathfrak{t}}
. One can see that the condition
\Lambda_{n}A=-A^{t}\Lambda_{n}
simply means that
A
is anti-symmetric w.r.t.
A^{\mathfrak{t}}
, that is,
A^{\mathfrak{t}}=-A
. Thus the Lie algebra in those cases has a nice interpretation.

As in the symplectic case, the characteristic polynomial of a matrix
M\in SO_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
must be self-reciprocal. Moreover, also in this case one can derive the Jordan-type decomposition by observing the action of
M
on
V=\mathbb{F}_{q}^{n}
, which gives it a structure of an
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
-module. As explained in
[
30
]
, one can decompose the space to a direct sum
\bigoplus_{f\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{r}},i}(\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]/(f)^{n_{i}})
, such that
n=\sum_{f\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{r}},i}n_{i}\deg(f)
. For a given
f\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{r}}
, we define the
f
-primary component

V_{f}\cong\bigoplus_{i\geq 1}(\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]/f^{i})^{m_{i}}.

Let
g\in O_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Consider now the primary components of
g
associated with the polynomials
x\pm 1
,

V_{x\pm 1}\cong\bigoplus(\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]/(x\pm 1)^{i})^{m_{i}}.

Wall
[
33
, p. 38]
proved that for even
i
the multiplicity
m_{i}
must be even. For odd
i
, similarly to the symplectic case, he attached a certain invariant form (one of two forms
\Psi_{-},\Psi_{+}
) to the space
(\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]/(x\pm 1)^{i})^{m_{i}}
. As in the symplectic case, the sign of the form is determined by the induced action of
g
on
(\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]/(x\pm 1)^{i})^{m_{i}}
.

Theorem 4.8

(
[
33
]
, p. 38-9, rephrased)
.

Fix
\epsilon=\pm
. Two matrices
g,h\in O_{n}^{\epsilon}
are conjugates if and only if

1.

Their conjugacy class data is the same and in addition,

2.

For every odd
i
, the sign of the form corresponding with
(\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]/(x\pm 1)^{i})^{m_{i}}
is the same between
g,h
.

Definition 4.9
.

Fix
\epsilon=\pm
. For a matrix
g\in O_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, we define the orthogonal conjugacy class data by the multiset

((f^{i},m_{i}))_{f\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{r}}\atop{f\neq x\pm 1\atop{i=1,\ldots}}}\cup((f^{i},{m_{i}}))_{2|i\atop{f=x\pm 1\atop{i=1,\ldots}}}\cup((f^{i},{m_{i}},\mu(i)))_{2\nmid i\atop{f=x\pm 1\atop{i=1,\ldots}}},

where
\mu(i)=\pm
is the sign of the invariant attached to the space
(\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]/(x\pm 1)^{i})^{m_{i}}
.

The last theorem by Wall shows that the orthogonal conjugacy class data parametrize conjugacy classes in
O_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. When it is clear from the context that we consider the conjugacy class of
g
in
O_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, we will sometimes call the orthogonal conjugacy class data just the conjugacy class data of
g
. We also define an orthogonal signed partition, which provides a purely combinatorial parametrization of
O^{\epsilon}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-conjugacy classes.

Definition 4.10
.

An orthogonal signed partition is a collection of partitions
(\lambda_{f})_{f\in\mathcal{P}}
(some of them are tagged partitions), at most finitely many of them are non-empty, such that

1.

For
\alpha=\pm 1
,

(a)

Each even part of
\lambda_{x-\alpha}
has even multiplicity.

(b)

Let
i>0
be odd and assume that there is at least one part in
\lambda_{x-\alpha}
with size
i
. To the collection of all parts of size
i
we attach a sign
\mu(i)=\pm
(corresponding to
\mu(i)
in definition
4.9
).

2.

For
f\in\mathcal{P}
with
f\neq x\pm 1
,
\lambda_{f}=\lambda_{f^{\mathrm{r}}}
.

Let
\lambda
be an orthogonal signed partition. We will denote
|\lambda|=\sum_{f\in\mathcal{P}}|\lambda_{f}|
. The set of orthogonal signed partitions with
|\lambda|=n
gives a combinatorial parametrization of
O^{\epsilon}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-conjugacy classes, since a polynomial
f\in\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{r}}
is either in
\mathcal{P}
or factors as
f=hh^{\mathrm{r}}
for
h,h^{\mathrm{r}}\in\mathcal{P}
.

To complement these results, we would like to have (similarly to the symplectic case) a way to join matrices with different primary components, such that the resulting matrix will be in the correct orthogonal group. The problem is this: if we have matrices
A,B
preserving the forms
J_{A},J_{B}
, the diagonal join will preserve the matrix
\mathrm{diag}(J_{A},J_{B})
, which is not of the standard types we defined before. Thus we need first to understand how to compute the type of
\mathrm{diag}(J_{A},J_{B})
, and later to work out a linear transformation to the corresponding standard form.

This will be done using the orthogonal join, which in the case of the orthogonal group is more complicated, and must treat the Witt types of the associated quadratic forms. We will now briefly discuss the Witt ring
W(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. See
[
19
, §2]
for a detailed exposition of Witt rings in this setting and in general.

Definition 4.11
.

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional vector space over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, and let
Q:V\times V\to\mathbb{F}_{q}
be a quadratic form. The pair
(V,Q)
is called a quadratic space.

We sometimes abuse notation slightly and call the quadratic space
(V,Q)
simply
V
. As an example for a quadratic space, we define
\mathbb{H}=(\mathbb{F}_{q}^{2},(x,y)\mapsto x^{2}-y^{2})
. It is not hard to see that this is a quadratic space.
\mathbb{H}
is called the hyperbolic space.

For
(V_{1},Q_{1}),(V_{2},Q_{2})
one may define their direct sum
(V_{1}\oplus V_{2},Q_{1}\oplus Q_{2})
, where
Q_{1}\oplus Q_{2}
is the quadratic form defined via the equation

(Q_{1}\oplus Q_{2})(v_{1}\oplus v_{2},v_{1}^{\prime}\oplus v_{2}^{\prime})=Q_{1}(v_{1},v_{1}^{\prime})+Q_{2}(v_{2},v_{2}^{\prime}).

We denote by
V_{1}^{m}
the direct sum
\underbrace{V_{1}\oplus V_{1}\cdots\oplus V_{1}}_{m\text{ times}}
.

Definition 4.12
.

Let
(V_{1},Q_{1}),(V_{2},Q_{2})
be two quadratic spaces over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. A linear map
L:V_{1}\to V_{2}
is called an isomorphism of quadratic spaces if it is an isomorphism of vector spaces such that
Q_{1}=Q_{2}\circ L
. If such a map exists, we say that the quadratic spaces
V_{1},V_{2}
are isomorphic, and write
V_{1}\cong V_{2}
.

Definition 4.13
.

Let
(V_{1},Q_{1}),(V_{2},Q_{2})
be two quadratic spaces.
V_{1},V_{2}
are called Witt equivalent if
V_{1}\cong V_{2}\oplus\mathbb{H}^{m}
for some non-negative integer
m
. We denote
V_{1}\sim V_{2}
.

One can show that if
V_{1}\sim V_{1}^{\prime}
,
V_{2}\sim V_{2}^{\prime}
, then
V_{1}\oplus V_{2}\sim V_{1}^{\prime}\oplus V_{2}^{\prime}
, and
V_{1}\otimes V_{2}\sim V_{1}^{\prime}\otimes V_{2}^{\prime}
(for an appropriate definition of the tensor product of quadratic spaces see
[
19
, Chapter 1, §6]
). This induces a ring structure on the set of quadratic spaces over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
modulo Witt equivalence. The resulting ring
W(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is called the Witt ring. For a quadratic form
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\mapsto a_{1}x_{1}^{2}+\cdots+a_{n}x^{n}
, we denote its equivalence class in the Witt ring by
\langle a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\rangle
.

Theorem 4.14
.

Let
\delta\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
be a non-square. The Witt ring is composed of the elements
\langle 0\rangle,\langle 1\rangle,\langle\delta\rangle,\langle 1-\delta\rangle
(corresponding with the equivalence classes of the hyperbolic form,
x^{2}
,
\delta x^{2}
and
x^{2}-\delta y^{2}
, respectively). Moreover,

1.

If
q\equiv 1\pmod{4}
,
W(F)\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}[\epsilon|\epsilon^{2}=0]
.

2.

If
q\equiv 3\pmod{4}
,
W(F)\cong\mathbb{Z}/4\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

See
[
19
, Corollary 3.6]
for a proof.
∎

Let
M
be a symmetric matrix over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
.
M
defines a quadratic form, which by the previous theorem must be equivalent to one of the elements
\langle 0\rangle,\langle 1\rangle,\langle\delta\rangle,\langle 1-\delta\rangle
. The Witt type of
M
will be its Witt equivalence class.

Now that we considered the necessary background regarding the Witt ring, we turn back to our problem of forming the orthogonal join. Let
A\in O_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{A})
,
B\in O_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{B})
. The block matrix

A\oplus B:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A&0\\
0&B\end{array}\right)

is in
O_{n+m}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{A}\oplus J_{B})
, where

J_{A}\oplus J_{B}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}J_{A}&0\\
0&J_{B}\end{array}\right).

Even when
J_{A},J_{B}
are in one of the standard forms defined above,
J_{A}\oplus J_{B}
need not be. However, it must be congruent to one of the standard forms
K_{0},K_{1},K_{\delta},K_{1,-\delta}
(of Witt types
\langle 0\rangle,\langle 1\rangle,\langle\delta\rangle,\langle 1-\delta\rangle
, respectively). We remark that as a consequence when
n
is odd, the only possible Witt types for a symmetric
M\in M_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
are
\langle 1\rangle,\langle\delta\rangle
. Similarly, for
n
even, the only possible Witt types are
\langle 0\rangle,\langle 1-\delta\rangle

To understand which one of the forms is appropriate, we must compute the Witt type of
J_{A}\oplus J_{B}
. This corresponds to forming the addition
\langle J_{A}\rangle+\langle J_{B}\rangle
in the Witt ring. Suppose that the standard form
K
satisfies
\langle K\rangle=\langle J_{A}\rangle+\langle J_{B}\rangle
. By the definition of the Witt type, there is always a matrix
X
such that

X^{t}(J_{A}\oplus J_{B})X=K.

(4.2)

When
J_{A},J_{B}
are standard forms, we denote
K
by
J_{A\oplus_{\mathrm{orth}}B}
. We are now ready to define the orthogonal sum
A\oplus_{\mathrm{orth}}B
.

Claim 4.15
.

Let
A\in O_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{A})
,
B\in O_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{B})
. Let
K,X
be as above. Then, the matrix
A\oplus_{\mathrm{orth}}B:=X^{-1}(A\oplus B)X
preserves the form
K
.
A\oplus_{\mathrm{orth}}B
is called the orthogonal direct sum of
A
and
B
.

Proof.

Indeed,

(A\oplus_{\mathrm{orth}}B)^{t}K(A\oplus_{\mathrm{orth}}B)=X^{t}(A^{t}\oplus B^{t})X^{-t}\cdot X^{t}(J_{A}\oplus J_{B})X\cdot X^{-1}(A\oplus B)X=K.

∎

In particular, when
J_{A},J_{B}
are standard forms,
J_{A\oplus_{\mathrm{orth}}B}
is also standard and
A\oplus_{\mathrm{orth}}B\in O_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{A\oplus_{\mathrm{orth}}B})
. We now consider what happens to the associated Lie algebras.

Claim 4.16
.

Let
A\in O_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{A})
,
B\in O_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{B})
. Let
K,X
be as above. Then,

\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{F}_{q},K)=X^{-1}\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{A}\oplus J_{B})X.

Proof.

Indeed, let
Y\in\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{A}\oplus J_{B})
. Using equation (
4.2
) and the definition of the Lie algebra,

K(X^{-1}YX)=X^{t}(J_{A}\oplus J_{B})YX=-X^{t}Y^{t}(J_{A}\oplus J_{B})X=-X^{t}Y^{t}X^{-t}X^{t}(J_{A}\oplus J_{B})X=(X^{-1}YX)^{t}K.

Hence we have the inclusion

\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{F}_{q},K)\subset X^{-1}\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{A}\oplus J_{B})X,

Thus the LHS is contained in the RHS, and by symmetry we get equality.
∎

When
J_{A},J_{B}
are standard that means that
\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{A\oplus_{\mathrm{orth}}B})=X^{-1}\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{A}\oplus J_{B})X
.

4.1.4
The unitary group

Let
\tau:\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}
be the
q
-Frobenius map, i.e.
\tau(x)=x^{q}
. We extend the action of Frobenius to matrices in
M_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}})
by setting
\tau(M)
to be the matrix obtained from
M
by raising each entry to the
q
-th power. Now define
M^{*}=\tau(M^{t})
. The unitary group over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is defined by

U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=\{M\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}):MM^{*}=I\}.

The associated Lie algebra is

\mathfrak{u}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=\{M\in M_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}):M^{*}=-M\}.

Similarly to the symplectic and orthogonal case, the characteristic polynomial of a unitary matrix admits symmetries. For
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]
monic, let
\tau(f)
be the action of Frobenius on each coefficient separately. Then, define
\tilde{f}(t)=\frac{t^{n}\tau(f)(1/t)}{\tau(f)(0)}
. For a unitary matrix
M
, if
\mathrm{char}(M)=f
, then
\tilde{f}=f
. Such a polynomial is called self-skew-reciprocal. A polynomial is called
\sim
-irreducible if it cannot be decomposed as the product of two self-skew-reciprocal polynomials. Denote by
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
the set of monic
\sim
-irreducible polynomials. Finally, we note that
f
is
\tau(f(0))
-skew palindromic.

As in the
GL_{n}
case, conjugacy classes are determined by the (usual) Jordan form over
GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}})
. However, there are some restrictions on the conjugacy class data. Let
g\in U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be a unitary matrix. View
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}^{n}
as an
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]
-module by the action
f(x)v=f(g)v
. It turns out (see
[
33
, p. 34]
) that one can always write

V\cong\bigoplus_{f\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}}\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k_{f}}\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]/(f)^{m_{i}}\cong\bigoplus_{f\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}}V_{f},V_{f}\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{k_{f}}\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]/(f)^{m_{i}}.

That is, the primary parts of
g
must belong to
\sim
-irreducible polynomials. To join two unitary matrices, we simply write them on the diagonal of a block matrix. We end the section with the definitions of a unitary conjugacy class data and a unitary partition.

Definition 4.17
.

For a matrix
g\in U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, we define the unitary conjugacy class data by the multiset

((f^{i},m_{i}))_{f\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}\atop{i=1,\ldots}}.

Definition 4.18
.

A unitary partition
\lambda
is a collection of partitions
(\lambda_{f})_{f\in\mathcal{P}}
, at most finitely many of them are non-empty, such that
\lambda_{f}=\lambda_{\tilde{f}}
.

Let
\lambda
be a unitary partition. We will denote
|\lambda|=\sum_{f\in\mathcal{P}}|\lambda_{f}|
. The set of unitary partitions with
|\lambda|=n
gives a combinatorial parametrization of
U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-conjugacy classes, since a polynomial
f\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
is either in
\mathcal{P}
or it factors as
f=h\tilde{h}
where
h,\tilde{h}\in\mathcal{P}
.

4.2
The effect of lifting on traces and the characteristic polynomial

For the following subsection, let
G\subset GL_{n}
be a finite matrix group from the following list:
G=GL_{n}
,
SL_{n}
,
SO_{n}
,
Sp_{2n}
,
U_{n}
. The mod
\pi^{k}
reduction map
G(\mathcal{O})\to G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
is surjective for all
k
by Lemma
2.7
, so we can fix once and for all for every
k
a system
H_{G,k}\subset G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
of representatives for
G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k-1}\mathcal{O})
. Also, let
\mathfrak{g}
be the associated Lie algebra. For the rest of section
4.2
, we let
k\geq 2
unless explicitly stated otherwise.

Lemma 4.19
.

Any
A\in G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
can be written uniquely as the product

A=A_{0}(1+\pi^{k-1}A_{1})

where
A_{0}\in H_{G,k}
and
A_{1}\in\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
.

Proof.

We prove the lemma for every matrix group separately.

G=GL_{n},SL_{n}
.

Let
M\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
, and write
M=M_{0}+\pi^{k-1}M_{1}
, where
M_{0}\in H_{G,k}
. Denote the
i
’th row of a matrix
X
by
X_{i}
. The determinant is multilinear in the rows, and we write this explicitly by

\det(M_{0}+\pi^{k-1}M_{1})=\sum_{(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})\in\{1,2\}^{n}}\det((M_{s_{1}})_{1},\ldots,(M_{s_{n}})_{n})=\\
=\det(M_{0})+\pi^{k-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\det((M_{0})_{1},\ldots,(M_{1})_{i},\ldots,(M_{0})_{n})=\det(M_{0})+\pi^{k-1}\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(M_{0})M_{1}).

No matter what
M_{1}
is, the determinant is thus invertible. Hence, we get that
M=M_{0}+\pi^{k-1}M_{1}
where
M_{1}
is any matrix
\pmod{\pi}
, and since
M_{0}
is invertible, we get the original claim. For
SL_{n}
, we have the additional constraint
\det(M)=1
. Since
M_{0}\in G
, we conclude that we must have
\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(M_{0})M_{1})\equiv 0\pmod{\pi}
. If we write
M_{1}=M_{0}M_{1}^{\prime}
, we get that

\det(M_{0})\mathrm{tr}(M_{1}^{\prime})=\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(M_{0})M_{0}M_{1}^{\prime})\equiv 0\pmod{\pi}\iff\mathrm{tr}(M_{1}^{\prime})\equiv 0\pmod{\pi},

and thus
M_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, and the claim follows.

G=Sp_{2n}
.

Let
M\in Sp_{2n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
, writing
M=M_{0}+\pi^{k-1}M_{1}
, where
M_{0}\in H_{G,k}
. Let
\Omega_{n}
be the matrix representing the standard symplectic form. Then for
M
to be symplectic, we need to have

\Omega_{n}=M\Omega_{n}M^{t}=(M_{0}+\pi^{k-1}M_{1})\Omega_{n}(M_{0}^{t}+\pi^{k-1}M_{1}^{t})=M_{0}\Omega_{n}M_{0}^{t}+\pi^{k-1}(M_{0}\Omega_{n}M_{1}^{t}+M_{1}\Omega_{n}M_{0}^{t}).

Since we assumed
M_{0}
is symplectic, we are left with the condition
M_{0}\Omega_{n}M_{1}^{t}+M_{1}\Omega_{n}M_{0}^{t}=0\pmod{\pi}
. Write
M_{1}=M_{0}M_{2}
. Then we have

M_{0}\Omega_{n}M_{2}^{t}M_{0}^{t}+M_{0}M_{2}\Omega_{n}M_{0}^{t}=0\iff\Omega_{n}M_{2}^{t}+M_{2}\Omega_{n}=0,

which means that
M_{2}
is in
\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
.

G=SO_{n}
.

Let
M\in O_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
, and write as usual
M=M_{0}+\pi^{k-1}M_{1}
with
M_{0}\in H_{G,k}
. Let
K
be the matrix representing the symmetric form preserved by the orthogonal group. Then

K=M^{t}KM=(M_{0}^{t}+\pi^{k-1}M_{1}^{t})K(M_{0}+\pi^{k-1}M_{1})=M_{0}^{t}KM_{0}+\pi^{k-1}(M_{0}^{t}KM_{1}+M_{1}^{t}KM_{0}).

This implies that
M_{0}^{t}KM_{1}+M_{1}^{t}KM_{0}=0
, and the proof ends as in the symplectic case. The proof for the special orthogonal group goes the same, noting that
\mathfrak{so}=\mathfrak{go}
. We remark that the reduction of
O_{n},SO_{n}
modulo
\pi^{k}
might have different signs when we change
n
, and hence we discussed both options.

G=U_{n}
.

Let
M\in U_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
. Write as usual
M=M_{0}+\pi^{k-1}M_{1}
with
M_{0}\in H_{G,k}
. Then,

I=M^{*}M=(M_{0}^{*}+\pi^{k-1}M_{1}^{*})(M_{0}+\pi^{k-1}M_{1})=M_{0}^{*}M_{0}+\pi^{k-1}(M_{0}^{*}M_{1}+M_{1}^{*}M_{0}).

This implies
M_{1}^{*}M_{0}=-M_{0}^{*}M_{1}
. Writing
M_{1}=M_{0}M_{1}^{\prime}
, we get that
M_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
.

∎

Using this decomposition, one can study the distribution of traces of powers and of the next-to-leading coefficients characteristic polynomial.

Lemma 4.20
.

Let
A\in G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
be written as
A=A_{0}(1+\pi^{k-1}A_{1})
, where
A_{0}\in H_{G,k}
and
A_{1}\in\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Then,

\mathrm{tr}(A^{r})=\mathrm{tr}(A_{0}^{r})+r\pi^{k-1}\mathrm{tr}(A_{0}^{r}A_{1}).

Proof.

Write

A^{r}=A_{0}^{r}+\pi^{k-1}\sum_{i=1}^{r}A_{0}^{i}A_{1}A_{0}^{r-i}.

The claim now follows since the trace map is cyclic.
∎

In particular, if one understands the distribution of traces of powers for
G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k-1}\mathcal{O})
, the distribution of traces of powers for
G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
reduces to the value distribution of the linear functional

\partial\mathrm{tr}_{A_{0}}:\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\to\mathbb{F}_{q},A_{1}\mapsto r\mathrm{tr}(\overline{A_{0}}^{r}A_{1}).

(4.3)

One can get similar results for the characteristic polynomial.

Lemma 4.21
.

Let
G
be one of the groups considered above.
Let
A\in G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
be written as
A=A_{0}(1+\pi^{k-1}A_{1})
, where
A_{0}\in H_{G,k}
and
A_{1}\in\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Then,

\mathrm{char}(A)=\mathrm{char}(A_{0})-\pi^{k-1}\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{0})A_{0}A_{1}).

Further, if
G\neq GL_{n},U_{n}
,

\mathrm{char}(A)=\mathrm{char}(A_{0})-\pi^{k-1}x\cdot\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{0})A_{1}).

Proof.

Write
\mathrm{char}(A)=\det(xI-(A_{0}+\pi^{k-1}A_{0}A_{1}))=\det((xI-A_{0})-\pi^{k-1}A_{0}A_{1})
. Denote the
i
’th row of a matrix
X
by
X_{i}
, and denote
M_{1}=xI-A_{0},M_{2}=-\pi^{k-1}A_{0}A_{1}
. The determinant is multilinear in the rows, and we write this explicitly by

\det((xI-A_{0})-\pi^{k-1}A_{1})=\det((M_{1})_{1}+(M_{2})_{1},\ldots,(M_{1})_{n}+(M_{2})_{n})=\sum_{(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})\in\{1,2\}^{n}}\det((M_{s_{1}})_{1},\ldots,(M_{s_{n}})_{n}).

Now note that since
M_{2}=-\pi^{k-1}A_{0}A_{1}
, and
\pi^{2k-2}=0
in
\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}
, we have

\det((xI-A_{0})+\pi^{k-1}A_{0}A_{1})=\det(xI-A_{0})-\pi^{k-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\det((xI-A_{0})_{1},\ldots,(A_{0}A_{1})_{i},\ldots,(xI-A_{0})_{n})

Developing the determinant in the summand w.r.t. the
i
’th row, we get that

\mathrm{char}(A)=\mathrm{char}(A_{0})-\pi^{k-1}\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{0})A_{0}A_{1}).

This finishes the first part of the claim. For
G\neq GL_{n},U_{n}
, we note that the trace map vanishes on the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Thus,

\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{0})A_{0}A_{1})=\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{0})(A_{0}-x)A_{1})+\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{0})xA_{1})=\\
=-\mathrm{char}(A_{0})\mathrm{tr}(A_{1})+x\cdot\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{0})A_{1})=x\cdot\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{0})A_{1}).

∎

Thus, given results on the distribution of the next-to-leading coefficients of the characteristic polynomial for
G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k-1}\mathcal{O})
, one can lift them to
G(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
by studying linear maps on the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. To do this, we define the
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-linear maps

\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}:\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\to\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<n},A_{1}\mapsto\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-\overline{A_{0}})A_{0}A_{1}).

(4.4)

We remark that the image of
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}
is indeed inside
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<n}
due to the fact that
\mathrm{char}(A),\mathrm{char}(A_{0})
are monic of degree
n
. We also remark that for
G\neq GL_{n},U_{n}
we have

\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}(A_{1})=x\cdot\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-\overline{A_{0}})A_{1}).

4.3
Explicit representatives for primary conjugacy classes

In sections
6
-
10
we will compute the image of the linear maps

\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}
defined in (
4.4
) for the finite matrix groups
G=GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q}),SL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q}),Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q}),SO_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
and
U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. For any of the groups
G
considered, and for all
a\in G
, the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
is preserved by conjugation by
a
:
a\mathfrak{g}a^{-1}=\mathfrak{g}
. Now note that for any
a\in G
,
A_{0}\in G(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,
A_{1}\in\mathfrak{g}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,

\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{0})\cdot aA_{1}a^{-1})=\mathrm{tr}(a^{-1}\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{0})aA_{1})=\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-a^{-1}A_{0}a)A_{1}).

We conclude that
\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}})
depends only on the conjugacy class of
\overline{A_{0}}
in
G(\mathbb{F}_{q})
(at least in the cases of
G\neq GL_{n},U_{n}
. For
G=GL_{n},U_{n}
we will use slightly modified ideas described in the corresponding sections). Hence it is useful to compute the matrix
\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{0})
explicitly (at least up to conjugation).

Therefore, we would like to construct explicit representatives for the various conjugacy classes in each group. We will study the image
\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}})
over an algebraic extension
\mathbb{F}_{q^{l}}
in which
\mathrm{char}(A_{0})
splits completely, and use this data to understand the image over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Thus we will be interested in conjugacy classes of
G(\mathbb{F}_{q^{l}})
for matrices whose characteristic polynomial splits completely into linear factors. We will also compute the matrix
\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{0})
for the explicit representatives
A_{0}
. In fact, we may focus on cases where
\mathrm{char}(M)=\min(M)=(x-\alpha)^{e}
for some
\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{l}}
,
e>0
. We will later use a “join” operator to join different primary parts, creating more complex conjugacy classes.

4.3.1
For
GL_{n}
and
SL_{n}

As is well known any matrix
M\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
whose characteristic polynomial splits completely admits a Jordan normal form. The Jordan form characterizes such conjugacy classes. As mentioned above, it is enough to treat the case of
\mathrm{char}(M)=\min(M)=(x-\alpha)^{e}
, in which
M
is a conjugate of a Jordan block
J_{e}(\alpha)
, where here (and for the rest of the paper)

J_{e}(\alpha):=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\alpha&1&0&\cdots&0\\
0&\alpha&1&\ddots&0\\
&&\ddots&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&&\alpha&1\\
&&&0&\alpha\\
\end{array}\right),\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{q},e\geq 1\text{ integer}.

Lemma 4.22
.

Let
J_{e}(\alpha)
be a Jordan block. Then,

X_{\alpha}^{e}:=\mathrm{Adj}(x-J_{e}(\alpha))=(x-\alpha)^{e}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}(x-\alpha)^{-1}&(x-\alpha)^{-2}&(x-\alpha)^{-3}&\cdots&(x-\alpha)^{-e}\\
0&(x-\alpha)^{-1}&(x-\alpha)^{-2}&\cdots&(x-\alpha)^{-(e-1)}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\cdots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0&(x-\alpha)^{-1}&(x-\alpha)^{-2}\\
0&0&\cdots&0&(x-\alpha)^{-1}\end{array}\right).

Proof.

We have
\mathrm{Adj}(x-J_{e}(\alpha))=(x-\alpha)^{e}(x-J_{e}(\alpha))^{-1}
. Note that
x-J_{e}(\alpha)=-J_{e}(\alpha-x)
, and using the formula for the inverse of a Jordan block we finish.
∎

For
SL_{n}
, we note that
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}=\{a\in M_{n}:\mathrm{tr}(a)=0\}
. This Lie algebra admits more symmetries than conjugation by
SL_{n}
: indeed, for any
g\in GL_{n}
,
a\in\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
, we have
\mathrm{tr}(gag^{-1})=\mathrm{tr}(a)=0
. Thus,
gag^{-1}\in\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
as well. We get that to compute the image of
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}
for
A_{0}\in SL_{n}
, one can replace
A_{0}
by an element from its conjugacy class in
GL_{n}
(in particular, a matrix given in a Jordan form).

4.3.2
For
Sp_{2n}

We discussed
Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-conjugacy classes in section
4.1.2
. To compute
\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{0})
for representatives of
Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-conjugacy classes, we would like to deal with simple building blocks like the Jordan block. The analog of a single Jordan block is a matrix conjugate in
GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
to one of the following types I, II, and III. We also provide conjugacy class representatives (from
Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
) to each type, following
[
31
]
.

1.

Type I:
The class with Jordan form
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}J_{m}(\alpha)&0\\
0&J_{m}(\alpha^{-1})\end{array}\right)\text{ for }\alpha\neq\pm 1
. The representative for this class is
A_{\alpha,\alpha^{-1}}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}J_{m}(\alpha)^{-1}&0\\
0&J_{m}(\alpha)^{t}\end{array}\right).

2.

Type II:
The class with Jordan form
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}J_{m}(\alpha)&0\\
0&J_{m}(\alpha)\end{array}\right)
for
\alpha=\pm 1
,
m
odd. The representative for this class is
A_{\alpha}^{m}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}J_{m}(\alpha)^{-1}&0\\
0&J_{m}(\alpha)^{t}\end{array}\right)
.

3.

Type III:
The class with Jordan form
J_{2m}(\alpha)
, for
\alpha=\pm 1
. Let
\delta\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
be a non-square, and define

a=\begin{cases}1,&\text{ for }+\text{ type,}\\
(-1)^{m}2\delta,&\text{ for }-\text{ type.}\\
\end{cases}

Then, a representative for such matrices with
\pm
sign is

A_{\alpha,\pm}^{2m}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\alpha J_{m}(1)^{-1}&0\\
aS&\alpha J_{m}(1)^{t}\end{array}\right),

where we note that
a
depends on the sign, and we denote

S=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}1&-1&\cdots&(-1)^{m-2}&(-1)^{m-1}\\
0&0&\cdots&0&0\\
&&\ddots&&\\
0&0&\cdots&0&0\end{array}\right).

We may construct every conjugacy class from these building blocks using the triangular join as in Definition
4.7
. It now remains to compute the adjoint matrix
\mathrm{Adj}(x-M)
for
M
a representative for one of the types. Before we do this, we need the following

Proposition 4.23
.

Let
e>0
be an integer and let
\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Let
Y_{\alpha}^{e}=\mathrm{Adj}(x-J_{e}(\alpha)^{-1})
. Then,

Y_{\alpha}^{e}=(x-\alpha^{-1})^{e}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\frac{-\alpha}{1-x\alpha}&\frac{-1}{(1-x\alpha)^{2}}&\cdots&\frac{-x^{e-3}}{(1-x\alpha)^{e-1}}&\frac{-x^{e-2}}{(1-x\alpha)^{e}}\\
&\frac{-\alpha}{1-x\alpha}&&&\frac{-x^{e-3}}{(1-x\alpha)^{e-1}}\\
&&\ddots&&\vdots\\
&&&\frac{-\alpha}{1-x\alpha}&\\
&&&&\frac{-\alpha}{1-x\alpha}\end{array}\right).

Now Let
\alpha=\pm 1
. Let
S
be as defined above in the description of Type III. Define a sequence of rational functions

f_{1}=\frac{1}{x-\alpha}
,
f_{2}=\frac{1}{(x-\alpha)^{2}}+\frac{1}{(x-\alpha)}
,…,
f_{e}=\frac{x^{e-2}}{(x-\alpha)^{e}}+\frac{x^{e-3}}{(x-\alpha)^{e-1}}+\cdots+\frac{1}{(x-\alpha)^{2}}+\frac{1}{(x-\alpha)}
. Then,

Z_{\alpha}^{2e}:=(x-\alpha)^{2e}(x-\alpha J_{e}(1))^{-t}S(x-\alpha J_{e}(1)^{-1})^{-1}=\\
=(x-\alpha)^{2e}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\frac{f_{1}}{(x-\alpha)}&\frac{-f_{2}}{(x-\alpha)}&\frac{f_{3}}{(x-\alpha)}&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{e-1}f_{e}}{(x-\alpha)}\\
\frac{\alpha f_{1}}{(x-\alpha)^{2}}&\frac{-\alpha f_{2}}{(x-\alpha)^{2}}&\frac{\alpha f_{3}}{(x-\alpha)^{2}}&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{e-1}\alpha f_{e}}{(x-\alpha)^{2}}\\
&&\ddots&&\\
\frac{\alpha^{e-2}f_{1}}{(x-\alpha)^{e-1}}&\frac{-\alpha^{e-2}f_{2}}{(x-\alpha)^{e-1}}&\frac{\alpha^{e-2}f_{3}}{(x-\alpha)^{e-1}}&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{e-2}\alpha^{e-2}f_{e}}{(x-\alpha)^{e-1}}\\
\frac{\alpha^{e-1}f_{1}}{(x-\alpha)^{e}}&\frac{-\alpha^{e-1}f_{2}}{(x-\alpha)^{e}}&\frac{\alpha^{e-1}f_{3}}{(x-\alpha)^{e}}&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{e-1}\alpha^{e-1}f_{e}}{(x-\alpha)^{e}}\end{array}\right).

Proof.

We start with the claim on
Y_{\alpha}^{e}
. It is enough to compute
(x-J_{e}(\alpha)^{-1})^{-1}
. Write
J_{e}(\alpha)=\alpha+J_{e}(0)
, where
J_{e}(0)^{e}=0
is nilpotent. We have:

(x-J_{e}(\alpha)^{-1})^{-1}=\frac{1}{x-(\alpha+J_{e}(0))^{-1}}=\frac{-(\alpha+J_{e}(0))}{1-(\alpha+J_{e}(0))x}=\frac{-(\alpha+J_{e}(0))}{(1-x\alpha)-xJ_{e}(0)}=\frac{\frac{-(\alpha+J_{e}(0))}{1-x\alpha}}{1-\frac{x}{1-x\alpha}J_{e}(0)}.

Since
J_{e}(0)
is nilpotent, we get

(x-J_{e}(\alpha)^{-1})^{-1}=-\frac{\alpha+J_{e}(0)}{1-x\alpha}\left(1+\frac{x}{1-x\alpha}J_{e}(0)+\cdots+\frac{x^{e-1}}{(1-x\alpha)^{e-1}}J_{e}(0)^{e-1}\right).

Rewriting this we get

(x-J_{e}(\alpha)^{-1})^{-1}=\frac{-1}{1-x\alpha}\left(\alpha+\left(1+\frac{\alpha x}{1-x\alpha}\right)J_{e}(0)+\cdots+\left(\frac{x^{e-2}}{(1-x\alpha)^{e-2}}+\frac{\alpha x^{e-1}}{(1-x\alpha)^{e-1}}\right)J_{e-1}(0)\right).

For
i\geq 0
,

\frac{x^{i}}{(1-x\alpha)^{i}}+\frac{\alpha x^{i+1}}{(1-x\alpha)^{i+1}}=\frac{x^{i}}{(1-x\alpha)^{i+1}},

which finishes the proof of the first claim. Regarding the second claim, we first note that

(x-\alpha J_{e}(1))^{-1}=\frac{1}{\alpha}\left(\frac{x}{\alpha}-J_{e}(1)\right)^{-1}=\frac{1}{\alpha(x/\alpha-1)^{e}}X_{1}^{e}\left(\frac{x}{\alpha}\right)
,

(x-\alpha J_{e}(1)^{-1})^{-1}=\frac{1}{\alpha}(\frac{x}{\alpha}-J_{e}(1)^{-1})^{-1}=\frac{1}{\alpha(x/\alpha-1)^{e}}Y_{1}^{e}(\frac{x}{\alpha})
. Noting that
\alpha^{2}=1
(and hence also
\alpha^{2e}=1
), we get that

(x-\alpha)^{2e}(x-\alpha J_{e}(1))^{-t}S(x-\alpha J_{e}(1)^{-1})^{-1}=(X_{1}^{e})^{t}\left(\frac{x}{\alpha}\right)SY_{1}^{e}\left(\frac{x}{\alpha}\right).

Again using the fact that
\alpha^{2}=1
, we see that

Y_{1}^{e}\left(\frac{x}{\alpha}\right)=(x/\alpha-1)^{e}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\frac{-1}{1-x/\alpha}&\frac{-1}{(1-x/\alpha)^{2}}&\cdots&\frac{-(x/\alpha)^{e-3}}{(1-x/\alpha)^{e-1}}&\frac{-(x/\alpha)^{e-2}}{(1-x/\alpha)^{e}}\\
&\frac{-1}{1-x/\alpha}&&&\frac{-(x/\alpha)^{e-3}}{(1-x/\alpha)^{e-1}}\\
&&\ddots&&\vdots\\
&&&\frac{-1}{1-x/\alpha}&\\
&&&&\frac{-1}{1-x/\alpha}\end{array}\right)=\\
=\alpha^{e+1}(x-\alpha)^{e}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\frac{1}{x-\alpha}&\frac{-1}{(x-\alpha)^{2}}&\cdots&\frac{(-1)^{e}x^{e-3}}{(x-\alpha)^{e-1}}&\frac{(-1)^{e+1}x^{e-2}}{(x-\alpha)^{e}}\\
&\frac{1}{x-\alpha}&&&\frac{(-1)^{e}x^{e-3}}{(x-\alpha)^{e-1}}\\
&&\ddots&&\vdots\\
&&&\frac{1}{x-\alpha}&\\
&&&&\frac{1}{x-\alpha}\end{array}\right).

Hence,

\left(X_{1}^{e}\left(\frac{x}{\alpha}\right)\right)^{t}SY_{1}^{e}\left(\frac{x}{\alpha}\right)=\alpha^{e}(x-\alpha)^{e}\left(X_{1}^{e}\left(\frac{x}{\alpha}\right)\right)^{t}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}f_{1}&-f_{2}&f_{3}&\cdots&(-1)^{e-1}f_{e}\\
0&0&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\cdots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0&0&0\\
0&0&\cdots&0&0\end{array}\right).

Now, compute

X_{1}^{e}\left(\frac{x}{\alpha}\right)=(x/\alpha-1)^{e}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}(x/\alpha-1)^{-1}&(x/\alpha-1)^{-2}&(x/\alpha-1)^{-3}&\cdots&(x/\alpha-1)^{-e}\\
0&(x/\alpha-1)^{-1}&(x/\alpha-1)^{-2}&\cdots&(x/\alpha-1)^{-(e-1)}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\cdots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0&(x/\alpha-1)^{-1}&(x/\alpha-1)^{-2}\\
0&0&\cdots&0&(x/\alpha-1)^{-1}\end{array}\right)=\\
=\alpha^{-e}(x-\alpha)^{e}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\frac{\alpha}{x-\alpha}&\frac{\alpha^{2}}{(x-\alpha)^{2}}&\frac{\alpha^{3}}{(x-\alpha)^{3}}&\cdots&\frac{\alpha^{e}}{(x-\alpha)^{e}}\\
0&\frac{\alpha}{x-\alpha}&\frac{\alpha^{2}}{(x-\alpha)^{2}}&\cdots&\frac{\alpha^{e-1}}{(x-\alpha)^{e-1}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\cdots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0&\frac{\alpha}{x-\alpha}&\frac{\alpha^{2}}{(x-\alpha)^{2}}\\
0&0&\cdots&0&\frac{\alpha}{x-\alpha}\end{array}\right)=\\
=\alpha^{-e+1}(x-\alpha)^{e}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\frac{1}{x-\alpha}&\frac{\alpha}{(x-\alpha)^{2}}&\frac{\alpha^{2}}{(x-\alpha)^{3}}&\cdots&\frac{\alpha^{e-1}}{(x-\alpha)^{e}}\\
0&\frac{1}{x-\alpha}&\frac{\alpha}{(x-\alpha)^{2}}&\cdots&\frac{\alpha^{e-2}}{(x-\alpha)^{e-1}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\cdots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0&\frac{1}{x-\alpha}&\frac{\alpha}{(x-\alpha)^{2}}\\
0&0&\cdots&0&\frac{1}{x-\alpha}\end{array}\right).

Plugging this back into the last equation we get the result.
∎

Lemma 4.24
.

For
e>0
integer and
\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
, denote by
X_{\alpha}^{e}
the matrix
\mathrm{Adj}(x-J_{e}(\alpha))
as computed in Lemma
4.22
, and let
Y_{\alpha}^{e},Z_{\alpha}^{2e}
be as defined in Proposition
4.23
. Finally, let
a
be as defined in the definition of Type III matrices. Then

1.

\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{\alpha,\alpha^{-1}}^{m})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(x-\alpha)^{m}Y_{\alpha}^{m}&\\
&(x-\alpha^{-1})^{m}(X_{\alpha}^{m})^{t}\end{array}\right).

2.

\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{\alpha}^{2m+1})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(x-\alpha)^{2m+1}Y_{\alpha}^{2m+1}&\\
&(x-\alpha)^{2m+1}(X_{\alpha}^{2m+1})^{t}\end{array}\right).

3.

\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{\alpha,\pm}^{2m})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(x-\alpha)^{m}Y_{\alpha}^{m}&0\\
aZ_{\alpha}^{2m}&(x-\alpha)^{m}(X_{\alpha}^{m})^{t}\end{array}\right)
.

Proof.

Using Proposition
4.23
assertions 1,2 are simply an application of the formula
\mathrm{Adj}(M)=\det(M)M^{-1}
. As for the last assertion, it is enough to compute
(x-A_{\alpha,\pm}^{2m})^{-1}
. Let
S
be as in the definition of Type III matrices. Let
\tilde{S}
be the block matrix
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
S&0\end{array}\right)
. Write
x-A_{\alpha,\pm}^{2m}=R-a\tilde{S}
(
R
is defined implicitly via this equation). We note that

R^{-1}a\tilde{S}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}*&0\\
0&*\end{array}\right)\cdot\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
&0\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
&0\end{array}\right)

as a block matrix (here
*
denotes some block matrices we do not write explicitly, to avoid unnecessary complications). Further, by the structure of
R^{-1}a\tilde{S}
, we see that
(R^{-1}a\tilde{S})^{2}=0
. Hence,

(1+R^{-1}a\tilde{S})R^{-1}\cdot(R-a\tilde{S})=(1+R^{-1}a\tilde{S})(1-R^{-1}a\tilde{S})=1-R^{-1}a\tilde{S}+R^{-1}a\tilde{S}-(R^{-1}a\tilde{S})^{2}=1.

We get that
(x-A_{\alpha,\pm}^{2m})^{-1}=(1+aR^{-1}\tilde{S})R^{-1}
. Using Proposition
4.23
we finish the proof.
∎

4.3.3
For
SO_{n}^{\pm}

We once again remind the reader that for us,
\mathbb{F}_{q}
is a finite field of odd characteristic
p
. For the rest of section
4.3.3
, fix some
\epsilon=\pm
. We discussed
O^{\pm}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-conjugacy classes in section
4.1.3
. As in the previous subsection, we now focus on conjugacy classes of matrices
M\in O_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, where
\mathrm{char}(M)
splits in
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
.

The analog of a single Jordan block for
O^{\epsilon}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is a matrix that is conjugate in
GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
to one of the following types I, II, and III. We also provide conjugacy class representatives from
O^{\epsilon}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, following
[
31
]
.

1.

Type I:
The class with Jordan block
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}J_{m}(\alpha)&\\
&J_{m}(\alpha^{-1})\end{array}\right)
for
\alpha\neq\pm 1
. For Witt type
\langle 0\rangle
, a representative for this class is the matrix
A_{\alpha,\alpha^{-1}}^{m}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}J_{m}(\alpha)&\\
&J_{m}(\alpha)^{-\mathfrak{t}}\end{array}\right)
. We do not construct an explicit representative for Witt type
\langle 1,\delta\rangle
. Instead in Section
9.2
we will use the fact that in an extension field,
\langle 1,\delta\rangle
becomes congruent to
\langle 0\rangle
. This, together with Lemma
9.2
below, will enable us to reduce the proof to the Witt type
\langle 0\rangle
case.

2.

Type II:
The class with Jordan block
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}J_{m}(\alpha)&\\
&J_{m}(\alpha)\end{array}\right)
, for
\alpha=\pm 1
,
m
even. As in Type I, we only consider Witt type
\langle 0\rangle
, for which a representative is
A_{\alpha}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}J_{m}(\alpha)&\\
&J_{m}(\alpha)^{-\mathfrak{t}}\par\end{array}\right)
.

3.

Type III:
The class with Jordan block

J_{2m+1}(\alpha)
, for
\alpha=\pm 1
. Similarly to Type I and Type II, we may treat only the case of Witt type
\langle 1\rangle
(In Section
9.2
, we will reduce the case of Witt type
\langle\delta\rangle
to the case of Witt type
\langle 1\rangle
using Lemma
9.2
below). Define
S
to be the
m\times m
matrix with all entries
0
, apart from the last row, whose entries alternate between
-1
and
1
(starting from
1
). Also define
u=(0,\ldots,0,1)^{t}
,
v=(-1,1,-1,\cdots,(-1)^{m})
. Then the block matrix

A_{\alpha}:=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}J_{m}(\alpha)&u&\frac{(-1)^{m}}{2}\alpha S\\
0&\alpha&v\\
0&0&J_{m}(\alpha)^{-\mathfrak{t}}\end{array}\right)
is a representative of this conjugacy class. The case of Witt type
\langle 1\rangle
translates to choosing the
+
sign in the orthogonal signed partition, and the
\langle\delta\rangle
Witt type case translates to choosing the
-
sign (see Section
4.1.3
for a reminder on the significance of signs). One can check that this matrix is symplectic and that its minimal and characteristic polynomial are equal to
(x-\alpha)^{2m+1}
.

Now we want to compute
\mathrm{Adj}(x-M)
for each type, where
M
is a matrix of that type. To do that, we need the following

Proposition 4.25
.

Let
e>0
be an integer and
\alpha=\pm 1
. Using the notation from the description of the Type III case, write
S_{0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{(-1)^{e}}{2}\alpha S\\
v\end{array}\right)
. Define a sequence of rational functions
g_{1}=\frac{\alpha}{1-x\alpha}
,
g_{2}=\frac{\alpha}{1-x\alpha}-\frac{1}{(1-x\alpha)^{2}}
,…,
g_{e}=\frac{\alpha}{1-x\alpha}-\frac{1}{(1-x\alpha)^{2}}+\cdots+\frac{(-1)^{e-1}x^{e-2}}{(1-x\alpha)^{e}}
. Then,

W_{\alpha}^{2e+1}:=(x-\alpha)^{2e+1}(x-J_{e+1}(\alpha))^{-1}S_{0}(x-J_{e}(\alpha)^{-1})^{-\mathfrak{t}}=\\
=(x-\alpha)^{2e+1}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}f_{1}(x-\alpha)^{-e}\left(\frac{(-1)^{e}}{2}\alpha-\frac{1}{x-\alpha}\right)&&\cdots&&f_{e}(x-\alpha)^{-e}(-1)^{e-1}\left(\frac{(-1)^{e}}{2}\alpha-\frac{1}{x-\alpha}\right)\\
&&\ddots&&\\
\vdots&&\cdots&&\\
f_{1}(x-\alpha)^{-1}\left(\frac{(-1)^{e}}{2}\alpha-\frac{1}{x-\alpha}\right)&&&&f_{e}(x-\alpha)^{-1}(-1)^{e-1}\left(\frac{(-1)^{e}}{2}\alpha-\frac{1}{x-\alpha}\right)\\
\frac{f_{1}}{x-\alpha}&&\cdots&&\frac{(-1)^{e+1}f_{e}}{x-\alpha}\end{array}\right).

Proof.

Note that
J_{e}(\alpha)^{-\mathfrak{t}}=J_{e}(\alpha)^{-1}
. Hence,

W_{\alpha}^{2e+1}=X_{\alpha}^{e+1}S_{0}Y_{\alpha}^{e}
. We compute directly using the definition of
Y_{\alpha}^{e}
,

S_{0}Y_{\alpha}^{e}=(x-\alpha)^{e}\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}0&&\cdots&&0\\
&&\ddots&&\\
0&&\cdots&&0\\
\frac{(-1)^{e+1}}{2}\alpha g_{1}&\frac{(-1)^{e+2}}{2}\alpha g_{2}&\cdots&&\frac{(-1)^{2e}}{2}\alpha g_{e}\\
g_{1}&-g_{2}&\cdots&(-1)^{e-2}g_{e-1}&(-1)^{e-1}g_{e}\end{array}\right).

Thus, using the last equation and the definition of
X_{\alpha}^{e+1}
,

W_{\alpha}^{2e+1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}g_{1}(x)(x-\alpha)^{-e}\left(\frac{(-1)^{e+1}}{2}\alpha+\frac{1}{x-\alpha}\right)&&\cdots&&g_{e}(x)(x-\alpha)^{-e}(-1)^{e-1}\left(\frac{(-1)^{e+1}}{2}\alpha+\frac{1}{x-\alpha}\right)\\
&&\ddots&&\\
\vdots&&\cdots&&\\
g_{1}(x)(x-\alpha)^{-1}\left(\frac{(-1)^{e+1}}{2}\alpha+\frac{1}{x-\alpha}\right)&&&&g_{e}(x)(x-\alpha)^{-1}(-1)^{e-1}\left(\frac{(-1)^{e+1}}{2}\alpha+\frac{1}{x-\alpha}\right)\\
\frac{g_{1}(x)}{x-\alpha}&&\cdots&&\frac{(-1)^{e-1}g_{m}(x)}{x-\alpha}\end{array}\right).

∎

Lemma 4.26
.

Using the notation of Lemmas
4.24
and
4.22
,

1.

\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{\alpha,\alpha^{-1}}^{m})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(x-\alpha^{-1})^{m}X_{\alpha}^{m}&\\
&(x-\alpha)^{m}(Y_{\alpha}^{m})^{\mathfrak{t}}\end{array}\right).

2.

\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{\alpha}^{2m})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(x-\alpha^{-1})^{m}X_{\alpha}^{m}&\\
&(x-\alpha)^{m}(Y_{\alpha}^{m})^{\mathfrak{t}}\end{array}\right)
.

3.

\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{\alpha}^{2m+1})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(x-\alpha^{-1})^{m+1}X_{\alpha}^{m+1}&-W_{\alpha}^{2m+1}\\
0&(x-\alpha)^{m}(Y_{\alpha}^{m})^{\mathfrak{t}}\end{array}\right)
.

Proof.

Assertions 1 and 2 follow similarly to the analogous assertions in Lemma
4.24
, recalling the definition of the anti-diagonal transpose
M\mapsto M^{\mathfrak{t}}
from Section
4.1.3
.

For assertion 3, it is enough to compute
(x-A_{\alpha}^{2m+1})^{-1}
. Write

x-A_{\alpha}^{2m+1}=B+C
, where

B=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}x-J_{m+1}(\alpha)&\\
&x-J_{m}(\alpha)^{-1}\end{array}\right),C=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-S_{0}\\
0&0\end{array}\right),

and
S_{0}
is defined as in Proposition
4.25
. We note that
B^{-1}C
is of the form
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&*\\
0&0\end{array}\right)
and so
(B^{-1}C)^{2}=0
. Thus

(1-B^{-1}C)B^{-1}(B+C)=(1-B^{-1}C)(1+B^{-1}C)=1-(B^{-1}C)^{2}=1,

so that
(x-A_{\alpha})^{-1}=(1-B^{-1}C)B^{-1}
. The claim now follows using Proposition
4.25
.
∎

5
The typical degree of the minimal polynomial

For the various groups we consider, we prove below equidistribution for the traces of powers or the next-to-leading coefficients of the characteristic polynomial, whenever the minimal polynomial modulo
\pi
,
\min(\overline{A_{0}})
, is of sufficiently high degree. We shall prove in this section that this is the typical case, in a precise sense. Our methods rely on the formulae for the distribution of matrices in conjugacy classes for the finite classical matrix groups, due to Fulman
[
8
]
.

5.1
For
GL_{n},SL_{n}

We examine the probability that a matrix
M
has an exceptionally small minimal polynomial.

Claim 5.1
.

Let
M\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be chosen uniformly at random, and let
h\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
. Then, for
0\leq\delta<n
integer,

\Pr[\deg\min(M)=n-\delta\cap\mathrm{char}(M)=h]<q^{-\frac{n^{2}}{n-\delta}+o(n)}.

The rate of decay is independent of
\delta
(but may depend on
q
).

Proof.

Factor
h=\mathrm{char}(M)=\prod_{\phi\in\mathcal{P}}\phi^{e_{\phi}}
. Now let
\min(M)=\prod_{\phi\in\mathcal{P}}\phi^{d_{\phi}}
. By (
2.1
),

\Pr[\deg\min(M)<n-\delta\cap\mathrm{char}(M)=h]=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\phi}\vdash e_{\phi}\\
\max\lambda_{\phi}=d_{\phi}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\prod_{\phi}q^{\deg\phi\cdot\sum(\lambda_{\phi,i}^{{}^{\prime}})^{2}}\prod(\frac{1}{q^{\deg\phi}})_{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}}\ll\\
\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\phi}\vdash e_{\phi}\\
\max\lambda_{\phi}=d_{\phi}\end{subarray}}\frac{q^{o(n)}}{\prod_{\phi}q^{\deg\phi\cdot\sum(\lambda_{\phi,i}^{{}^{\prime}})^{2}}}.

The proof for why
\prod(\frac{1}{q^{\deg\phi}})_{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}
is
q^{o(n)}
is essentially the same as the proof of Lemma
3.3
, hence is omitted. The number of partitions of
n
is
q^{o(n)}
, so we just need to bound from above the summand

\frac{1}{\prod_{\phi}q^{\deg\phi\cdot\sum(\lambda_{\phi,i}^{{}^{\prime}})^{2}}}.
Note that since
\lambda_{\phi}
is a partition of
e_{\phi}
with maximal part
d_{\phi}
,
\lambda^{\prime}_{\phi}
is a partition of
e_{\phi}
with exactly
d_{\phi}
parts. To minimize the sum
\sum(\lambda_{\phi,i}^{{}^{\prime}})^{2}
we thus take each part to be of the same size
\frac{e_{\phi}}{d_{\phi}}
. This gives

\frac{1}{\prod_{\phi}q^{\deg\phi\cdot\sum(\lambda_{\phi,i}^{{}^{\prime}})^{2}}}<\frac{1}{\prod_{\phi}q^{\deg\phi\cdot d_{\phi}\cdot\frac{e_{\phi}^{2}}{d_{\phi}^{2}}}}=\frac{1}{\prod_{\phi}q^{e_{\phi}\deg\phi\cdot\frac{e_{\phi}}{d_{\phi}}}}.

We thus want to minimize
\sum e_{\phi}\deg\phi\frac{e_{\phi}}{d_{\phi}}
. Define a probability measure on
\mathcal{P}
by setting
\Pr[\phi]=\frac{e_{\phi}\deg\phi}{n}
. We thus want to minimize
\mathbb{E}[\frac{e_{\phi}}{d_{\phi}}]
. Note that

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{d_{\phi}}{e_{\phi}}\right]=\frac{\deg(\min(M))}{n}<\frac{n-\delta}{n}.

Now by Jensen’s inequality, we get

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{e_{\phi}}{d_{\phi}}\right]<\frac{n}{n-\delta},

which concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 5.2
.

One could improve the bound a little by using Holder’s defect formula (see
[
28
, §6]
). Also, for some special polynomials (e.g. squarefree) one can improve the results by other methods. However, since the finite field results bound the ranges in our main theorems, this does not improve our main result, hence we did not pursue this.

Remark 5.3
.

Despite the previous remark, the range in Claim
5.1
is not too far from optimal. One can see that by taking
h=(x-1)^{n}
, and then approximating the probability to get a conjugacy class which contains
\lfloor\frac{n}{n-\delta}\rfloor
Jordan blocks
J_{n-\delta}(1)
.

We get as a corollary

Claim 5.4
.

Let
M\in SL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be chosen uniformly at random, and let
h\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
be a polynomial with
h(0)=(-1)^{n}
. Then, for
0\leq\delta<n
integer,

\Pr[\deg\min(M)=n-\delta\cap\mathrm{char}(M)=h]<q^{-\frac{n^{2}}{n-\delta}+o(n)}.

Proof.

Note that
\mathrm{char}(M)(0)=(-1)^{n}
is equivalent to
M\in SL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Thus if we combine the last claim with the fact that
[GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q}):SL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})]=q-1
, we finish.
∎

5.2
For
Sp_{2n}

We want to understand the probability that a matrix
M\in Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, chosen uniformly at random, has an exceptionally small minimal polynomial. To do this, we first recall some results of Fulman
[
8
]
on the distribution of
Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
-conjugacy classes. In the current section, we use the notation introduced and discussed in section
4.1.2
.

The collection of polynomials arising as the characteristic polynomial of a matrix in
Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
for some
n
is the set of monic, even-degree self-reciprocal polynomials with
f(0)\neq 0
. Equivalently, one can show that it is the set of polynomials that factor into irreducibles as follows,

f=(x-1)^{2a}(x+1)^{2b}\prod_{i=1}^{r}P_{i}^{e_{i}}\prod_{j=1}^{s}(Q_{j}Q_{j}^{\mathrm{r}})^{s},

where
P_{i}^{\mathrm{r}}=P_{i}
and
P_{i},Q_{i}\neq x,x\pm 1
are distinct irreducible polynomials.

Theorem 5.5

(
[
8
]
, Theorem 1)
.

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{\phi})_{\phi\in\mathcal{P}}
be a signed symplectic partition with
|\lambda|=2n
. Let
C
be the conjugacy class of
Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
parameterized by
\lambda
. Then,

\Pr[M\in C]=\prod_{\phi\in\mathcal{P}}\frac{1}{q^{\deg\phi(\sum_{h<i}hm_{h}(\lambda_{\phi})m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}(i-1)m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})^{2})}\prod_{i}A(\phi^{i})}

Where here, for
\phi=x\pm 1

A(\phi^{i})=\begin{cases}|Sp_{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|,&\text{ if }i=1\pmod{2},\\
q^{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})/2}|O_{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}^{\mu(i)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|,&\text{ else.}\end{cases}

Here
\mu(i)
is the sign attached to the
i
-th part of
\phi
by
\lambda
(see section
4.1.2
). For
\phi\neq x\pm 1
,

A(\phi^{i})=\begin{cases}|U_{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{\deg\phi/2}})|,&\text{ if }\phi=\phi^{\mathrm{r}},\\
|GL_{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{\deg\phi}})|^{1/2},&\text{ else.}\end{cases}

It will be useful to reformulate Fulman’s result. To do that, we first estimate the size of
\frac{A(\phi^{i})}{q^{\frac{\deg\phi}{2}m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})^{2}}}
.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
\phi\in\mathcal{P}
. Then,

\frac{A(\phi^{i})}{q^{\frac{\deg\phi}{2}m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})^{2}}}=\begin{cases}\Theta\left(q^{\frac{1}{2}m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}\right),&\text{ if }\phi=x\pm 1\text{ and }i=1\pmod{2},\\
\Theta(1),&\text{ else.}\end{cases}

Proof.

We split to cases depending on whether
\phi=x\pm 1
or not.

\phi\neq x\pm 1
.
By
[
33
, §2.6]
we have

|U_{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{\deg\phi/2}})|=\Theta(q^{\frac{\deg\phi}{2}m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})^{2}}),|GL_{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{\deg\phi}})|^{1/2}=\Theta(q^{\frac{\deg\phi}{2}m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})^{2}}),

where the constant implicit in the
\Theta
notation is uniformly bounded in both
m_{i},q
. Hence the result follows.

\phi=x\pm 1
.
Again by
[
33
, §2.6]
,

|Sp_{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|=\Theta(q^{\frac{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})^{2}+m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}{2}}),|O_{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi}^{\pm})}^{\mu(i)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|=\Theta(q^{\frac{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})^{2}-m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}{2}}).

where the constant implicit in the
\Theta
notation is uniformly bounded in both
m_{i},q
. Now, the result follows from the definition of
A(\phi^{i})
.

∎

Corollary 5.7
.

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{\phi})_{\phi\in\mathcal{P}}
be a signed symplectic partition with
|\lambda|=2n
. Let
C
be the conjugacy class of
Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
parameterized by
\lambda
. Then,

\Pr[M\in C]\ll q^{o(n)}\prod_{\phi\in\mathcal{P}}\frac{1}{q^{\frac{\deg\phi}{2}\sum_{i}(\lambda_{\phi,i}^{{}^{\prime}})^{2}}}.

(5.1)

Proof.

Using
[
8
, Lemma 1]
we get that for any partition
\lambda_{\phi}
, we have

\sum_{h<i}hm_{h}(\lambda_{\phi})m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}(i-1)m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}((\lambda_{\phi})_{i}^{\prime 2}-m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})^{2}).

(5.2)

Combining this with the previous proposition and applying it to the formula in Fulman’s Theorem
5.5
we get the desired result.
∎

We can now prove a result on the typical degree of the minimal polynomial.

Theorem 5.8
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=2n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
be a self-reciprocal polynomial, and assume that
x\nmid f
. Then, for
0\leq\delta<n
integer,

\Pr[\deg\min(M)=n-\delta\cap\mathrm{char}(M)=f]\ll q^{-\frac{n^{2}}{2(n-\delta)}+o(n)}.

Proof.

Factor
f=\mathrm{char}(M)=\prod_{\phi\in\mathcal{P}}\phi^{e_{\phi}}
,
\min(M)=\prod_{\phi\in\mathcal{P}}\phi^{d_{\phi}}
. By Corollary
5.7
, we have

\Pr[\deg\min(M)=n-\delta\cap\mathrm{char}(M)=f]\ll q^{o(n)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{\phi}\vdash e_{\phi}\\
\max\lambda_{\phi}=d_{\phi}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\prod_{\phi}q^{\frac{\deg\phi}{2}\sum(\lambda_{\phi,i}^{{}^{\prime}})^{2}}},

where the sum is over signed symplectic partitions. The total number of partitions
\lambda=(\lambda_{\phi})
is
q^{o(n)}
, so we just need to bound from above the summand

\frac{1}{\prod_{\phi}p^{\frac{\deg\phi}{2}\sum(\lambda_{\phi,i}^{{}^{\prime}})^{2}}}
.We finish as in the proof of Claim
5.1
.
∎

5.3
For
SO_{n}^{\pm}

Let
\epsilon=\pm
be fixed and work in
O_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,
SO_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. In the present section, we use the notation introduced and discussed in section
4.1.3
. Similarly to the symplectic case, the characteristic polynomial
f
of a matrix
M\in O_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
factors as follows,

f=(x-1)^{a}(x+1)^{b}\prod_{j=1}^{r}P_{i}^{e_{i}}\prod_{j=1}^{s}(Q_{j}Q_{j}^{\mathrm{r}})^{s},

where
P_{i}^{\mathrm{r}}=P_{i}
and
P_{i}
,
Q_{i}\neq x,x\pm 1
are distinct irreducible polynomials.

Theorem 5.9

(
[
8
]
, Theorem 2)
.

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{\phi})_{\phi\in\mathcal{P}}
be a signed orthogonal partition with
|\lambda|=n
. Let
C
be the conjugacy class of
O^{\epsilon}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
parametrized by
\lambda
. Then,

\Pr[M\in C]<\prod_{\phi\in\mathcal{P}}\frac{1}{q^{\deg\phi(\sum_{h<i}hm_{h}(\lambda_{\phi})m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}(i-1)m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})^{2})}\prod_{i}B(\phi^{i})}.

Here, for
\phi=x\pm 1
,

B(\phi^{i}):=\begin{cases}q^{-m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})/2}|Sp_{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|,&\text{ if }i=1\pmod{2},\\
|O_{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}^{\mu(i)}(\mathbb{F}_{q})|,&\text{ else.}\end{cases}

Where
\mu(i)
is the sign attached to the
i
-th part of
\lambda_{\phi}
(see section
4.1.3
). For
\phi\neq x\pm 1
,

B(\phi^{i})=\begin{cases}|U_{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{\deg\phi/2}})|,&\text{ if }\phi=\phi^{\mathrm{r}},\\
|GL_{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{\deg\phi}})|^{1/2},&\text{ else.}\end{cases}

Proposition 5.10
.

Let
\phi\in\mathcal{P}
. Then,

\frac{B(\phi^{i})}{q^{\frac{\deg\phi}{2}m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})^{2}}}=\begin{cases}\Theta\left(q^{-\frac{1}{2}m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}\right),&\text{ if }\phi=x\pm 1\text{ and }i=0\pmod{2},\\
\Theta(1),&\text{ else.}\end{cases}

Proof.

We note that for
\phi\neq x\pm 1
,
B(\phi^{i})=A(\phi^{i})
, so that the result follows from proposition
5.6
. For
\phi=x\pm 1
, following the proof of proposition
5.6
we see that if
i
is odd,

B(\phi^{i})=\Theta(1)
, and else it is
\Theta(q^{-\frac{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}{2}})
.
∎

Corollary 5.11
.

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{\phi})_{\phi\in\mathcal{P}}
be a signed orthogonal partition with
|\lambda|=n
. Let
C
be the conjugacy class of
O_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
parameterized by
\lambda
. Then,

\Pr_{M\in O^{\epsilon}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}[M\in C]\ll q^{n/2+o(n)}\prod_{\phi\in\mathcal{P}}\frac{1}{q^{\frac{\deg\phi}{2}\sum_{i}(\lambda_{\phi,i}^{{}^{\prime}})^{2}}}.

(5.3)

Proof.

The proof is almost the same as in the symplectic case, only now we have to bound the sum
\sum_{i\text{ is even}}\frac{m_{2i}(\lambda_{\phi})}{2}
for
\phi=x\pm 1
. We note that if the total weight of the even parts is
k
, then the sum of multiplicities of the even parts is at most
k/2
. Since
k<n
, we get that
\sum_{i\text{ is even}}\frac{m_{2i}(\lambda_{\phi})}{2}<\frac{n}{4}
. Since there are two possible choices for
\phi
we get the result.
∎

Theorem 5.12
.

Let
f\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
be a self-reciprocal polynomial, and assume that
x\nmid f
. Then, for
0\leq\delta<n
integer,

\Pr_{M\in SO^{\epsilon}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}[\deg\min(M)=n-\delta\cap\mathrm{char}(M)=h]\ll q^{\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n^{2}}{2(n-\delta)}+o(n)}.

Proof.

The bound for
O^{\epsilon}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is essentially the same as in the symplectic case, only now we use the bound from the last corollary. To go from
O_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
to
SO^{\epsilon}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, we observe that for
M\in O_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,
\mathrm{char}(M)(0)=(-1)^{n}
is equivalent to
M\in SO_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Hence since
[O_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q}):O_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})]=2
we prove the theorem.
∎

5.4
For
U_{n}

The characteristic polynomial
f
of a matrix
M\in U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
factors as follows,

f=\prod_{i=1}^{r}P_{i}^{e_{i}}\prod_{j=1}^{s}(Q_{j}\widetilde{Q_{j}})^{d_{j}},

where
\widetilde{P_{i}}=P_{i}
and
P_{i},Q_{i}
are distinct irreducible polynomials in
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]
. In the following section, we use the notation introduced and discussed in section
4.1.4
.

Theorem 5.13

(
[
7
]
, §3)
.

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{\phi})_{\phi\in\mathcal{P}}
be a unitary partition with
|\lambda|=n
. Let
C
be the conjugacy class of
U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
parameterized by
\lambda
. Then,

\Pr[M\in C]=\prod_{\phi}\frac{1}{q^{2\deg\phi(\sum_{h<i}hm_{h}(\lambda_{\phi})m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}(i-1)m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})^{2})}\prod_{i}C(\phi^{i})},

Where here,

C(\phi^{i})=\begin{cases}|U_{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{\deg\phi}})|,&\text{ if }\widetilde{\phi}=\phi,\\
|GL_{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{2\deg\phi}})|^{1/2},&\text{ else.}\end{cases}

Corollary 5.14
.

Let
\lambda=(\lambda_{\phi})_{\phi\in\mathcal{P}}
be a unitary partition with
|\lambda|=n
. Let
C
be the conjugacy class of
U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
parametrized by
\lambda
. Then,

\Pr[M\in C]\ll q^{o(n)}\prod_{\phi}q^{-\deg\phi\sum_{i}(\lambda_{\phi,i}^{\prime})^{2}}.

Proof.

We recall that
|U_{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{\deg\phi}})|=\Theta(q^{\deg\phi m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})^{2}})
, and also
|GL_{m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{2\deg\phi}})|=\Theta(q^{2\deg\phi\cdot m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})^{2}})
. Thus,
C(\phi^{i})=\Theta(q^{\deg\phi\cdot m_{i}(\lambda_{\phi})^{2}}).
Combining this with equation (
5.2
), we get the result.
∎

Now using the previous corollary and a similar proof to that of claim
5.1
, we get

Claim 5.15
.

Let
M\in U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be chosen uniformly at random, and let
h\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
be a self-skew-reciprocal polynomial. Then, for
0\leq\delta<n
integer,

\Pr[\deg\min(M)=n-\delta\cap\mathrm{char}(M)=h]<q^{-\frac{n^{2}}{n-\delta}+o(n)}.

6
Traces and characteristic polynomial for
GL_{n}

For the rest of Section
6
,
A
will be a matrix from
GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
for some
k\geq 1
, unless explicitly stated otherwise.

6.1
The image of
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}

Recall that we defined the map
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}:\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\to\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<n}
by
A_{1}\mapsto\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{0})A_{0}A_{1})
. We note that in the case of
G=GL_{n}
, since
A_{1}\mapsto A_{0}A_{1}
is a permutation on
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
,
\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}})
is equal to the image of the map
A_{1}\mapsto\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{0})A_{1})
. Hence, as explained in Section
4.3
, one can move from
A_{0}
to any element in its conjugacy class without changing
\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}})
. To calculate the image, we will need the following preparatory lemma:

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
A\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,
B\in GL_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be two matrices. Then,

\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A\oplus B})=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(\mathrm{char}(B)\cdot\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A}),\mathrm{char}(A)\cdot\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{B})).

Proof.

We note that
\mathfrak{gl}_{n+m}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=M_{n+m}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Write an element
A_{1}\in M_{n+m}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
as a block matrix

A_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}X&Y\\
Z&W\end{array}\right).

Then,

\partial\mathrm{char}_{A\oplus B}(A_{1})=\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Adj}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}x-A&0\\
0&x-B\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}X&Y\\
Z&W\end{array}\right)\right).

Note that the entries of
Y,Z
do not contribute to the result. Thus we may assume freely that they are zero. We get that

\partial\mathrm{char}_{A\oplus B}(A_{1})=\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Adj}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}x-A&0\\
0&x-B\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}X&0\\
0&W\end{array}\right)\right)=\\
=\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Adj}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{char}(B)\mathrm{Adj}(x-A)&0\\
0&\mathrm{char}(A)\mathrm{Adj}(x-B)\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}X&0\\
0&W\end{array}\right)\right)=\\
=\mathrm{char}(B)\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A)X)+\mathrm{char}(A)\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-B)W),

which finishes the proof.
∎

Theorem 6.2
.

Let
A\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be a matrix. Then,
\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A})=\left\{\frac{\mathrm{char}A}{\min A}a:a\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<\deg\min A}\right\}
.

Proof.

It’s enough to prove the claim over an extension field
\mathbb{F}_{q^{l}}
over which
\mathrm{char}A
splits completely. Indeed, assuming this, the image is a linear space contained in the RHS. If there is equality over an extension field, then
\mathrm{rank}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A})=\deg\min A
over an extension field. But the rank is invariant to field extensions, thus we get the result.

Further, we claim that it is enough to prove the claim for the case of
A
primary (that is,
\mathrm{char}A=(x-\alpha)^{n}
). This can be shown by induction on the number of different primary parts in
A
. if
A=B\oplus C
, where
C
is primary and
\gcd(\mathrm{char}B,\mathrm{char}C)=1
, then by Lemma
6.1
and the induction hypothesis we get

\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{B\oplus C})=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{q^{l}}}\left\{\mathrm{char}C\left\{\frac{\mathrm{char}B}{\min B}b:\deg b<\deg\min B\right\},\mathrm{char}B\left\{\frac{\mathrm{char}C}{\min C}c:\deg c<\deg\min C\right\}\right\},

and this proves the result using the Chinese remainder theorem for
\mathbb{F}_{q^{l}}[x]
and comparing dimensions.

It remains to prove the claim for a primary matrix. Let
A
be such that
\mathrm{char}A=(x-\alpha)^{n}
. Write
A
as a direct sum of
r
matrices
A_{i}
, each with
\mathrm{char}A_{i}=\min A_{i}=(x-\alpha)^{m_{i}}
, and such that
m_{1}\geq m_{2}\geq\cdots\geq m_{r}
. We again prove the claim by induction on the number of parts
r
. Write
A=B\oplus A_{r}
. Then,

\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{B\oplus A_{r}})=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{q^{l}}}\left\{(x-\alpha)^{m_{r}}\left\{\frac{(x-\alpha)^{n-m_{r}}}{(x-\alpha)^{m_{1}}}b:\deg b<m_{1}\right\},(x-\alpha)^{n-m_{r}}\left\{c:\deg c<\deg m_{r}\right\}\right\}.

which proves the result. Thus, we are only left with the case of a matrix
A_{0}
with
\mathrm{char}A_{0}=\min A_{0}=(x-\alpha)^{r}
. We note that since conjugation from elements in
GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{l}})
preserves the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{l}})
, we can study the image of
A_{1}\mapsto\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{0})gA_{1}g^{-1})
instead, for any
g\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{l}})
. The trace is cyclic and by the properties of the adjoint matrix, this is equal to
\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-gA_{0}g^{-1})A_{1})
. Thus without loss of generality, we may assume that
A_{0}
is given in Jordan form. But then we know that it is a Jordan block, and we have by Lemma
4.22
,

\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{0})=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}(x-\alpha)^{r-1}&(x-\alpha)^{r-2}&(x-\alpha)^{r-3}&\cdots&1\\
0&(x-\alpha)^{r-1}&(x-\alpha)^{r-2}&\cdots&x-\alpha\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\cdots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0&(x-\alpha)^{r-1}&(x-\alpha)^{r-2}\\
0&0&\cdots&0&(x-\alpha)^{r-1}\end{array}\right).

Since
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{l}})=M_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q^{l}})
, one can see that the claim follows for such
A_{0}
, and thus we finish the proof.
∎

6.2
Distribution of a single trace

Our goal is to bound the total variation distance
d_{TV}(\mu_{\mathrm{tr}(A^{r})},\mu_{U})
for
A\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
chosen uniformly at random w.r.t. the Haar probability measure. By Lemma
2.10
, it is enough to bound
d_{TV}(\mu_{\mathrm{tr}(\rho_{k}(A^{r}))},\mu_{\rho_{k}(U)})
, and show that it remains
o(1)
as
n\to\infty
, uniformly in
k
. In this part, we assume
p\nmid r
.

Theorem 6.3
.

Let
U
be the uniform probability measure on
\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}
,
A
chosen u.a.r. from
GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
. Let
r=r(n)
be such that
\log r=o(n^{2})
,
p\nmid r
. Then,

d_{TV}(\mu_{\mathrm{tr}(A^{r})},\mu_{U})\to_{n\to\infty}0,

uniformly in
k
.

Proof.

Let
A=A_{0}+\pi^{k-1}A_{0}A_{1}
, where
A_{0}
is chosen from a set of representatives for
GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k-1}\mathcal{O})
,
A_{1}\in\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. From Lemma
4.20
, we have
\mathrm{tr}(A^{r})=\mathrm{tr}(A_{0}^{r})+r\pi^{k-1}\mathrm{tr}(A_{0}^{r}A_{1})
. Recall that we denoted the linear functional
A_{1}\mapsto\mathrm{tr}(A_{0}^{r}A_{1})
by
\partial\mathrm{tr}_{\overline{A_{0}}}
. This linear functional is either identically zero or equidistributed. Note that for it to be identically zero we must have
\overline{A_{0}}^{r}
in the orthogonal space to
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
under the bilinear form
\mathrm{tr}
. But
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=M_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, and
\overline{A_{0}}\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, thus
\partial\mathrm{tr}_{\overline{A_{0}}}
always equidsitributes. By recursion, we get that for any
x\in\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}
,

\Pr[\mathrm{tr}(A^{r})=x]=\Pr[\mathrm{tr}(\overline{A}^{r})=\overline{x}]\prod_{i=2}^{k}\Pr[\mathrm{tr}(\rho_{i}(A)^{r})=\rho_{i}(x)|\mathrm{tr}(\rho_{i-1}(A)^{r})=\mathrm{tr}(\rho_{i-1}(x))]=q^{-(k-1)}\Pr[\mathrm{tr}(\overline{A}^{r})=\overline{x}].

By
[
11
]
, for any
x\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
we have

|\Pr[\mathrm{tr}(\overline{A}^{r})=\overline{x}]-q^{-1}|\to_{n\to\infty}0,

independently of
k
. Thus, we get the desired result on the total variation distance, by summing the error terms and using the triangle inequality.
∎

As a corollary, we get using Lemma
2.10
.

Theorem 6.4
.

Let
U
be the Haar probability measure on
\mathcal{O}
, and let
r=r(n)
be such that
\log r=o(n^{2})
, and
p\nmid r
. Let
A
be chosen from
GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
, uniformly at random w.r.t. the Haar measure. Then,

d_{TV}(\mu_{\mathrm{tr}(A^{r})},\mu_{U})\to_{n\to\infty}0.

Remark 6.5
.

Modifications of this argument can prove similar results on a single trace of power for the other groups. However, since there is no extended range result for a single trace of power in the other finite groups, and since we were not able to get better results here than the results for the joint distribution of traces of powers, we did not pursue this.

6.3
Distribution of the characteristic polynomial

To compute the total variation distance

d_{TV}(\mu_{\mathrm{TR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{G}},\mu_{\mathrm{UTR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}})
we pass through estimating the distribution of the characteristic polynomial in short intervals of
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
. While we cannot get a discrepancy bound for the distribution in intervals as in
[
12
]
, which is good for any interval, we do manage to show that on average the discrepancy is small. For the rest of the section, fix
0\leq d_{1},d_{2}<n
two integers such that
d_{1}+d_{2}=d<n-1
.

6.3.1
One step from
\pi^{k-1}
to
\pi^{k}

The basic idea of the proof is sort of an induction step, assuming we already know that the distribution modulo
\pi^{k-1}
is close to uniform and going up to mod
\pi^{k}
. The base case
k=1
is given by our extension of the results in
[
12
]
, namely Proposition
3.25
. The induction step is based on the properties of
\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}})
, as discussed in Section
6.1
.

Let
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
be a monic polynomial of degree
n
, and let
A\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
be written as
A_{0}+\pi^{k}A_{1}
, where
A_{0}\in H_{G,k}
is a representative for
A\pmod{\pi^{k-1}}
. Recall that then, the possible values for
\mathrm{char}(A)
are precisely the polynomials in
\mathrm{char}(A_{0})+\pi^{k-1}\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{\overline{A}_{0}})
, and that

\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{\overline{A}_{0}})=\left\{\frac{\mathrm{char}A_{0}}{\min A_{0}}a\Bigg|a\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<\deg\min A_{0}}\right\}.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
be a monic polynomial of degree
n
. Then

\Pr\left[\mathrm{char}(A)\equiv g\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}}\Bigg|\mathrm{char}(A)\equiv g\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k-1}}},\deg\min\overline{A}>d\right]=q^{-(d+1)}.

Proof.

We show that for any possible choice of
A\mod{\pi^{k-1}}
, the conditional probability is
q^{-(d+1)}
, and this will prove the claim.
Write
A=A_{0}+\pi^{k-1}A_{0}A_{1}
. Then,

\mathrm{char}(A)=\mathrm{char}(A_{0})+\pi^{k-1}\partial\mathrm{char}_{\overline{A}_{0}}(A_{1}).

We are given that
\mathrm{char}(\rho_{k-1}(A))\equiv g\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k-1}}}
, thus we can write
g-\mathrm{char}(A_{0})=e_{0}(x)+\pi^{k-1}e_{1}(x)
, where
\deg e_{0}(x)<n-d_{1}
and
e_{0}(x)=0\pmod{x^{d_{2}+1}}
. Hence to have
\mathrm{char}(A)\equiv g\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}}
we actually need to have

\deg(\partial\mathrm{char}_{\overline{A_{0}}}(A_{1})-\overline{e_{1}})<n-d_{1},\partial\mathrm{char}_{\overline{A_{0}}}(A_{1})-\overline{e_{1}}\equiv 0\pmod{x^{d_{2}+1}}.

Since
\deg(\overline{e_{1}}),\deg(\partial\mathrm{char}_{\overline{A_{0}}}(A_{1}))<n
this is equivalent to having

x^{n}+\partial\mathrm{char}_{\overline{A}_{0}}(A_{1})\equiv x^{n}+\overline{e_{1}}\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi}}.

Finally, since
\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}
is monic, this is equivalent to having

x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}+\partial\mathrm{char}_{\overline{A_{0}}}(A_{1})\equiv x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}+\overline{e_{1}}\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi}}.

Let
r,s\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<n}
be two polynomials. We denote by
1_{r,s}
the indicator that

x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}+r\equiv x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}+s\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi}}.

We now note that the map
\partial\mathrm{char}_{\overline{A}_{0}}:\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\to\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<n}
is linear, hence all fibers are of the same size. Thus, it is enough to argue that the image is equidistributed modulo
R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi}
. Now we are ready to compute the probability directly, using Hayes characters. From the above discussion, it is equal to

q^{-\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}}1_{\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}a,\overline{e_{1}}}=\\
=q^{-\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}}q^{-(d+1)}\sum_{\chi\in\widehat{R}_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi}}\chi\left(\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}(x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}+a)\right)\overline{\chi}\left(x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}+\overline{e_{1}}\right)=\\
