=q^{-(d+1)}\sum_{\chi\in\widehat{R}_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi}}\chi\left(\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}\right)\overline{\chi}\left(x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}+\overline{e_{1}}\right)q^{-\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}\sum_{a\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}}\chi(x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}+a).

Here the innermost sum equals
1_{\chi=\chi_{0}}
by the the orthogonality of characters, hence we finish.
∎

6.3.2
Distribution mod
\pi^{k}
given mod
\pi
behavior

Let
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
be a monic polynomial of degree
n
. Let
B\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be a matrix with
\mathrm{char}B\equiv\overline{g}\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi}}
. Define the discrepancy of the conditional distribution starting from
B
to be

\Delta_{B,k}^{g}:=\Pr\left[{\mathrm{char}(A)\equiv g\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}}}\Bigg|{\overline{A}=B}\right]-q^{-(d+1)(k-1)}.

For
\deg\min B>d
, the discrepancy is zero.

Proposition 6.7
.

Let
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
be a monic polynomial of degree
n
, and let
B\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be a matrix with
\mathrm{char}(B)\equiv g\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi}}
. Then, if
\deg\min(B)>d
,
\Delta_{B,k}^{g}=0
.

Proof.

One can write

\Pr\left[{\mathrm{char}(A)\equiv g\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}}}\Bigg|{\overline{A}=B}\right]=\\
=\prod_{i=2}^{k}\Pr\left[{\mathrm{char}(\rho_{i}(A))\equiv\rho_{i}(g)\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{i}}}}\Bigg|{\mathrm{char}(\rho_{i-1}(A))\equiv\rho_{i-1}(g)\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{i-1}}},\overline{A}=B}\right].

We get that to compute
\Delta_{B,k}^{g}
, it is enough to compute each term in the product above.
Using Lemma
6.6
, we get that for all
i
,

\Pr\left[{\mathrm{char}(\rho_{i}(A))\equiv\rho_{i}(g)\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{i}}}}\Bigg|{\mathrm{char}(\rho_{i-1}(A))\equiv\rho_{i-1}(g)\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{i-1}}},\overline{A}=B}\right]=q^{-(d+1)},

so that
\Delta_{B,k}^{g}=0
.

∎

In the case of
B
with
\deg\min B
small, we would like to bound this discrepancy as tightly as possible, to get a result on the distribution of
\mathrm{TR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{G,k}
(or equivalently, on the distribution of
\mathrm{char}A
in Hayes residue classes). We start by considering the lifts of
B\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, when
\deg\min B=l\leq d
.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
be a monic polynomial of degree
n
. Let
B\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be a matrix with
\deg\min B=l\leq d
. Let
\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k}=\{\mathrm{char}(A):A\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}),\overline{A}=B\}
. Then,

1.

|\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k}|=q^{(k-1)l}
. Moreover, each element of
\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k}
is in a different residue class modulo
R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}
.

2.

\Pr\left[{\mathrm{char}(A)\equiv g\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}}}\Bigg|{\overline{A}=B}\right]=1_{\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k}\cap gR_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}\neq\emptyset}q^{-(k-1)l}
. Equivalently, we have

\Delta_{B,k}^{g}=1_{\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k}\cap gR_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}\neq\emptyset}q^{-(k-1)l}-q^{-(d+1)(k-1)}.

Proof.

We prove the claim by induction on
k
. For
k=1
this is trivial. Suppose that we proved the proposition for all natural numbers
<k
, and prove it to
k
. We note that the fibers of the map
\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k}\to\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k-1}
,
h\mapsto\rho_{k-1}(h)
, are precisely of the form
f_{0}+\pi^{k-1}\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{B})
, for some
f_{0}\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
. We note that by Theorem
6.2
, each fiber is of size
q^{l}
. Finally, we note that by Lemma
6.6
with
d=l
, each of the elements in the fiber is in a different residue class of
R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}
, hence the first assertion follows for
k
. As for the second assertion, it follows for
k
from the previous proposition when we take
d=l
, together with the first assertion of the current proposition.
∎

We now let
\mathcal{G}
be a set of representatives for the lifts of
\mathrm{char}(B)
to a monic polynomial
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
of degree
n
, modulo the relation
R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}
. We note that
|\mathcal{G}|=q^{(d+1)(k-1)}
.

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
B\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Then,

1.

If
\deg\min{B}>d
, we have
\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{B,k}^{g}|=0
.

2.

If
\deg\min B=l\leq d
, we have
\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{B,k}^{g}|\leq 2
.

Proof.

The first claim follows directly from Proposition
6.7
. The second follows from the triangle inequality, together with the two parts of Proposition
6.8
.
∎

Let
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
be a monic polynomial of degree
n
. We define the discrepancy of a polynomial
\overline{f}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
such that
\overline{f}\equiv\overline{g}\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi}}
by

\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}:=\Pr[\mathrm{char}(A)\equiv g\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}}|\mathrm{char}(\overline{A})=\overline{f}]-q^{-(d+1)(k-1)}.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
\overline{f}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
be a polynomial, and let
\mathcal{G}
be a set of representatives for the possible lifts of
\overline{f}
to a monic polynomial in
(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
of degree
n
, modulo the relation
R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}
.

1.

If
\deg\mathrm{rad}\overline{f}>d
, then
\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}|=0
.

2.

If
\deg\mathrm{rad}\overline{f}=s\leq d
, then
\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}|\leq 2\Pr_{B\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}[\deg\min(B)\leq d|\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f}]
.

Proof.

We have

\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\\
\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{B,k}^{g}\cdot\Pr[\overline{A}=B|\mathrm{char}(\overline{A})=\overline{f}].

By Proposition
6.7
we conclude that

\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\\
\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f},\deg\min B\leq d\end{subarray}}\Delta_{B,k}^{g}\cdot\Pr[\overline{A}=B|\mathrm{char}(\overline{A})=\overline{f}]

For
\deg\mathrm{rad}\overline{f}>d
, since
\mathrm{rad}\overline{f}|\min B
, we get that
\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}=0
and this proves the first assertion of the lemma. For
\deg\mathrm{rad}\overline{f}=s\leq d
, summing the previous equation over all
g\in\mathcal{G}
and then applying the triangle inequality and exchanging the order of summation we get

\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}|\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\\
\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f},\deg\min B\leq d\end{subarray}}\Pr[\overline{A}=B|\mathrm{char}(\overline{A})=\overline{f}]\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{B,k}^{g}|\leq\\
\leq 2\Pr_{B\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}[\deg\min(B)\leq d|\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f}].

∎

6.3.3
Discrepancy bound on distribution in Hayes residue classes

Now, for a monic polynomial
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
of degree
n
we define the discrepancy of its Hayes residue class to be

\Delta_{g}:=\Pr[\mathrm{char}A\equiv g\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}}]-q^{-k(d+1)}.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a set of representatives for monic polynomials in
(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
of degree
n
modulo the relation
R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}
. Then,

\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{g}|=O\left(q^{-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+d}{n}+q^{d-\frac{n^{2}}{d}+o(n)}\right).

Proof.

Let
g\in\mathcal{G}
. We have (for
A\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
)

\Pr[\mathrm{char}A\equiv g\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}}]=\\
=\sum_{\overline{f}\equiv\overline{g}\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi}}\atop{\overline{f}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]}}\Pr[\mathrm{char}A\equiv g\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}}|\mathrm{char}\overline{A}=\overline{f}]\Pr[\mathrm{char}\overline{A}=\overline{f}]=\\
=\sum_{\overline{f}\equiv\overline{g}\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi}}\atop{\overline{f}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]}}\left[q^{-(k-1)(d+1)}+\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}\right]\Pr[\mathrm{char}\overline{A}=\overline{f}]=\\
=q^{-(k-1)(d+1)}\Pr[\mathrm{char}\overline{A}\equiv\overline{g}\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi}}]+\sum_{\overline{f}\equiv\overline{g}\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi}}}\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}\cdot\Pr[\mathrm{char}\overline{A}=\overline{f}].

By Corollary
3.25
, we have the following bound on the mod
\pi
discrepancy:

\Pr[\mathrm{char}\overline{A}\equiv\overline{g}\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi}}]=q^{-(d+1)}+O\left(q^{-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+d}{n}\right).

Hence,

|\Delta_{g}|<O\left(q^{-(k-1)(d+1)-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+d}{n}\right)+\sum_{\overline{f}\equiv\overline{g}\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi}}}|\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}|\cdot\Pr[\mathrm{char}\overline{A}=\overline{f}].

Summing the last equation over all
g\in\mathcal{G}
, and using Lemma
6.10
, we get that

\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{g}|<\\
<O\left(q^{d-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+d}{n}\right)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\overline{f}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]\\
\deg\mathrm{rad}\overline{f}\leq d\end{subarray}}2\Pr_{B\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}[\deg\min(B)\leq d|\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f}]\Pr_{B\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}[\mathrm{char}B=\overline{f}].

We note that by Claim
5.1
,

\Pr_{B\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}[\deg\min(B)\leq d|\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f}]\Pr_{B\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}[\mathrm{char}B=\overline{f}]=\Pr[\deg\min(B)\leq d\cap\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f}]<q^{-\frac{n^{2}}{d}+o(n)},

By Lemma
3.3
, the number of polynomials in
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
with a radical of degree
\leq d
is
q^{d+o(n)}
. Hence we get the result.
∎

6.4
Joint distribution of traces

We would like to bound the total variation distance
d_{TV}(\mu_{\mathrm{TR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{GL}},\mu_{\mathrm{UTR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}})
. Using Lemma
2.10
we may reduce to the
\pmod{\pi^{k}}
total variation distance. We first recall our Corollary
3.25
, which extends the result
[
12
, Theorem 1.3]
. It follows from it that there is a constant
c_{q}^{GL}
for which there is a super-exponential bound for the discrepancy of the distribution of
\mathrm{char}(B)
(for
B\in GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
) in Hayes residue classes of length
d<c_{q}^{GL}\cdot n
. The constant
c_{q}^{GL}
is determined by the range of
d
for which

q^{d-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+d}{n}=_{n\to\infty}o(1).

Theorem 6.12
.

Let
0\leq d_{1},d_{2}<n
be integers such that
d_{1}+d_{2}=d<n-1
. Let
\mathrm{TR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{GL,k}
be the random variable attaching to a matrix
M\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
its
(d_{1},d_{2})
-trace datum, and let
\mathrm{UTR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{k}
be the uniform measure on
(d_{1},d_{2})
-trace data, as defined in Section
3.2
. Then, the total variation distance

d_{TV}(\mu_{\mathrm{TR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{GL,k}},\mu_{\mathrm{UTR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{k}})=\\
=\sum_{\mathfrak{a}\text{ is a }(d_{1},d_{2})-\text{trace datum}}\Bigg|\Pr_{M\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})}[M\text{ has trace data }\mathfrak{a}]-q^{-(kd-S(d_{1},k)-S(d_{2},k))}\Bigg|<\\
<O\left(q^{d-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+d}{n}+q^{d-\frac{n^{2}}{d}+o(n)}\right).

In particular, as long as
d<c_{q}^{GL}\cdot n
, this total variation distance is
o(1)
as
n\to\infty
, uniformly in
k
.

Proof.

Let
S=S(d_{1},k)+S(d_{2},k)
.
Lemma
3.10
gives that there are polynomials
\{g_{j}\}_{j=1}^{q^{S+k}}\subset(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
, such that

\Pr_{M\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})}[M\text{ has trace datum }\mathfrak{a}]=\sum_{i=1}^{q^{S+k}}\Pr_{M\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})}[M\equiv g_{j}\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}}]=q^{S-kd}+\sum_{i=1}^{q^{S+k}}\Delta_{g_{j}}.

Now, let
\mathcal{G}=\{g_{j}\}_{j=1}^{S+k},\mathcal{G}\subset(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
be a set of representatives for the monic polynomials modulo
R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}
. Using the triangle inequality we get that

\sum_{\mathfrak{a}\text{ trace datum}}\Bigg|\Pr_{M\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})}[M\text{ has trace data }\mathfrak{a}]-q^{-(kd-S)}\Bigg|<\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{g}|.

Now the theorem follows from Lemma
6.11
.
∎

By taking
k\to\infty
we get as a consequence Theorem
1.3
(using Lemma
2.10
), which we restate here for convenience.

Theorem 6.13
.

Let
0\leq d_{1},d_{2}<n
be integers such that
d_{1}+d_{2}=d<n-1
. Let
\mathrm{TR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{GL}
be the random variable attaching to a matrix
M\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
its
(d_{1},d_{2})
-trace datum. Let
\mathrm{UTR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}
be uniform
(d_{1},d_{2})
-trace datum. Then, we have the following total variation bound

d_{TV}(\mu_{\mathrm{TR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{GL}},\mu_{\mathrm{UTR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}})=O\left(q^{d-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+d}{n}+q^{d-\frac{n^{2}}{d}+o(n)}\right).

In particular, for
d<c_{q}^{GL}\cdot n
, this total variation distance is
o(1)
as
n\to\infty
.

As a direct consequence, we get the following

Theorem 6.14
.

Fix
d_{1},d_{2}>0
. Let
B_{-d_{2}},\ldots,B_{-1},B_{1},\ldots,B_{d_{1}}
be a fixed collection of balls in
\mathcal{O}
, and let
U_{-1},\ldots,U_{-d_{2}},U_{1},\ldots,U_{d_{1}}
be independent uniform variables on
\mathcal{O}
. Set
Z_{i}=\frac{1}{|i|_{p}}U_{i}
. Then,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\Pr_{M\in GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})}\left[\left(\mathrm{tr}(M^{i})-1_{p|i}\sigma(\mathrm{tr}(M^{i/p}))\in B_{i}\right)_{0\neq i=-d_{2}}^{d_{1}}\right]=\prod_{0\neq i=-d_{2}}^{d_{1}}\Pr[Z_{i}\in B_{i}].

7
Traces and characteristic polynomial for
SL_{n}

7.1
The image of
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}

Let
A_{0}\in SL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Since
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
is preserved by conjugation from
GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, the proofs for
SL_{n}
are close to the proofs for
GL_{n}
. However, there are some modifications to make. In this section, we use the map
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}
with domain equal to either
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
,
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}
. When there is a chance of confusion we write
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}^{SL}
,
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}^{GL}
(respectively) to make clear which domain we refer to. Whenever we write
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}
alone we always mean the map
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}^{SL}
. For the current section
7
,
A
will be a matrix in
SL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
unless explicitly stated otherwise.

Theorem 7.1
.

Let
B\in SL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be a matrix. Then,
\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{B})=\left\{\frac{\mathrm{char}B}{\min B}b:\deg b<\deg\min B,b(0)=0\right\}
.

Proof.

We note that
\mathfrak{gl}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is the direct sum of
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
and the one dimensional space

\mathbb{F}_{q}E_{11}
,
where
E_{11}
is the matrix with
1
in the
(1,1)
entry and zero elsewhere. Comparing dimensions we get that
\dim\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{B}^{SL})\geq\dim(\partial\mathrm{char}_{B}^{GL})-1=\deg\min B-1
.

Now let
B_{0}
be any specific lift of
B
to
SL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{2}\mathcal{O})
, and
B^{\prime}=B_{0}(I+\pi B_{1})
, where
B_{1}\in\mathfrak{sl}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be any other lift. We recall that by Lemma
4.21
,

\mathrm{char}(B^{\prime})=\mathrm{char}(B_{0})-\pi\partial\mathrm{char}_{B}^{SL}(B_{1}).

Since
B^{\prime},B_{0}\in SL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{2}\mathcal{O})
we must have that the free coefficients of
\mathrm{char}(B^{\prime}),\mathrm{char}(B_{0})
are equal. Hence, the free coefficient of
\partial\mathrm{char}_{B}^{SL}(B_{1})
must be zero. Thus,

\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{B}^{SL}(B_{1}))\subset\left\{\frac{\mathrm{char}B}{\min B}b:\deg b<\deg\min B,b(0)=0\right\},

and is of maximal dimension, hence the equality.
∎

7.2
Distribution of the characteristic polynoimal

The strategy for proving the total variation bound on
d_{TV}(\mu_{\mathrm{TR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{g}},\mu_{\mathrm{UTR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}})
for p-adic matrix groups
G\neq GL_{n}
is the same as for
GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
. In the
SL_{n}
case, we omit many of the proofs, due to the high similarity to the
GL_{n}
case. The main difference is that in this case, the free coefficient is determined, thus some of the constants look different. We now fix a choice of
0\leq d_{1},d_{2}<n
such that
d_{1}+d_{2}=d<n-1
for the rest of the section.

7.2.1
One step from
\pi^{k-1}
to
\pi^{k}

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
be a monic polynomial of degree
n
, with
g(0)=(-1)^{n}
. Then

\Pr[\mathrm{char}(A)\equiv g\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}}|\mathrm{char}(\rho_{k-1}(A))\equiv\rho_{k-1}(g)\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k-1}}},\deg\min\overline{A}\geq d+1]=q^{-d}.

Proof.

The polynomials with
g(0)=(-1)^{n}
form a Hayes residue class mod
R_{0,x,\pi^{k-1}}
. Hence this follows from Lemma
6.6
and the formula for conditional probability.
∎

7.2.2
Distribution mod
\pi^{k}
given mod
\pi
behavior

We define the discrepancy
of the conditional distribution given a polynomial
\overline{f}\equiv\overline{g}\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi}}
by

\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}:=\Pr[\mathrm{char}(A)\equiv g\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}}|\mathrm{char}(\overline{A})=\overline{f}]-q^{-(k-1)d}.

The
SL_{n}
analog of the main lemma of Section
6.3.2
is then

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
\overline{f}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
be a polynomial with
\overline{f}(0)=(-1)^{n}
, and let
\mathcal{G}
be a set of representatives for the possible lifts of
\overline{f}
to a monic polynomial in
(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
of degree
n
with
g(0)=(-1)^{n}
, modulo
R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}
.

1.

If
\deg\mathrm{rad}\overline{f}\geq d
, then
\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}|=0
.

2.

If
\deg\mathrm{rad}\overline{f}=s<d
, then
\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}|\leq 2\Pr_{B\in SL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}[\deg\min(B)<d|\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f}]
.

The proof is omitted since it is essentially the same as the proof of Lemma
6.10
.

7.2.3
Discrepancy bound on distribution in Hayes residue classes

For
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
monic of degree
n
with
g(0)=(-1)^{n}
, we define the discrepancy
\Delta_{g}
by

\Delta_{g}:=\Pr[\mathrm{char}(A)\equiv g\pmod{R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}}]-q^{-kd}.

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a set of representatives for monic polynomials
g
in
(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
of degree
n
, with
g(0)=(-1)^{n}
, modulo the relation
R_{d_{1},x^{d_{2}+1},\pi^{k}}
. Then,

\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{g}|=O\left(q^{-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+d}{n}+q^{d-\frac{n^{2}}{d}+o(n)}\right).

Proof.

The proof is the same as that of Lemma
6.11
, with the modifications of using the analogous results for
SL_{n}
(proven in the current section) when needed. To bound the mod
\pi
discrepancy, we use our extension of
[
12
, Theorem 4.6]
, namely Corollary
3.26
.
∎

7.3
Joint distribution of traces

As in Section
6.4
, we start by a bound on the total variation distance of the mod
\pi^{k}
variables,
\mathrm{TR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{SL,k},\mathrm{UTR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{k}
.

Theorem 7.5
.

Let
\mathrm{TR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{SL,k}
be the random variable attaching to a matrix
M\in SL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
its
(d_{1},d_{2})
-trace datum, and let
\mathrm{UTR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{k}
be a uniform
(d_{1},d_{2})
-trace datum, as defined in Section
3.2
. Let
S=S(d_{1},k)+S(d_{2},k)
. Then,

d_{TV}(\mu_{\mathrm{TR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{SL,k}},\mu_{\mathrm{UTR}_{d_{1},d_{2}}^{k}})=\sum_{\mathfrak{a}\text{ a }(d_{1},d_{2})\text{-trace data}}\Bigg|\Pr_{M\in SL_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})}[M\text{ has }(d_{1},d_{2})\text{-trace data }\mathfrak{a}]-q^{-(kd-S)}\Bigg|<\\
<O\left(q^{\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q^{2}-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+d}{n}+q^{d-\frac{n^{2}}{d}+o(n)}\right).

In particular, as long as
d<c_{q}^{SL}\cdot n
, this total variation distance is
o(1)
as
n\to\infty
, uniformly in
k
.

The proof is omitted since it is essentially the same as that of Theorem
6.12
, combining the observation that since the free coefficient is determined, many of the summands in Lemma
3.14
are
0
. From here Theorem
1.4
follows by taking
k\to\infty
in the previous theorem. We also get as a corollary

Theorem 7.6
.

Fix
d_{1},d_{2}>0
. Let
B_{-d_{2}},\ldots,B_{-1},B_{1},\ldots,B_{d_{1}}
be a collection of balls in
\mathcal{O}
, and let
U_{-1},\ldots,U_{-d_{2}},U_{1},\ldots,U_{d_{1}}
be independent, uniform random variables on
\mathcal{O}
. Set
Z_{i}=\frac{1}{|i|_{p}}U_{i}
. Then,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\Pr_{M\in SL_{n}(\mathcal{O})}\left[\left(\mathrm{tr}(M^{i})-1_{p|i}\sigma(\mathrm{tr}(M^{i/p}))\in B_{i}\right)_{0\neq i=-d_{2}}^{d_{1}}\right]=\prod_{0\neq i=-d_{2}}^{d_{1}}\Pr[Z_{i}\in B_{i}].

8
Traces and characteristic polynomial for
Sp_{2n}

8.1
The image of
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}

Recall the map
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}:\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\to\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<2n}
, defined (for
G=Sp_{2n}
) by
A_{1}\mapsto x\cdot\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{0})A_{1})
. As discussed in Section
4.3
, to compute the image of
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}
one can move from
A_{0}
to any element in its conjugacy class. To calculate the image, we will need the following preparatory lemma:

Lemma 8.1
.

Let
A\in Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,
B\in Sp_{2m}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, and suppose that
A
is an upper-triangular (or lower-triangular) block matrix. Let
A\triangle B\in Sp_{2n+2m}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be their triangular join. Then,

\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A\triangle B})=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(\mathrm{char}(B)\cdot\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A}),\mathrm{char}(A)\cdot\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{B})).

Proof.

Write

A=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A_{1}&A_{3}\\
0&A_{2}\end{array}\right),A\triangle B=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}A_{1}&0&A_{3}\\
0&B&0\\
0&0&A_{2}\end{array}\right).

We note that

\mathrm{Adj}(x-A\triangle B)=\mathrm{char}(A)\mathrm{char}(B)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}x-A_{1}&0&-A_{3}\\
0&x-B&0\\
0&0&x-A_{2}\end{array}\right)^{-1}.

A simple calculation shows now that

\mathrm{Adj}(x-A\triangle B)=\mathrm{char}(A)\mathrm{char}(B)\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}(x-A_{1})^{-1}&0&(x-A_{1})^{-1}A_{3}(x-A_{2})^{-1}\\
0&(x-B)^{-1}&0\\
0&0&(x-A_{2})^{-1}\end{array}\right).

Now let
C\in\mathfrak{sp}_{2n+2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. We can write
C
as a
3\times 3
block matrix, and note that whenever a block in
\mathrm{Adj}(x-A\triangle B)
is zero, the corresponding block in
C
does not contribute to
\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A\triangle B)C)
, thus we may assume for simplicity that it is zero. Thus, we may write

C=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}C_{1}&0&C_{3}\\
0&C_{B}&0\\
0&0&C_{2}\end{array}\right).

By the structure of
\mathfrak{sp}_{2n+2m}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, we get that
C_{B}\in\mathfrak{sp}_{2m}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,
C_{A}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}C_{1}&C_{3}\\
0&C_{2}\end{array}\right)\in\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Again, a simple calculation shows that

\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A\triangle B)C)=\mathrm{char}(B)\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A)C_{A})+\mathrm{char}(A)\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-B)C_{B}),

thus proving the claim.
∎

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
A_{0}\in Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,
A_{1}\in\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Then
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}(A_{1})
is a
2n
-palindromic polynomial of degree
<2n
.

Proof.

Indeed, lift
A_{0}
to a matrix in
Sp_{2n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{2}\mathcal{O})
, denoted also
A_{0}
by a small abuse of notation. Writing
A=A_{0}+\pi A_{0}A_{1}
we have that
f=\mathrm{char}(A)
is self-reciprocal. Write
f=\mathrm{char}(A_{0})+\pi\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}(A_{1})
. Now,

f(x)=x^{2n}f(1/x)=x^{2n}\mathrm{char}(A_{0})(1/x)+\pi x^{2n}\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}(A_{1})(1/x).

Since
\mathrm{char}(A_{0})
is itself self-reciprocal, we get that
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}(A_{1})
is
2n
-palindromic.
∎

Theorem 8.3
.

Let
A_{0}\in Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be a symplectic matrix. Then,

\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}})=\left\{a:a=\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}b,b\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<\deg\min\overline{A}_{0}}^{\mathrm{pal}}\right\}.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
\mathrm{char}(A_{0})
splits completely (otherwise we move to work in an algebraic extension
\mathbb{F}_{q^{l}}
, as in the proof of Theorem
6.2
). Now using Lemma
8.1
we may reduce to the case of a matrix which is of Type I, II or III as defined in Section
4.3.2
(the proof for this reduction being essentially the same as the argument given in the proof of Theorem
6.2
).

Type I.

We want to compute
\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{\alpha,\alpha^{-1}}^{m}})
. Let
A_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A&B\\
C&D\end{array}\right)\in\mathfrak{sp}_{2m}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, so that
B,C
are symmetric and
A=-D^{t}
. Using Lemma
4.24
, we see that

\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{\alpha,\alpha^{-1}}^{m}}(A_{1})=\mathrm{tr}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(x-\alpha)^{m}Y_{\alpha}^{m}&\\
&(x-\alpha^{-1})^{m}(X_{\alpha}^{m})^{t}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A&B\\
C&D\end{array}\right)=\\
=\mathrm{tr}(A(x-\alpha)^{m}Y_{\alpha}^{m})+\mathrm{tr}(D(x-\alpha^{-1})^{m}(X_{\alpha}^{m})^{t})=\mathrm{tr}\left(A((x-\alpha)^{m}Y_{\alpha}^{m}-(x-\alpha^{-1})^{m}X_{\alpha}^{m})\right).

Since
A
is arbitrary, we get that

\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{\alpha,\alpha^{-1}}^{m}})=(x-\alpha)^{m}(x-\alpha^{-1})^{m}\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}\bigg(\frac{\alpha}{1-x\alpha}+\frac{1}{x-\alpha},\frac{1}{(1-x\alpha)^{2}}+\frac{1}{(x-\alpha)^{2}},\\
\frac{x}{(1-x\alpha)^{3}}+\frac{1}{(x-\alpha)^{3}},\cdots,\frac{x^{m-2}}{(1-x\alpha)^{m}}+\frac{1}{(x-\alpha)^{m}}\bigg).

We claim that this is simply
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<2m}^{\mathrm{pal}}
. Note that by Proposition
8.2
, the image must be contained in this set. On the other hand, the
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-dimension of
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<2m}^{\mathrm{pal}}
is
m
, thus it is enough to exhibit
m
linearly independent functions in the image. But each element in the spanning set above vanishes to a different order at
\alpha
, thus we prove the claim for Type I matrices.

Type II.
As in the previous case we get that

\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{\alpha,\pm}^{m}})=(x-\alpha)^{2m}\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}\Bigg(\frac{\alpha}{1-x\alpha}+\frac{1}{x-\alpha},\frac{1}{(1-x\alpha)^{2}}+\frac{1}{(x-\alpha)^{2}},\\
\frac{x}{(1-x\alpha)^{3}}+\frac{1}{(x-\alpha)^{3}},\cdots,\frac{x^{m-2}}{(1-x\alpha)^{m}}+\frac{1}{(x-\alpha)^{m}}\Bigg).

By Proposition
8.2
, all polynomials in the image are
2m
-palindromic. However, since
\alpha=\alpha^{-1}
in this case, every polynomial in the image is divisible by
(x-\alpha)^{m}
. The space of polynomials in
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<2m}^{\mathrm{pal}}
which are divisible by
(x-\alpha)^{m}
is simply
(x-\alpha)^{m}\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<m}^{\mathrm{pal}}
, and since
m
is odd, its dimension is
\frac{m-1}{2}
. Hence to show the result we need to find
\frac{m-1}{2}
linearly independent elements in the image. Now note that for
i=1,\cdots,\frac{m-1}{2}
,

(x-\alpha)^{2m}\left(\frac{x^{2i-2}}{(1-x\alpha)^{2i}}+\frac{1}{(x-\alpha)^{2i}}\right)=(x-\alpha)^{2m-2i}(1-\alpha x^{2i-2})

is of order
2m-2i
at
\alpha
(as
1-\alpha x^{2i-2}
does not vanish at
\alpha
), thus we proved the result for Type II matrices.

Type III.
Let
A_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A&B\\
C&D\end{array}\right)\in\mathfrak{sp}_{2m}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, so that
B,C
are symmetric and
A=-D^{t}
. Using Lemma
4.24
, we see that

\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{\alpha,\pm}^{2m}}(A_{1})=\mathrm{tr}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(x-\alpha)^{m}Y_{\alpha}^{m}&0\\
Z_{\alpha}^{2m}&(x-\alpha)^{m}(X_{\alpha}^{m})^{t}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A&B\\
C&D\end{array}\right)=\\
=\mathrm{tr}((x-\alpha)^{m}Y_{\alpha}^{m}A)+\mathrm{tr}(BZ_{\alpha}^{2m})+\mathrm{tr}((x-\alpha)^{m}(X_{\alpha}^{m})^{t}D)=\\
=\mathrm{tr}\left(A((x-\alpha)^{m}Y_{\alpha}^{m}-(x-\alpha^{-1})^{m}X_{\alpha}^{m})\right)+\mathrm{tr}(BZ_{\alpha}^{2m}).

Since
A
is arbitrary and
B
is arbitrary symmetric, the image is simply the
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-span of the polynomials

(x-\alpha)^{2m}\left(\frac{\alpha}{1-x\alpha}+\frac{1}{x-\alpha},\frac{1}{(1-x\alpha)^{2}}+\frac{1}{(x-\alpha)^{2}},\frac{x}{(1-x\alpha)^{3}}+\frac{1}{(x-\alpha)^{3}},\cdots,\frac{x^{m-2}}{(1-x\alpha)^{m}}+\frac{1}{(x-\alpha)^{m}}\right),\\
(x-\alpha)^{2m}\left(\frac{(-1)^{r-1}\alpha^{s}f_{r}}{(x-\alpha)^{s}}+\frac{(-1)^{s-1}\alpha^{r}f_{s}}{(x-\alpha)^{r}}\right)_{r,s=1}^{m}.

Here,
f_{i}
are defined as in Proposition
4.23
. We claim that the span is simply
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<2m}^{\mathrm{pal}}
. To prove the claim we need to find
m
linearly independent functions in the image. We note that
(x-\alpha)^{2m}\frac{(-1)^{j-1}\alpha^{j}f_{j}}{(x-\alpha)^{j}}
(that is, choosing
r=s=j
) vanishes to order
2m-2j
at
1
, so we finish the proof.
∎

8.2
Distribution of the characteristic polynomial

In computing the distribution and discrepancies of the characteristic polynomial, we follow the same steps as in Section
6.3
. For the rest of Section
8
,
A
will be a matrix from
Sp_{2n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
, unless explicitly stated otherwise.

8.2.1
One step from
\pi^{k-1}
to
\pi^{k}

Let
A\in Sp_{2n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
be written as
A=A_{0}+\pi^{k-1}A_{0}A_{1}
, where
A_{0}\in Sp_{2n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
is a representative for
A\pmod{\pi^{k-1}}
. When we fix
A_{0}
, the possibilities for
\mathrm{char}(A)
ranges over
\mathrm{char}(A_{0})+\pi^{k-1}\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}})
. Recall that from Theorem
8.3
,

\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}})=\left\{a\Bigg|a=\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}b,b\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}^{\mathrm{pal}}\right\}.

Since
\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}
is palindromic, the condition on
b
being palindromic is equivalent to
a
being palindromic.

Definition 8.4
.

For
B\in Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, define
\delta(B)=\lceil\frac{\deg\min B-1}{2}\rceil
. Slightly more generally, for
r\in\mathbb{N}
we let
\delta(r)=\lceil\frac{r-1}{2}\rceil
.

We use the structure of
\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}})
to prove the following lemma, on the distribution of
\mathrm{char}A
given
A_{0}
.

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
be a monic polynomial of degree
2n
. Then for
d<n
,

\Pr[\mathrm{char}A\in I(g,2n-d)|\mathrm{char}(\rho_{k-1}(A))\in I(\rho_{k-1}(g),2n-d),\delta(\deg\min\overline{A})\geq d]=q^{-d}.

Proof.

We show that for any possible choice of
A_{0}
with
\delta(\deg\min\overline{A_{0}})\geq d
, the conditional probability is
q^{-d}
, from which the result follows.

We are given that
\mathrm{char}(\rho_{k-1}(A_{0}))\in I(\rho_{k-1}(g),2n-d)
, hence
\mathrm{char}(A_{0})-g=e_{0}(x)+\pi^{k}e_{1}(x)
. Here
\deg e_{0}(x)<2n-d
by assumption, and
\deg e_{1}(x)<2n
since
g,\mathrm{char}(A_{0})
are both monic. Thus to have
\mathrm{char}(A)\in I(g,2n-d)
we actually need to have

\deg(\partial\mathrm{char}_{\overline{A_{0}}}(A_{1})-\overline{e_{1}})<2n-d.

We assume, WLOG, that
\overline{e_{1}}(0)=0
(in reality
\overline{e_{1}}(0)
might be non-zero, but the interval defined later will not be different, so we can assume that). Since
\deg(\partial\mathrm{char}_{\overline{A_{0}}}(A_{1})),\deg(\overline{e_{1}})<2n
the last condition is equivalent to

x^{2n}+\partial\mathrm{char}_{\overline{A_{0}}}(A_{1})\in I(x^{2n}+\overline{e_{1}},2n-d).

Since
x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}
is monic of degree
2n
, this is equivalent to

x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}+\partial\mathrm{char}_{\overline{A_{0}}}(A_{1})\in I\left(x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}+\overline{e_{1}},2n-d\right).

For
r,s
polynomials in
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<2n}
, let us denote by
1_{r,s}
the indicator that

x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}+r\in I\left(x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}+s,2n-d\right).

Note that
\dim\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{\overline{A}_{0}}(A_{1}))=\delta(A_{0})=\delta
. Here, by assumption,
\delta\geq d
. We compute the probability in question using Hayes characters:

\Pr_{A_{1}\in\mathfrak{sp}_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}[\partial\mathrm{char}_{\overline{A_{0}}}(A_{1})\in I(\overline{e_{1}},2n-d)]=q^{-\delta}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]\\
\deg(b)<\deg\min\overline{A}_{0}\\
b\text{ is palindromic}\end{subarray}}1_{\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}b,\overline{e_{1}}}=\\
=q^{-\delta}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]\\
\deg(b)<\deg\min\overline{A}_{0}\\
b\text{ is palindromic}\end{subarray}}q^{-d}\sum_{\chi\in\widehat{R}_{d,1,\pi}}\chi\left(x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}+\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A}_{0}}{\min\overline{A}_{0}}b\right)\overline{\chi}\left(x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}+\overline{e_{1}}\right)=\\
=q^{-d}\sum_{\chi\in\widehat{R}_{d,1,\pi}}\chi\left(\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A}_{0}}{\min\overline{A}_{0}}\right)\overline{\chi}\left(x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}+\overline{e_{1}}\right)q^{-\delta}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]\\
\deg(b)<\deg\min\overline{A}_{0}\\
b\text{ is palindromic}\end{subarray}}\chi(x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}+b).

Using Lemma
3.19
we get that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]\\
\deg(b)<\deg\min\overline{A}_{0}\\
b\text{ is palindromic}\end{subarray}}\chi(x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}+b)=q^{\delta}1_{\chi^{\prime}=\chi_{0}}.

Substituting this into the previous equation we get the result.
∎

8.2.2
Distribution mod
\pi^{k}
given mod
\pi
behavior

Let
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
be a monic polynomial of degree
2n
. Define the discrepancy of the conditional distribution given a matrix
B\in Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
such that
\mathrm{char}(B)\in I(\overline{g},2n-d)
to be

\Delta_{B,k}^{g}:=\Pr[\mathrm{char}(A)\in I(g,2n-d)|\overline{A}=B]-q^{-d(k-1)}.

Define
\delta
as in Definition
8.4
. For
\delta(B)>d
the discrepancy is zero.

Proposition 8.6
.

Let
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
be a monic polynomial of degree
2n
,
d<n
. For every matrix
B\in Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
with
\mathrm{char}(B)\in I(\overline{g},2n-d)
and
\delta(B)>d
, we have
\Delta_{B,k}^{g}=0
.

Proof.

We write

\Pr[\mathrm{char}(A)\in I(g,2n-d)|\overline{A}=B]=\\
=\prod_{i=2}^{k}\Pr[\mathrm{char}(\rho_{i}(A))\in I(\rho_{i}(g),2n-d)|\mathrm{char}(\rho_{i-1}(A))\in I(\rho_{i-1}(g),2n-d),\overline{A}=B],

hence using Lemma
8.5
we get that indeed
\Delta_{B,k}^{g}=0
.
∎

Now we consider how
\mathrm{char}(A)
behaves for lifts
A\in Sp_{2n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
of
B
, when
\delta(B)<d
.

Proposition 8.7
.

Let
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
be a monic polynomial of degree
2n
. Let
B\in Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be a matrix with
\delta(B)=l<d
. Let
\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k}=\{\mathrm{char}(A):A\in Sp_{2n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}),\overline{A}=B\}
. Then,

1.

|\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k}|=q^{(k-1)l}
. Moreover, each element of
\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k}
has different
l
next-to-leading coefficients modulo
\pi^{k}
.

2.

\Pr[\mathrm{char}(A)\in I(g,2n-d)|\overline{A}=B]=1_{\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k}\cap I(g,2n-d)\neq\emptyset}q^{-(k-1)l}
. Equivalently, we have

\Delta_{B,k}^{g}=1_{\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k}\cap I(g,2n-d)\neq\emptyset}q^{-(k-1)l}-q^{-(k-1)d}.

Proof.

We prove the claim by induction on
k
. For
k=1
this is trivial. Suppose that we proved the claim for
k\geq 1
. We note that the fibers of the map
\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k}\to\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k-1}
are precisely of the form
f_{0}+\pi^{k-1}\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{B})
. We note that by Theorem
8.3
, this set is of size
q^{l}
. Finally, we note that by Lemma
6.6
with
d=l
, each element has different
l
next-to-leading coefficients mod
\pi^{k}
, hence the first assertion follows. As for the second assertion, it follows from the previous Proposition when we take
d=l
, together with the first assertion.
∎

We now let
\mathcal{G}
be a set of representatives for the lifts of
\mathrm{char}(B)
to a monic polynomial
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
of degree
2n
, modulo equality in the
d
next-to-leading coefficients. We note that
|\mathcal{G}|=q^{(k-1)d}
.

Proposition 8.8
.

Let
B\in Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Then,

1.

If
\delta(B)\geq d
, we have
\sum_{g\in G}|\Delta_{B,k}^{g}|=0
.

2.

If
\delta(B)=l<d
, we have
\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{B,k}^{g}|\leq 2
.

Proof.

The first claim follows directly from Proposition
8.6
. The second follows from the triangle inequality, together with the two parts of Proposition
8.7
.
∎

Let
\overline{f}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=2n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
be a polynomial s.t.
\overline{f}\in I(\overline{g},2n-d)
. We define the discrepancy starting from
\overline{f}
by

\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}=\Pr[\mathrm{char}(A)\in I(g,2n-d)|\mathrm{char}(\overline{A})=\overline{f}]-q^{-d(k-1)}.

Lemma 8.9
.

Let
\overline{f}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=2n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
be a polynomial, and let
\mathcal{G}
be a set of representatives for the possible lifts of
\overline{f}
to a monic polynomial in
(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
of degree
2n
, modulo equality in the
d
next-to-leading coefficients.

1.

If
\delta(\deg\mathrm{rad}\overline{f})\geq d
, then
\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}|=0
.

2.

If
\delta(\deg\mathrm{rad}\overline{f})=s<d
, then
\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}|\leq 2\Pr_{B\in Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}[\delta(B)<d|\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f}]
.

Proof.

We have

\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\in Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\\
\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f}\end{subarray}}\Delta_{B,k}^{g}\cdot\Pr[\overline{A}=B|\mathrm{char}(\overline{A})=\overline{f}].

By Proposition
8.6
we conclude that

\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}B\in Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\\
\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f},\delta(B)<d\end{subarray}}\Delta_{B,k}^{g}\cdot\Pr[\overline{A}=B|\mathrm{char}(\overline{A})=\overline{f}]

Now using the triangle inequality and Proposition
8.8
we get the results.
∎

8.2.3
Discrepancy bound on distribution in intervals

Similarly to the
GL_{n}
case, we define for a monic polynomial
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
of degree
2n
the discrepancy of the surrounding interval,

\Delta_{g}=\Pr[\mathrm{char}A\in I(g,2n-d)]-q^{-kd}.

Lemma 8.10
.

Let
d<n
, and let
\mathcal{G}
be a set of representatives for the monic polynomials
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
of degree
2n
, modulo equivalence in the
d
next-to-leading coefficients. Then,

\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{g}|=O\left(q^{d-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}+\frac{n}{2}}(1+\frac{1}{q^{2}-1})^{n}\binom{n+2d-1}{n}+q^{2d-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}+o(n)}\right).

Proof.

Write

\Pr[\mathrm{char}A\in I(g,2n-d)]=\sum_{\overline{f}\in I(\overline{g},2n-d)}\Pr[\mathrm{char}A\in I(g,2n-d)|\mathrm{char}\overline{A}=\overline{f}]\Pr[\mathrm{char}\overline{A}=\overline{f}]=\\
=\sum_{\overline{f}\in I(\overline{g},2n-d)}\left[q^{-(k-1)d}+\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}\right]\Pr[\mathrm{char}\overline{A}=\overline{f}]=\\
=q^{-(k-1)d}\Pr[\mathrm{char}\overline{A}\in I(\overline{g},2n-d)]+\sum_{\overline{f}\in I(\overline{g},2n-d)}\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}\cdot\Pr[\mathrm{char}\overline{A}=\overline{f}].

By
[
12
, Theorem 6.21]
,

\Pr[\mathrm{char}\overline{A}\in I(\overline{g},2n-d)]=q^{-d}+O\left(q^{-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}+\frac{n}{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q^{2}-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+2d-1}{n}\right).

Hence,

|\Delta_{g}|<O\left(q^{-(k-1)d-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}+\frac{n}{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q^{2}-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+2d-1}{n}\right)+\sum_{\overline{f}\in I(\overline{g},2n-d)}|\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}|\cdot\Pr[\mathrm{char}\overline{A}=\overline{f}]

Summing the last equation over all
g\in\mathcal{G}
, and using Lemma
8.9
, we get that

\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{g}|<O\left(q^{d-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}+\frac{n}{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q^{2}-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+2d-1}{n}\right)+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\overline{f}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]\\
\delta(\deg\mathrm{rad}\overline{f})<d\end{subarray}}2\Pr_{B\in Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}[\delta(B)<d|\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f}]\Pr[\mathrm{char}B=\overline{f}]

Note that if
\delta(B)<d
then
\deg\min(B)<2d
. Now by Theorem
5.8
,

\Pr_{B\in Sp_{2n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}[\delta(B)<d|\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f}]\Pr[\mathrm{char}B=\overline{f}]<\Pr[\deg\min(B)<2d\cap\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f}]<q^{-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}+o(n)},

and by Lemma
3.3
, the number of polynomials in
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=2n}
with a radical of degree
<2d
is
q^{2d+o(n)}
, hence we get the result.
∎

8.3
Joint distribution of traces

As in the
GL_{n}
case, the total variation distance of
\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{Sp}
from
\mathrm{UTR}_{d}
may be computed as the limit of the total variation distance of the
\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}
variables

\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{Sp,k}
,
\mathrm{UTR}_{d}^{k}
. We recall that by the discussion of
[
12
, Theorem 6.21]
, There is a constant
c_{q}^{Sp}
such that for
d<c_{q}^{Sp}\cdot n
,

q^{d-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}+\frac{n}{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q^{2}-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+2d-1}{n}=_{n\to\infty}o(1).

Theorem 8.11
.

Let
d<n
, and let
\{a_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq d}
be a trace datum. Let
\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{Sp,k}
be the random variable attaching to a matrix
M\in Sp_{2n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
its trace datum of length
d
, and let
\mathrm{UTR}_{d}^{k}
be the uniform measure on trace data, as defined in Section
3.2
. Then,

d_{TV}(\mu_{\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{Sp,k}},\mu_{\mathrm{UTR}_{d}^{k}})=\sum_{\{a_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq d}\text{ trace datum}}\Bigg|\Pr_{M\in Sp_{2n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})}[M\text{ has trace data }\{a_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq d}]-q^{-(kd-S(d,k))}\Bigg|<\\
<O\left(q^{d-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}+\frac{n}{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q^{2}-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+2d-1}{n}+q^{2d-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}+o(n)}\right).

Proof.

We follow closely the proof of Theorem
6.12
. Lemma
3.10
gives that there are polynomials
\{g_{j}\}_{j=1}^{q^{S}}\subset\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}[x]
, such that

\Pr_{M\in Sp_{2n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})}[M\text{ has trace data }\{a_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq d}]=\sum_{i=1}^{q^{S}}\Pr_{M\in Sp_{2n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})}[M\in I(g_{j},n-d-1)]=q^{S-kd}+\sum_{i=1}^{q^{S}}\Delta_{g_{j}}.

Denote
\mathcal{G}=\{g_{j}\}_{j=1}^{q^{S}}
.
\mathcal{G}
is a set of representatives for the monic polynomials modulo equality in the
d
next-to-leading coefficients (that is, a set of representatives for the Hayes equivalence relation
R_{1,d}
). Using the triangle inequality we get that

\sum_{\{a_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq d}\text{ trace data}}\Bigg|\Pr_{M\in Sp_{2n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})}[M\text{ has trace data }\{a_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq d}]-q^{-(kd-S(d,k))}\Bigg|<\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{g}|.

Now the theorem follows from Lemma
8.10
.
∎

By taking
k\to\infty
we get Theorem
1.5
as a consequence (using Lemma
2.10
). We restate it here for convenience.

Theorem 8.12
.

Let
d<n
, and let
\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{Sp}
be the random variable attaching to a matrix
M\in Sp_{2n}(\mathcal{O})
its trace datum of length
d
. Let
\mathrm{UTR}_{d}
be the random variable
(a_{i})_{i=1}^{d}
, such that
a_{i}\sim\frac{u_{i}}{|i|_{p}}
, where
u_{i}\sim\mathcal{O}
is sampled uniformly at random w.r.t. the Haar measure. Then,

d_{TV}(\mu_{\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{Sp}},\mu_{\mathrm{UTR}_{d}})=O\left(q^{d-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}+\frac{n}{2}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q^{2}-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+2d-1}{n}+q^{2d-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}+o(n)}\right).

In particular, for
d<c_{q}^{Sp}\cdot n
, this total variation distance is
o(1)
as
n\to\infty
.

We get as a result

Theorem 8.13
.

Fix
d>0
, and let
B_{1},\ldots,B_{D}
be balls in
\mathcal{O}
. Let
Z_{i}\sim\frac{1}{|i|_{p}}U_{i}
, where
U_{i}
are independent uniform random variables on
\mathcal{O}
. Then,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\Pr_{M\in Sp_{2n}(\mathcal{O})}\left[\left(\mathrm{tr}(M^{i})-1_{p|i}\sigma(\mathrm{tr}(M^{i/p}))\right)_{i=1}^{d}\right]=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\Pr[Z_{i}\in B_{i}].

9
Traces and characteristic polynomial for
SO_{n}

For the rest of the section,
A
would be a matrix in
SO_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
unless explicitly stated otherwise.

9.1
The image of
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}

For the rest of the section, fix
\epsilon=\pm 1
and work in
SO_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. As we have done for the previous groups, we want to show that for any
A_{0}\in SO_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, the map
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}:\mathfrak{so}_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\to\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<n}
behaves well under the orthogonal join. We first work with
O_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
for convenience.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
A\in O_{m}^{\pm}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,
B\in O_{n}^{\pm}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Then,

\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A\oplus_{\mathrm{orth}}B})=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(\mathrm{char}(B)\cdot\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A}),\mathrm{char}(A)\cdot\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{B})),

where here
\oplus_{\mathrm{orth}}
is the orthogonal join, as defined in Section
4.1.3
.

Proof.

Let
J_{A}
,
J_{B}
,
J_{A\oplus_{\mathrm{orth}}B}
be the forms preserved by
A
,
B
and
A\oplus_{\mathrm{orth}}B
(respectively) and let
X
be the matrix defined in equation (
4.2
). Also let
Y\in\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{A\oplus_{\mathrm{orth}}B})
. Then, using Claim
4.16
, setting
Y^{\prime}=X^{-1}YX
, we have
Y^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{F}_{q},\mathrm{diag}(J_{A},J_{B}))
. Moreover,

\partial\mathrm{char}_{A\oplus_{\mathrm{orth}}B}(Y)=\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A\oplus_{\mathrm{orth}}B)Y)=\mathrm{tr}(X^{-1}\mathrm{Adj}(x-\mathrm{diag}(A,B))X\cdot Y^{\prime}).

A simple calculation shows that
\mathrm{Adj}(x-\mathrm{diag}(A,B))=\mathrm{diag}(\mathrm{char}(B)\mathrm{Adj}(x-A),\mathrm{char}(A)\mathrm{Adj}(x-B))
. Thus, using the fact that the trace is invariant under conjugation, we get

\partial\mathrm{char}_{A\oplus_{\mathrm{orth}}B}(Y)=\mathrm{tr}\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{char}(B)\mathrm{Adj}(x-A)&0\\
0&\mathrm{char}(A)\mathrm{Adj}(x-B)\end{array}\right)Y^{\prime}\right).

Write
Y^{\prime}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}Y^{\prime}_{11}&Y^{\prime}_{12}\\
Y^{\prime}_{21}&Y^{\prime}_{22}\end{array}\right)
. Note that without loss of generality, we may assume
Y^{\prime}_{12},Y^{\prime}_{21}=0
, since they do not contribute to the result. Now,
For
Y^{\prime}
to be in
\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{F}_{q},\mathrm{diag}(J_{A},J_{B}))
we simply need to have
Y^{\prime}_{11}\in\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{A})
,
Y^{\prime}_{22}\in\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{B})
, hence the result.
∎

The ideas in the previous Lemma can be used to prove the following

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
A\in O(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{A})
, and let
J_{B}
be a matrix of a form congruent to the form defined by
J_{A}
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Let
X
be the matrix such that
J_{B}=X^{t}J_{A}X
, and define
B=X^{-1}AX
. Then,

\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A})=\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{B}).

Proof.

We claim that
\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{B})=X^{-1}\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{A})X
. Indeed, if
Y\in\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{A})
then

J_{B}(X^{-1}YX)=X^{t}J_{A}YX=-X^{t}Y^{t}J_{A}X=-(X^{-1}YX)^{t}(X^{t}J_{A}X)=-(X^{-1}YX)^{t}J_{B},

hence
X^{-1}\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{A})X\subset\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{B})
, and comparing
\mathbb{F}_{q}
-dimensions we get equality. Now, let
Y\in\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{A})
. Then,

\partial\mathrm{char}_{A}(Y)=\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A)Y)=\mathrm{tr}(X^{-1}\mathrm{Adj}(x-B)Y^{\prime}X)=\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-B)Y^{\prime}),

where
B^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{o}(\mathbb{F}_{q},J_{B})
. Hence we get the result.
∎

The last lemma will be useful for us since it allows us to work only with Witt types
\langle 0\rangle
,
\langle 1\rangle
. Indeed, by passing to an extension of even degree
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2l}}/\mathbb{F}_{q}
the Witt type
\langle 1,\delta\rangle
becomes congruent to
\langle 0\rangle
and
\langle\delta\rangle
becomes congruent to
\langle 1\rangle
.

Theorem 9.3
.

Let
A\in SO_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Then,

\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A})=\left\{\frac{\mathrm{char}A}{\min A}a:\deg A<\deg\min A,a\text{ is palindromic}\right\}.

Proof.

By the usual argument, using Lemma
9.1
it is enough to treat the matrices of types I, II, and III as defined in Section
4.3.3
. We will only treat the matrices of types II and III for Witt types
\langle 0\rangle
,
\langle 1\rangle
as discussed above, reducing the other Witt types to those cases via Lemma
9.2
, by passing to a field extension of even dimension
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2l}}
in which the characteristic polynomial splits.

Type I.
Let
A_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A&B\\
C&D\end{array}\right)\in\mathfrak{so}_{n}^{\epsilon}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, so that
A=-D^{\mathfrak{t}}
, and
B
,
C
are
\mathfrak{t}
anti-symmetric. Using Lemma
4.26
, we get that

\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{\alpha,\alpha^{-1}}^{m}}(A_{1})=\mathrm{tr}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(x-\alpha^{-1})^{m}X_{\alpha}^{m}&\\
&(x-\alpha)^{m}(Y_{\alpha}^{m})^{\mathfrak{t}}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A&B\\
C&D\end{array}\right)=\\
=\mathrm{tr}(A(x-\alpha^{-1})^{m}X_{\alpha}^{m})+\mathrm{tr}(D(x-\alpha)^{m}(Y_{\alpha}^{m})^{\mathfrak{t}})=\mathrm{tr}(A(x-\alpha^{-1})^{m}X_{\alpha}^{m})+\mathrm{tr}(-A^{\mathfrak{t}}(x-\alpha)^{m}(Y_{\alpha}^{m})^{\mathfrak{t}})=\\
=(x-\alpha^{-1})^{m}\mathrm{tr}(AX_{\alpha}^{m})-(x-\alpha)^{m}\mathrm{tr}(AY_{\alpha}^{m})=\mathrm{tr}(A[(x-\alpha^{-1})^{m}X_{\alpha}^{m}-(x-\alpha)^{m}Y_{\alpha}^{m}]).

From here the result follows from the Type I case of the symplectic group, proved in Theorem
8.3
.

Type II.
This follows the same lines as the previous case, reducing to the Type II case of the symplectic group.

Type III.

The image is contained in
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<2m+1}^{\mathrm{pal}}
, a linear space of dimension
m
. To show equality, it is enough to find
m
linearly independent functions. Let
1\leq i,j\leq m
and write

X^{ij}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0&&X_{ij}\\
&0&\\
&&0\end{array}\right)\in\mathfrak{o}^{+}(\mathbb{F}_{q}),(X_{ij})^{\mathfrak{t}}=-X_{ij}.

More specifically we take
X_{ij}
as the matrix whose
i,j
entry is
1
and whose
m+1-j,m+1-i
entry is
-1
, and is zero elsewhere. Then we get (using the definition of
g_{j}
from Proposition
4.25
)

\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{\alpha}}(X^{ij})=(x-\alpha)^{2m+1}\left(\frac{(-1)^{m+1}}{2}\alpha-\frac{1}{x-\alpha}\right)\left((-1)^{j-1}g_{j}(x)(x-\alpha)^{-m-1+i}-(-1)^{m-i}g_{m+1-i}(x)(x-\alpha)^{-j}\right).

If
m>i,j>1
then we can compute the order at
\alpha
of
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{\alpha}}(X^{ij})
by computing the order of

\left(\frac{(-1)^{m+1}}{2}\alpha+\frac{1}{x-\alpha}\right)\left((-1)^{j-1}\frac{(-1)^{j-1}x^{j-2}}{(1-x\alpha)^{j}(x-\alpha)^{m+1-i}}-(-1)^{m-i}\frac{(-1)^{m-i}x^{m-1-i}}{(1-x\alpha)^{m+1-i}(x-\alpha)^{j}}\right)=\\
\left(\frac{(-1)^{m}}{2}\alpha-\frac{1}{x-\alpha}\right)\left(\frac{(-\alpha)^{j}x^{j-2}-(-\alpha)^{m+1-i}x^{m-1-i}}{(x-\alpha)^{m+1-i+j}}\right).

We note that since

(-\alpha)^{j}\alpha^{j-2}-(-\alpha)^{m+1-i}\alpha^{m-1-i}=(-1)^{j}-(-1)^{m+1-i},

if
m+1-i\neq j\pmod{2}
, the order of the pole this function at
\alpha
is
m+2-i+j
. Thus the order of vanishing of
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{\alpha}}(X^{ij})
at
\alpha
is
m-1+i-j
. This way we cover every odd order
1\leq j\leq 2m-4
.

If we take
A=0
,
B=B_{j}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccc}0_{m\times m}&u_{j}&0_{m\times m}\\
0_{1\times m}&0&v_{j}\\
0_{m\times m}&0_{m\times 1}&0_{m\times m}\end{array}\right)
, where
u_{j}
is the
m\times 1
vector with
1
in the
j
-th coordinate and
v_{j}
is the
1\times m
vector with
-1
in the
j
-th coordinate, we get that the following function is in the image:

(x-\alpha)^{2m+1}\left(\frac{(-1)^{j-1}g_{j}}{x-\alpha}-\frac{x^{j}}{(1-x\alpha)^{j+1}}\right).

Similarly to the previous computation, we can get that if
j
is odd then its order at
\alpha
is
2m-j
. This way we cover all possible odd order
1\leq j\leq 2m-1
so that the claim follows.
∎

9.2
Distribution of the characteristic polynomial

We follow the usual steps.

9.2.1
One step from
\pi^{k-1}
to
\pi^{k}

Let
A\in SO_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
be written as
A=A_{0}+\pi^{k-1}A_{0}A_{1}
, where
A_{0}\in H_{G,k}
is a representative in
SO_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
for
A\pmod{\pi^{k-1}}
,
A_{1}\in\mathfrak{so}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. When we fix
A_{0}
, the possibilities for
\mathrm{char}(A)
range over
\mathrm{char}(A_{0})+\pi^{k-1}\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}})
. Recall that from Theorem
8.3
,

\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}})=\left\{a:a=\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}b,b\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{<\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}^{\mathrm{pal}}\right\}.

Since
\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}
is palindromic, the condition on
a
being palindromic is equivalent to
b
being palindromic. We use this to prove the following lemma, on the distribution of
\mathrm{char}A
given
A_{0}
.

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
be a monic polynomial of degree
n
. Then for
d<n
,

\Pr[\mathrm{char}A\in I(g,n-d)|\mathrm{char}(\rho_{k-1}(A))\in I(\rho_{k-1}(g),n-d),\delta(\deg\min\overline{A})\geq d]=q^{-d}.

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as that of Lemma
8.5
, making adjustments for using claims for the special orthogonal group wherever necessary.
∎

9.2.2
Distribution mod
\pi^{k}
given mod
\pi
behavior

Define the discrepancy of the conditional distribution given a matrix
B\in SO_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
such that
\mathrm{char}(B)\in I(\overline{g},n-d)
to be

\Delta_{B,k}^{g}=\Pr[\mathrm{char}(A)\in I(g,n-d)|\overline{A}=B]-q^{-d(k-1)}.

As in the previous cases, when
\deg\min B
is large enough, the discrepancy is zero.

Proposition 9.5
.

Let
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
be a monic polynomial of degree
n
,
d<\frac{n}{2}
. For every matrix
B\in SO^{\epsilon}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
with
\mathrm{char}(B)\in I(\overline{g},n-d)
and
\delta(\deg\min(B))\geq d
, we have
\Delta_{B,k}^{g}=0
.

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as that of Proposition
6.7
, using Theorem
9.3
.
∎

Now we consider how
\mathrm{char}(A)
behaves for lifts
A
of
B
, when
\delta(B)<d
.

Proposition 9.6
.

Let
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
be a monic polynomial of degree
n
. Let
B\in SO_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be a matrix with
\deg\min B=l<d
. Let
\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k}=\{\mathrm{char}(A):A\in SO_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}),\overline{A}=B\}
. Then,

1.

|\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k}|=q^{(k-1)l}
. Moreover, each element of
\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k}
has different
l
leading coefficients modulo
\pi^{k}
.

2.

\Pr[\mathrm{char}(A)\in I(g,n-d)|\overline{A}=B]=1_{\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k}\cap I(g,n-d)\neq\emptyset}q^{-(k-1)l}
. Equivalently, we have

\Delta_{B,k}^{g}=1_{\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k}\cap I(g,n-d)\neq\emptyset}q^{-(k-1)l}-q^{-(k-1)d}.

Proof.

The proof is omitted as it is essentially the proof of Proposition
6.7
.
∎

We now let
\mathcal{G}
be a set of representatives for the lifts of
\mathrm{char}(B)
to a monic polynomial
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
of degree
n
, modulo equality in the
d
next-to-leading coefficients. We note that
|\mathcal{G}|=q^{(k-1)d}
.

Proposition 9.7
.

Let
B\in SO_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Then,

1.

If
\deg\min(B)\geq d
, we have
\sum_{g\in G}|\Delta_{B,k}^{g}|=0
.

2.

If
\deg\min(B)=l<d
, we have
\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{B,k}^{g}|\leq 2
.

Proof.

The first claim follows directly from Proposition
9.5
. The second follows from the triangle inequality using the two parts of Proposition
9.6
.
∎

Let
\overline{f}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
be a self-reciprocal polynomial such that
\overline{f}\in I(\overline{g},n-d)
. We define the discrepancy of starting from
f
by

\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}=\Pr[\mathrm{char}(A)\in I(g,n-d)|\mathrm{char}(\overline{A})=\overline{f}]-q^{-d(k-1)}.

Lemma 9.8
.

Let
\overline{f}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
be a self-reciprocal polynomial, and let
\mathcal{G}
be a set of representatives for the possible lifts of
\overline{f}
to a monic polynomial in
(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
of degree
n
, modulo equality in the
d
next-to-leading coefficients.

1.

If
\delta(\deg\mathrm{rad}\overline{f})\geq d
, then
\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}|=0
.

2.

If
\delta(\deg\mathrm{rad}\overline{f})=s<d
, then
\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}|\leq 2\Pr_{B\in SO_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}[\delta(\deg\min(B))<d|\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f}]
.

Proof.

The proof is the same as that of Lemma
6.10
, using Propositions
9.5
,
9.6
.
∎

9.2.3
Discrepancy bound on distribution in intervals

As usual we define we define for a monic polynomial
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
of degree
n
the discrepancy of the surrounding interval,

\Delta_{g}=\Pr[\mathrm{char}A\in I(g,n-d)]-q^{-kd}.

Lemma 9.9
.

Let
d<n
, and let
\mathcal{G}
be a set of representatives for the monic polynomials
g\in(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})[x]
of degree
n
, modulo equivalence in the
d
next-to-leading coefficients. Then,

\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{g}|=O\left(q^{d+\frac{n}{4}-\frac{n^{2}}{8d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q^{2}-1}\right)^{n/2}\binom{n/2+2d-3}{n/2}+q^{2d+\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n^{2}}{4d}+o(n)}\right).

Proof.

Let
g\in\mathcal{G}
. As in the proof of Lemma
6.11
, write

\Pr[\mathrm{char}A\in I(g,n-d)]=q^{-(k-1)d}\Pr[\mathrm{char}\overline{A}\in I(\overline{g},n-d)]+\sum_{\overline{f}\in I(\overline{g},n-d)}\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}\cdot\Pr[\mathrm{char}\overline{A}=\overline{f}].

By
[
12
, Theorem 7.9]
we have

\Pr_{B\in O_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}[\mathrm{char}B\in I(\overline{g},n-d)]=q^{-d}+O\left(q^{\frac{n}{4}-\frac{n^{2}}{8d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q^{2}-1}\right)^{n/2}\binom{n/2+2d-3}{n/2}\right).

Since
[O_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q}):SO_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})]=2
we get the same bound for
SO_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, therefore

\Pr[\mathrm{char}\overline{A}\in I(\overline{g},n-d)]=q^{-d}+O\left(q^{\frac{n}{4}-\frac{n^{2}}{8d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q^{2}-1}\right)^{n/2}\binom{n/2+2d-3}{n/2}\right).

Hence,

|\Delta_{g}|<O\left(q^{-(k-1)d+\frac{n}{4}-\frac{n^{2}}{8d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q^{2}-1}\right)^{n/2}\binom{n/2+2d-3}{n/2}\right)+\sum_{\overline{f}\in I(\overline{g},n-d)}\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}\cdot\Pr[\mathrm{char}\overline{A}=\overline{f}].

Summing this over all
g\in\mathcal{G}
, and using Lemma
9.8
, we get that

\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{g}|<O\left(q^{d+\frac{n}{4}-\frac{n^{2}}{8d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q^{2}-1}\right)^{n/2}\binom{n/2+2d-3}{n/2}\right)+\\
+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\overline{f}\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]\\
\delta(\deg\mathrm{rad}\overline{f})<d\end{subarray}}2\Pr_{B\in SO_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}[\delta(\deg\min(B))<d|\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f}]\Pr[\mathrm{char}{B}=\overline{f}].

Now by Theorem
5.12
,

\Pr_{B\in SO_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}[\delta(B)<d|\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f}]\Pr[\mathrm{char}{B}=\overline{f}]<\Pr_{B\in SO_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}[\deg\min(B)<2d\cap\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f}]<q^{\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n^{2}}{4d}+o(n)}.

By Lemma
3.3
, the number of polynomials in
\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
with a radical of degree
<2d
is
q^{2d+o(n)}
, hence we get the result.
∎

9.3
Joint distribution of traces

As in the
GL_{n}
case, the total variation distance of
\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{SO}
from
\mathrm{UTR}_{d}
may be computed as the limit of the total variation distance of the
\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}
random variables

\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{SO,k}
,
\mathrm{UTR}_{d}^{k}
. We recall that by the discussion of
[
12
, Theorem 7.9]
(and using a correction as in the previous section to move from
O_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
to
SO_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
), There is a constant
c_{q}^{SO}
such that for
d<c_{q}^{SO}\cdot n
,

q^{d+\frac{n}{4}-\frac{n^{2}}{8d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q^{2}-1}\right)^{n/2}\binom{n/2+2d-3}{n/2}=o(1).

Theorem 9.10
.

Let
d<\frac{n}{2}
, and let
\{a_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq d}
be a sequence of trace data. Let
\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{SO,k}
be the random variable attaching to a matrix
M\in SO_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
its trace data up to
d
, and let
\mathrm{UTR}_{d}^{k}
be the uniform measure on trace sequences, as defined in Section
3.2
. Then,

d_{TV}(\mu_{\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{SO,k}},\mu_{\mathrm{UTR}_{d}^{k}})=\sum_{\{a_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq d}\text{ trace data}}\Bigg|\Pr_{M\in SO_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})}[M\text{ has trace data }\{a_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq d}]-q^{-(kd-S(d,k))}\Bigg|<\\
<O\left(q^{d+\frac{n}{4}-\frac{n^{2}}{8d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q^{2}-1}\right)^{n/2}\binom{n/2+2d-3}{n/2}+q^{2d+\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n^{2}}{4d}+o(n)}\right).

Proof.

The proof is omitted, as it is essentially the same as the analogous one for
GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
. Note that depending on
q
,
SO_{n}(\mathcal{O})
may reduce to either
SO_{n}^{+}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
or
SO_{n}^{-}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, which is why we treated both groups.
∎

By taking
k\to\infty
and using Lemma
2.10
we get as a consequence Theorem
1.6
. As a corollary we get

Theorem 9.11
.

Fix
d>0
, and let
B_{1},\ldots,B_{d}
be fixed balls in
\mathcal{O}
. Let
Z_{i}\sim\frac{1}{|i|_{p}}U_{i}
, where
U_{i}
are independent uniform random variables on
\mathcal{O}
. Then,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\Pr_{M\in SO_{n}(\mathcal{O})}\left[\left(\mathrm{tr}(M^{i})-1_{p|i}\sigma(\mathrm{tr}(M^{i/p}))\in B_{i}\right)_{i=1}^{d}\right]=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\Pr[Z_{i}\in B_{i}].

10
Traces and characteristic polynomial for
U_{n}

We recall that we fixed an unramified quadratic extension
\mathcal{K}/\mathcal{F}
with a ring of integers
\mathcal{R}
and that the unitary group
U_{n}(\mathcal{O})
is a subgroup of
GL_{n}(\mathcal{R})
.

10.1
The image of
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}

As we saw in Lemma
4.21
, the map
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}:\mathfrak{u}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\to\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]
, defined by
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}(A_{1})=\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{0})A_{0}A_{1})
,
is essential to understanding the distribution of the characteristic polynomial. We now compute its image explicitly.

Lemma 10.1
.

Let
A\in U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
,
B\in U_{m}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Then

\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A\oplus B})=\mathrm{span}_{\mathbb{F}_{q}}(\mathrm{char}(B)\cdot\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A}),\mathrm{char}(A)\cdot\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{B})).

Proof.

Let
C_{1}\in\mathfrak{u}_{n+m}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, and write
C_{1}
as a block matrix

C_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}X&Y\\
Z&W\end{array}\right)
.
We have

\partial\mathrm{char}_{A\oplus B}(C_{1})=\mathrm{tr}\left(\mathrm{Adj}\left(x-\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A&\\
&B\end{array}\right)\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A&\\
&B\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}X&Y\\
Z&W\end{array}\right)\right)=\\
=\mathrm{tr}\left(\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{char}(B)\mathrm{Adj}(x-A)A&\\
&\mathrm{char}(A)\mathrm{Adj}(x-B)B\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}X&Y\\
Z&W\end{array}\right)\right)=\\
=\mathrm{char}(B)\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A)AX)+\mathrm{char}(A)\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-B)BW)=\mathrm{char}(B)\partial\mathrm{char}_{A}(X)+\mathrm{char}(A)\partial\mathrm{char}_{B}(W).

∎

Claim 10.2
.

Let
A_{0}\in U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
and let
A_{1}\in\mathfrak{u}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Let
g_{0}=\mathrm{char}(A_{0})
, and let
j\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}
be an element such that
\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}/\mathbb{F}_{q}}(j)=0
. Define
J_{A_{0}}(x)=J(x)=j(g_{0}-x^{n}+g_{0}(0))
. Then, the image of
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}
is contained in
\mathbb{F}_{q}\cdot J_{A_{0}}(x)\oplus\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<n}^{g_{0}(0)-\mathrm{skpal}}
.

Proof.

To compute the symmetries of the image, we work over
\mathcal{O}/\pi^{2}\mathcal{O}
. Abusing notation slightly we let
A_{0}\in U_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{2}\mathcal{O})
be a lift of
A_{0}
, and write
A=A_{0}+\pi A_{0}A_{1}
. By Lemma
4.21
, we get

\mathrm{char}(A)=\mathrm{char}(A_{0})+\pi\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}(A_{1}).

Let
g_{0}=\mathrm{char}(A_{0})
,
g_{1}=\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}(A_{1})
. We have
\widetilde{\mathrm{char}(A)}=\mathrm{char}(A)
, that is,

\frac{g_{0}^{*}(x)+\pi g_{1}^{*}}{\sigma(g_{0}(0))+\pi\sigma(g_{1}(0))}=g_{0}+\pi g_{1}.

(10.1)

We now focus on the LHS of (
10.1
). Note that
\frac{1}{\sigma(g_{0}(0))+\pi\sigma(g_{1}(0))}=\frac{1}{\sigma(g_{0}(0))}-\pi\frac{\sigma(g_{1})(0)}{\sigma(g_{0})(0)^{2}}
. Hence, LHS of (
10.1
) is

\tilde{g_{0}}+\pi\left(\frac{g_{1}^{*}}{\sigma(g_{0}(0))}-\tilde{g_{0}}\frac{\sigma(g_{1}(0))}{\sigma(g_{0}(0))}\right).

Using the fact that
\frac{g_{0}^{*}}{\sigma(g_{0}(0))}=\tilde{g_{0}}=g_{0}
, we get that the LHS of (
10.1
) is actually

g_{0}+\pi\left(\frac{g_{1}^{*}}{\sigma(g_{0}(0))}-g_{0}\frac{\sigma(g_{1}(0))}{\sigma(g_{0}(0))}\right).

Substituting this back into equation (
10.1
) we get (again by abusing notation and moving to mod
\pi
)

\left(\frac{g_{1}^{*}}{\sigma(g_{0}(0))}-g_{0}\frac{\sigma(g_{1}(0))}{\sigma(g_{0}(0))}\right)=g_{1}.

Here
g_{1}(x)=\mathrm{tr}(A_{1})\mathrm{char}(A_{0})-x\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{0})A_{1})
, hence
g_{1}(0)/g_{0}(0)=\mathrm{tr}(A_{1})
and so
\sigma(g_{1}(0))/\sigma(g_{0}(0))=-\mathrm{tr}(A_{1})
. Note that since
\mathrm{tr}(A_{1})
is an element of
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}
with
\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}/\mathbb{F}_{q}}(\mathrm{tr}(A_{1}))=0
, we can write
\mathrm{tr}(A_{1})=\alpha j
for
\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Write
g_{1}=\alpha jg_{0}+f
. The last equation above becomes

\frac{f^{*}}{\sigma(g_{0}(0))}=-\alpha jg_{0}+\frac{f^{*}}{\sigma(g_{0}(0))}+\alpha jg_{0}=\alpha jg_{0}+f.

Hence, recalling that
\frac{g_{0}^{*}}{\sigma(g_{0})}=g_{0}
, we get that

\frac{(f+\frac{1}{2}\alpha jg_{0})^{*}}{\sigma(g_{0}(0))}=f+\frac{1}{2}\alpha jg_{0}.

That means that
f+\frac{1}{2}\alpha jg_{0}
is
g_{0}(0)
-skew palindromic (w.r.t.
n
). We note that since
g_{1}
has degree
<n
, and
g_{0}
is monic,
f+\frac{1}{2}\alpha jg_{0}
is of degree precisely
n
and has leading coefficient
-\frac{1}{2}\alpha j
. Hence,
g_{1}=h_{\mathrm{temp}}+\frac{1}{2}\alpha jg_{0}
where
h_{\mathrm{temp}}
is in
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{=n}^{g_{0}(0)-\mathrm{skpal}}
has leading coefficient
-\frac{1}{2}\alpha j
. Since
h_{\mathrm{temp}}
is
g_{0}(0)
-skew palindromic, this means that its free coefficient is
\frac{1}{2}\alpha jg_{0}(0)
. Write
h_{\mathrm{temp}}=-\frac{1}{2}\alpha jx^{n}+\frac{1}{2}\alpha jg_{0}(0)+h
, where
h
is in
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]^{g_{0}(0)-\mathrm{skpal}}_{<n}
. Then, we eventually get that
g_{1}=h+\frac{1}{2}\alpha j(g_{0}-x^{n}+g_{0}(0))
. Since
\alpha
is arbitrary, we get the result.
∎

From now on,
A_{0}
(and hence
g_{0}
) are fixed; thus, whenever we speak about a skew palindromic polynomial, it will be
g_{0}(0)
-skew palindromic. Therefore we use the notations
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<n}^{\mathrm{skpal}}
, etc. instead of the more detailed but longer notation. Now we will compute the image of
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}
inside
\mathbb{F}_{q}\cdot J_{A_{0}}(x)\oplus\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<n}^{\mathrm{skpal}}
. We first do this for matrices
A_{0}\in U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\cap GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
and their conjugates (as for the other groups, the image of
\mathcal{L}_{A_{0}}
is invariant under conjugation of
A_{0}
).

Claim 10.3
.

Let
A_{0}\in U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\cap GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be a matrix with
\mathrm{char}(A_{0})=\min(A_{0})
. Then,

\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}})=\mathbb{F}_{q}\cdot J_{A_{0}}(x)\oplus\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<n}^{\mathrm{skpal}}.

Proof.

We note that by definition,
\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}}(A_{1})=\mathrm{tr}(\mathrm{Adj}(x-A_{0})A_{0}A_{1})
. We note that if we put
A_{1}\in M_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, we get the same result for this map as for the map defined for
GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, which was discussed in Theorem
6.2
. There, we proved that the image has dimension
n
. We also note that
M_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is the direct sum of the symmetric matrices and anti-symmetric matrices over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
,
M_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})=\mathrm{sym}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\oplus\mathrm{asym}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Note that if
A_{1}\in\mathrm{asym}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
then
A_{1}\in\mathfrak{u}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Also, if
0\neq j\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}
is such that
\mathrm{tr}_{\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}/\mathbb{F}_{q}}(j)=0
, and
A_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathrm{sym}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, then
A_{1}=jA_{1}^{\prime}\in\mathfrak{u}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. We get that
\dim\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}})\geq n
, hence the result.
∎

Now we prove our main result for this section.

Theorem 10.4
.

Let
A_{0}\in U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Then,
\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}})=\mathbb{F}_{q}\cdot J_{A_{0}}(x)\oplus\frac{\mathrm{char}(A_{0})}{\min(A_{0})}\cdot\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<\deg{\min{A_{0}}}}^{\mathrm{skpal}}
.

Proof.

We first assume that
A_{0}
,
A_{0}^{*}
do not share eigenvalues over the algebraic closure and that
\mathrm{char}(A_{0})=\min(A_{0})
. In this case, let
B_{0}=A_{0}\oplus A_{0}^{*}
. This matrix is conjugate to a matrix in
U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})\cap GL_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, and
\mathrm{char}(B_{0})=\min(B_{0})
. Hence
\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{B_{0}})
is of dimension
2n
. However by Lemma
10.1
,
\dim\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{B_{0}})=\dim\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}})+\dim\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}^{*}})
. It follows that
\dim\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}})=n
, hence the result follows by comparing dimensions.

For the more general case, we proceed by induction as in the proof of Theorem
6.2
, each time removing a different primary part, and using Lemma
10.1
.
∎

10.2
Distribution of the characteristic polynomial

We follow the usual steps. In the rest of the section,
A
will always be a matrix in
U_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
unless explicitly stated otherwise.

10.2.1
One step from
\pi^{k-1}
to
\pi^{k}

Let
A\in U_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
be written as
A=A_{0}+\pi^{k-1}A_{0}A_{1}
, where
A_{0}\in H_{U,k}
is a representative for
A\pmod{\pi^{k-1}}
. When we fix
A_{0}
, the possibilities for
\mathrm{char}(A)
ranges over
\mathrm{char}(A_{0})+\pi^{k-1}\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{\overline{A_{0}}})
. Recall that from Theorem
10.4
,

\mathrm{Im}(\partial\mathrm{char}_{A_{0}})=\mathbb{F}_{q}\cdot J_{A_{0}}(x)\oplus\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}\cdot\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<\deg{\min{A_{0}}}}^{\mathrm{skpal}}.

Lemma 10.5
.

Let
g\in(\mathcal{R}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{R})[x]
be a monic polynomial of degree
n
, and let
\delta=\delta(n)=\lceil\frac{n-1}{2}\rceil
. Then for
d<\frac{n}{2}
,

\Pr[\mathrm{char}A\in I(g,n-d)|\mathrm{char}(\rho_{k-1}(A))\in I(\rho_{k-1}(g),n-d),\delta(\deg\min\overline{A})>d]=q^{-2d}.

Proof.

We show that for any possible choice of
A_{0}
with
\delta(\deg\min\overline{A_{0}})>d
, the conditional probability is
q^{-2d}
, from which the result follows.

We are given that
\mathrm{char}(\rho_{k-1}(A_{0}))\in I(\rho_{k-1}(g),n-d)
, hence
\mathrm{char}(A_{0})-g=e_{0}(x)+\pi^{k}e_{1}(x)
, where
\deg e_{0}(x)<n-d
. Thus to have
\mathrm{char}(A)\in I(g,n-d)
we actually need to have

\deg\left(\overline{e_{1}}-\partial\mathrm{char}_{\overline{A_{0}}}(A_{1})\right)<n-d.

(10.2)

We note that since
\mathrm{char}(A_{0}),g
are monic we have
\deg\overline{e_{1}}<n
. By Theorem
10.4
, there are some
\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{q}
,
b\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]^{\mathrm{skpal}}_{<\deg\min A_{0}}
, such that
\partial\mathrm{char}_{\overline{A_{0}}}(A_{1})=\alpha J_{A_{0}}(x)+\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}b
. Hence the condition (
10.2
) is equivalent to

\deg\left((\overline{e_{1}}-\alpha J_{A_{0}}(x))-\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}b\right)<n-d.

Since both polynomials are of degree
<n
, and since
x^{\deg\min(A_{0})}\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}
is monic of degree
n
, we see that (
10.2
) is equivalent to demanding

x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}+\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}b\in I\left(x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}+(\overline{e_{1}}-\alpha J_{A_{0}}(x)),n-d\right).

For polynomials
r,s\in\mathbb{F}_{q}[x]
, denote by
1_{r,s}
the event that

x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}+r\in I\left(x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A_{0}}}{\min\overline{A_{0}}}+s,n-d\right).

We compute the probability for (
10.2
) to hold, using Hayes characters. By the discussion above, it is equal to

\Pr_{A_{1}\in\mathfrak{u}_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}\left[1_{\frac{\mathrm{char}(A_{0})}{\min(A_{0})}b,\overline{e_{1}}-\alpha J_{A_{0}}(x)}\right]=q^{-\delta(\deg\min(\overline{A_{0}}))-1}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{q}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<\deg\min(\overline{A_{0}})}^{\mathrm{skpal}}\end{subarray}}1_{\frac{\mathrm{char}(A_{0})}{\min(A_{0})}b,\overline{e_{1}}-\alpha J_{A_{0}}(x)}=\\
=q^{-\delta(\deg\min\overline{A_{0}})-1}\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{q}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<\deg\min(\overline{A_{0}})}^{\mathrm{skpal}}\end{subarray}}q^{-2d}\sum_{\chi\in R_{d,1}}\chi\left(\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A}_{0}}{\min\overline{A}_{0}}(x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}+b)\right)\overline{\chi}\left(\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A}_{0}}{\min\overline{A}_{0}}x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}+\overline{e_{1}}-\alpha J_{A_{0}}(x)\right)=\\
=q^{-2d-1}\sum_{\chi\in R_{d,1}\atop{\alpha\in\mathbb{F}_{q}}}\chi\left(\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A}_{0}}{\min\overline{A}_{0}}\right)\overline{\chi}\left(\frac{\mathrm{char}\overline{A}_{0}}{\min\overline{A}_{0}}x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}+\overline{e_{1}}-\alpha J_{A_{0}}(x)\right)q^{-\delta(\deg\min(\overline{A_{0}}))}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<\deg\min(\overline{A_{0}})}^{\mathrm{skpal}}\end{subarray}}\chi(x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}+b).

We note that by Lemma
3.22
, since
d<\min\overline{A_{0}}
, we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{<\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}^{\mathrm{skpal}}\end{subarray}}\chi(x^{\deg\min\overline{A_{0}}}+b)=q^{\delta(\deg\min(\overline{A}_{0}))}1_{\chi=\chi_{0}}.

Substituting this into the previous equation we get the result.
∎

10.2.2
Distribution mod
\pi^{k}
given mod
\pi
behavior

Let
g\in(\mathcal{R}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{R})[x]
be a monic polynomial of degree
n
. Define the discrepancy of the conditional distribution given a matrix
B\in U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
such that
\mathrm{char}(B)\in I(\overline{g},n-d)
to be

\Delta_{B,k}^{g}=\Pr[\mathrm{char}(A)\in I(g,n-d)|\overline{A}=B]-q^{-2d(k-1)}.

As in the previous cases, when
\deg\min B
is large enough, the discrepancy is zero.

Proposition 10.6
.

Let
g\in(\mathcal{R}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{R})[x]
be a monic polynomial of degree
n
,
d<\frac{n}{2}
. For every matrix
B\in U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
with
\mathrm{char}(B)\in I(\overline{g},n-d)
and
\delta(\deg\min(B))\geq d
, we have
\Delta_{B,k}^{g}=0
.

Proof.

The proof is essentially the same as that of Proposition
6.7
, using Theorem
10.4
.
∎

Now we consider how
\mathrm{char}(A)
behaves for lifts
A
of
B
, when
\delta(B)<d
.

Proposition 10.7
.

Let
g\in(\mathcal{R}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{R})[x]
be a monic polynomial of degree
n
. Let
B\in U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
be a matrix with
\delta(\deg\min B)=l<d
. Let
\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k}=\{\mathrm{char}(A):A\in U_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}),\overline{A}=B\}
. Then,

1.

|\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k}|=q^{2(k-1)l}
. Moreover, each element of
\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k}
has different
l
leading coefficients modulo
\pi^{k}
.

2.

\Pr[\mathrm{char}(A)\in I(g,n-d)|\overline{A}=B]=1_{\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k}\cap I(g,n-d)\neq\emptyset}q^{-2(k-1)l}
. Equivalently, we have

\Delta_{B,k}^{g}=1_{\mathcal{P}_{B}^{k}\cap I(g,n-d)\neq\emptyset}q^{-2(k-1)l}-q^{-2(k-1)d}.

Proof.

The proof is omitted as it is essentially the proof of Proposition
6.7
.
∎

We now let
\mathcal{G}
be a set of representatives for the lifts of
\mathrm{char}(B)
to a monic polynomial
g\in(\mathcal{R}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{R})[x]
, modulo equality in the
d
next-to-leading coefficients. We note that
|\mathcal{G}|=q^{2(k-1)d}
.

Proposition 10.8
.

Let
B\in U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})
. Then,

1.

If
\delta(\deg\min(B))\geq d
, we have
\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{B,k}^{g}|=0
.

2.

If
\delta(\deg\min(B))=l<d
, we have
\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{B,k}^{g}|\leq 2
.

Proof.

The first claim follows directly from Proposition
10.6
. The second follows from the triangle inequality, together with the two parts of Proposition
10.7
.
∎

Let
g\in(\mathcal{R}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{R})[x]
be a monic polynomial of degree
n
. Now we define the discrepancy of a polynomial
\overline{f}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}},\overline{f}\in I(\overline{g},n-d)
by

\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}=\Pr[\mathrm{char}(A)\in I(g,n-d)|\mathrm{char}(\overline{A})=\overline{f}]-q^{-2d(k-1)}.

Lemma 10.9
.

Let
\overline{f}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
be a
\sim
-symmetric polynomial, and let
\mathcal{G}
be a set of representatives for the possible lifts of
\overline{f}
to a monic polynomial of degree
n
in
(\mathcal{R}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{R})[x]
, modulo equality in the
d
next-to-leading coefficients.

1.

If
\delta(\deg\mathrm{rad}\overline{f})\geq d
, then
\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}|=0
.

2.

If
\delta(\deg\mathrm{rad}\overline{f})=s<d
, then
\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}|\leq 2\Pr_{B\in U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}[\deg\min(B)<d|\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f}]
.

Proof.

The proof is the same as that of Lemma
6.10
, using Propositions
10.6
,
10.7
.
∎

10.2.3
Discrepancy bound on distribution in intervals

As usual we define for a monic polynomial
g\in(\mathcal{R}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{R})[x]
of degree
n
the discrepancy of the surrounding interval,

\Delta_{g}=\Pr[\mathrm{char}A\in I(g,n-d)]-q^{-2kd}.

Lemma 10.10
.

Let
d<n/2
, and let
\mathcal{G}
be a set of representatives for the monic polynomials
g\in(\mathcal{R}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{R})[x]
of degree
n
, modulo equivalence in the
d
next-to-leading coefficients. Then,

\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{g}|=O\left(q^{2d+\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n^{2}}{4d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+2d-3}{n}+q^{4d-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}+o(n)}\right).

Proof.

As in the proof of Lemma
6.11
, write for
g\in\mathcal{G}

\Pr[\mathrm{char}A\in I(g,n-d)]=q^{-2(k-1)d}\Pr[\mathrm{char}\overline{A}\in I(\overline{g},n-d)]+\sum_{\overline{f}\in I(\overline{g},n-d)}\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}\cdot\Pr[\mathrm{char}\overline{A}=\overline{f}].

By
[
12
, Theorem 5.16]
,

\Pr[\mathrm{char}\overline{A}\in I(\overline{g},n-d)]=q^{-2d}+O\left(q^{\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n^{2}}{4d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+2d-3}{n}\right).

Hence,

|\Delta_{g}|<O\left(q^{-(k-1)d+\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n^{2}}{4d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+2d-3}{n}\right)+\sum_{\overline{f}\in I(\overline{g},n-d)}\Delta_{\overline{f},k}^{g}\cdot\Pr[\mathrm{char}\overline{A}=\overline{f}].

Summing this over all
g\in\mathcal{G}
, and using Lemma
10.9
, we get that

\sum_{g\in\mathcal{G}}|\Delta_{g}|<O\left(q^{-(k-1)d+\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n^{2}}{4d}}(1+\frac{1}{q-1})^{n}\binom{n+2d-3}{n}\right)+\\
+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\overline{f}\in\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]\\
\delta(\deg\mathrm{rad}\overline{f})<d\end{subarray}}2\Pr_{B\in U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}[\delta(\deg\min(B))<d|\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f}]\Pr[\mathrm{char}B=\overline{f}].

Now if
\delta(\deg\min(B))<d
in particular
\deg\min(B)<2d
, hence using Claim
5.15
,

\Pr_{B\in U_{n}(\mathbb{F}_{q})}[\delta(\deg\min(B))<d|\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f}]\Pr[\mathrm{char}B=\overline{f}]<\Pr[\deg\min(B)<2d\cap\mathrm{char}(B)=\overline{f}]<q^{-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}+o(n)},

and by Lemma
3.3
, the number of polynomials in
\mathbb{F}_{q^{2}}[x]_{=n}^{\mathrm{mon}}
with a radical of degree
<2d
is
q^{4d+o(n)}
, hence we get the result.
∎

10.3
Joint distribution of traces

As in the
GL_{n}
case, the total variation distance of
\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{U}
from
\mathrm{UTR}_{d}
may be computed as the limit of the total variation distance of the
\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O}
random variable

\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{U,k}
,
\mathrm{UTR}_{d}^{k}
. We recall that by the discussion of
[
12
, Theorem 5.16]
, There is a constant
c_{q}^{U}
such that for
d<c_{q}^{U}\cdot n
,

q^{\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n^{2}}{4d}}\left(1+\frac{1}{q-1}\right)^{n}\binom{n+2d-3}{n}=o(1).

Theorem 10.11
.

Let
d<\frac{n}{2}
, and let
\{a_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq d}
be a trace datum. Let
\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{U,k}
be the random variable attaching to a matrix
M\in U_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})
its trace datum of length
d
, and let
\mathrm{UTR}_{d}^{k}
be the uniform measure on trace data, as defined in Section
3.2
. Then,

d_{TV}(\mu_{\mathrm{TR}_{d}^{U,k}},\mu_{\mathrm{UTR}_{d}^{k}})=\sum_{\{a_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq d}\text{ trace data}}\Bigg|\Pr_{M\in U_{n}(\mathcal{O}/\pi^{k}\mathcal{O})}[M\text{ has trace datum }\{a_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq d}]-q^{-(kd-S(d,k))}\Bigg|<\\
<O\left(q^{2d+\frac{n}{2}-\frac{n^{2}}{4d}}(1+\frac{1}{q-1})^{n}\binom{n+2d-3}{n}+q^{4d-\frac{n^{2}}{2d}+o(n)}\right).

Proof.

The proof is omitted, as it is essentially the same as the analogous one for
GL_{n}(\mathcal{O})
.
∎

By taking
k\to\infty
and using Lemma
2.10
we get as a consequence Theorem
1.7
.
